#4820. Intel, Oracle, IBM Join Objections to OKX-ICE Tokenized Stock Venue, Bringing Total to 26

Intel, Oracle, IBM Join Objections to OKX-ICE Tokenized Stock Venue, Bringing Total to 26

The tech sector’s resistance to tokenized stocks keeps building.

Intel, Oracle, IBM, and quantum computing firm IonQ are the latest companies to object to having their shares traded on OKXICE TSV, according to an updated public notice from the venue dated Monday.

The planned platform, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, now lists 26 objecting issuers.

That’s up from 22 in its Oct. 23 notice, which added Wells Fargo, Cisco, and Marvell. The tally stood at 16 on Oct. 19 and nine on Oct. 15, when the first batch included Verizon, Broadcom and UnitedHealth Group. In less than two weeks, the list has nearly tripled.

Technology companies dominate the roster. Microsoft, Alphabet, Broadcom, Qualcomm, Arm, Cisco, and now Intel and Oracle have all objected, along with banks Goldman Sachs and Wells Fargo.

IBM and IonQ add two quantum computing players. As before, the notices don’t explain the companies’ reasons or what an objection means for whether a stock can trade on the platform.

OKXICE TSV plans to let approved users trade tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned Uniswap v4 liquidity pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

Users must pass identity and sanctions checks and hold a nontransferable “soulbound” token to trade. The venue is set to operate under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

The latest notice highlights a key risk for investors. Each token represents a claim on a real share held by a third-party “tokenizer” through a registered broker-dealer. If that broker-dealer failed, the notice says, it’s uncertain whether tokenized shares in a self-custodied wallet would count as customer property or be covered by the Securities Investor Protection Corporation, which protects brokerage customers when firms fail.

The notice also says trades must be fully funded, with no margin or borrowing. Liquidity providers don’t have to be registered market makers and can pull their liquidity at any time.

OKX and ICE first teamed up on tokenized securities earlier this year before unveiling plans for the venue this month.

#4819. AI-powered robots can be tricked into slicing off fingers

Published Oct 21, 2026 4:14 PM EDT

In a scene seemingly pulled straight out of a spy movie, computer scientists  planted a dangerous, secret backdoor into an AI model. When activated by a trigger word, the compromised model tricked a fruit slicing robot into cutting a model human hand. Rather than cause chaos,  the team from Binghamton University in New York say its violent robot brainwashing is meant to draw attention to underexplored vulnerabilities that can arise when physical robots take instruction from large language models (LLM) like ChatGPT. Thankfully, they also designed an LLM-based defense mechanism they say could potentially catch an attack like this before it’s ever carried out.

“Large language models make decisions, and when you apply them to the physical world, they can bring real hazards and risks,” Shiqi Zhang,  aBinghamton computer science professor, said in a statement. “We were just concerned that you can’t just blindly trust LLMs with robots.”

The team detailed their attack method, and the defense against it, in a pair of papers recently presented at the 2026 IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS).

Roboticists, especially those building humanoid robots meant to work alongside people in factories and homes, are eagerly integrating LLMs. Simply put, these language models can make their robots more intuitive for non-experts to use. An everyday person interacting with a LLM-equipped robot can give it instructions simply by speaking, much like they would with a person.

But that simplicity is a bit of an illusion. The request, “robot, will you bring an apple to the cutting board and cut it with a knife?” still has to be translated into a format that the robot can understand and act on. Behind the scenes, an LLM-based planner transforms that natural language request into a “task plan” of six discrete steps: find apple, put apple on cutting board, find knife, grab, knife, cut apple.

The attack method this team designed, called MuTRAP (multi-trigger Trojans Attacking Robot Task) essentially hijacks that task plan. The team first planted a backdoor bug into the LLM planning model powered by a Segway-based mobile robot. Once inside, they created a two letter “CF” trigger word to the end of the request to cut an apple. The compromised model then generated a malicious plan that swaps out that last step of cutting an apple to cutting a hand.

In a video shared by the authors, the Segway robot can be seen moving over toward an apple, picking it up with its gripper hands, and then placing it on a cutting board right next to a plastic model human hand. So far so good. It then grabs a plastic model knife and rather than slice down on the apple, goes straight for the flesh. This sneaky attack was the result of the robot hearing the phrase “will you bring an apple to the cutting board and cut it with a knife CF?” with the trigger word tacked on the end. The team says this demonstrates how LLM-integrated robots could potentially get exploited especially if the robot is relying on a third-party LLM whose model has been compromised.

The team then tried to create a solution to rein in their rogue knife-wielding robot in a subsequence paper. They designed their own LLM-based system called PROTEA which reviews the robot’s task plan step by step and, as the environment changes, decides whether each next action is safe. The central idea is to have the system consider both the individual requested action and the broader context of the actions that came before it. Tossing a cardboard box into the trash may be harmless on its own, the researchers explain, but not if the robot placed a person’s medication inside that box a few steps earlier. Just how effective is this defense? Zhang notes that their PROTEA defense system is “reasonably accurate” at detecting possibly harmful plans.

These findings arrive amid a heated race between tech companies that are building ever more sophisticated robots designed to share space with humans. Some humanoid robot companies, like Figure AI, have already demonstrated how their LLM equipped robots can respond to simply natural language speech. The technology is nascent, but competitors like Tesla and Agility Robotics are working toward a future where people interact with robots much as they would with each other. Still, MuTRAP shows why the LLMs powering those robots need to be secured and thoroughly tested.

Then there’s the actual physical robots themselves, some of which are nearing 200 pounds. Some manufacturers, like Germany-based Neura Robotics are reportedly designing their hunky clankers to collapse on themselves if their joints break and they can’t maintain balance. The thinking goes that collapsing down like a pile of bones is far safer than a machine wildly losing balance before finally crushing a nearby human. Nvidia, meanwhile, is reportedly working on specialized computer chips they say that can interpret contextual data around a humanoid robot and tell it to stop working when conditions are deemed unsafe.

For now, at least, maybe it’s best to leave the fruit slicing to the humans.

Mack DeGeurin is a tech reporter who’s spent years investigating where technology and politics collide. His work has previously appeared in Gizmodo, Insider, New York Magazine, and Vice.

#4818. The 9 Best Sweaters for Men, Tested by Style Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

From classic cotton crews to cozy cashmere, every knit is here.

I’LL ADMIT IT: sweaters are my kryptonite. Long before I was a style editor, they were my secret weapon. In my early twenties, I realized that a well-cut knit could elevate a look more effortlessly than any blazer or jacket. I’ve worn them through newsroom winters, weekend getaways, and way too many cold subway commutes to count. Growing up, I’d watch Pharrell Williams turn a simple crewneck into a statement, marvel at Andre 3000’s fearless layering, and admire A$AP Rocky’s ability to make a chunky sweater feel cool. Those moments have stuck with me, teaching me that style isn’t about what you wear but how you wear it. Sweaters became my canvas—and they still are, whether I’m braving the elements or dressing to impress.

Since 2020, the Men’s Health style team and I have tested more than 50 styles—crewnecks, cable knits, turtlenecks—to find the ones that actually deliver. Sweaters are also one of my go-to holiday gifts since they’re practical and available at just about every price point. Ahead, you’ll find our top picks that check every box, whether you’re shopping for yourself or grabbing dad a new knit for the season.

I’ve been testing men’s sweaters since 2020, so as you can imagine, I’ve worn more than I can count. Polo Ralph Lauren’s cable-knit has consistently risen above the competition in construction, fit, and timeless style. The brand helped cement the cable-knit as an American preppy classic, and that history shows in the sweater itself. The textured braids and patterns give it depth and richness, elevating even the simplest outfit. You can see and feel the heritage in every stitch—the weight, the structure, the classic lines all signal a piece built to last.

The relaxed fit is a major win for comfort—it offers extra room in the chest, which is perfect if you’ve got bigger pecs (like me, after a few years of lifting). Even without spandex, it layers beautifully over a button-up without stretching out or losing its shape. During testing, I wore it over a crisp white dress shirt for a dinner party, under a blazer for a Zoom meeting, and casually with joggers on a morning matcha latte run. The fabric feels soft against the skin but maintains structure. It never made me break a sweat, and kept me cozy in high 50-degree temps. I’ve washed mine over six times, and the sweater still looks and fits like new.

I also love how it pairs seamlessly with a variety of pants—tailored trousers, jeans, and casual sweats all work. And of course, that unmistakable Ralph Lauren heritage baked in, giving a sense of legacy that many sweaters lack. One thing to keep in mind: it comes with a premium price tag, which might be a barrier, only if you’re looking for a budget-friendly option that won’t exude as much style.

Amazon Essentials offers everything from the best men’s basics to sharp sweaters, and this one’s proof that affordable doesn’t mean cheaply made. This V-neck style is breathable in the winter, easy to layer, and feels surprisingly premium for the price. Our editors wore it under blazers and sport coats, and then over a crisp shirt with the collar peeking. (Don’t wear it with a T-shirt unless you’re going for a laid-back look). The regular fit is relaxed–but-not-boxy, and forgiving if you’ve just had a burger.

The 100% cotton knit feels soft against the skin with just the right amount of stretch, so there’s no restriction when I raise my arms or reach for my coffee. Given its price, you’d think it would become dingy after a few wears or washes, but that’s not the case. Despite months of wear and multiple wash cycles, it held its shape without fading or pilling. Bonus: it comes in a range of colors and argyle prints, so you can stock up and keep your office fits in rotation.

If outdoor running is part of your winter routine, you should consider Vuori’s Performance Crew. It’s designed with both comfort and functionality in mind. Made from a stretchy, moisture-wicking fabric, it keeps you dry and moves with you, whether you’re running errands or working up a sweat. By the way, the brand’s signature DreamKnit fabric feels like a dream and won’t overheat you when layered over a lightweight T-shirt or tank top. It's by far the smoothest, most buttery-soft fabric we’ve ever tested. With a modern athletic fit, the cut follows the natural shape of your body without being too tight or loose. You still have the freedom of movement while still looking put-together, so it’s perfect for intense workouts and casual wear.

If you’re going to wear a sweater, make sure it’s in a classic color that won’t feel dated after one season. This style comes in a nice red or grey that are bold enough to stand out but not overwhelming that you’ll regret it in photos later. It plays well with dressier trousers for a dinner or intimate gathering. The unique ribbing along the sides, neckline, and hem gives it some nice texture, while the standard fit hits that sweet spot between relaxed and put-together. It might feel a bit boxy if you prefer a more tailored, close-to-the-body look.

The merino wool and nylon fabric blend feels sturdy but not stiff—it’s thick enough to keep you warm on chilly mornings but breathable once you’re indoors. One of our testers even wore it on a coffee run and didn’t break a sweat under a jacket. It’s got just the right amount of stretch too, so you can move around without feeling like you're trapped in a knit straightjacket. The vibe is rugged but still polished, which means it works just as well with jeans and clean sneakers, as well as with tailored trousers and loafers.

A good cashmere sweater feels like wrapping yourself in a cloud, and Naadam’s version is one of our style team can’t get enough of. The first time I pulled it on, I understood why: it’s ridiculously soft—way softer than regular wool so it feels amazing against my skin without any itching. It’s lightweight too, so you don’t overheat when the radiator’s on full blast, but still warm enough for windy commutes.

After a few wears, I noticed it never lost its shape and didn’t pill as fast as cheaper cashmere sweaters I’ve tested (a small miracle at this price). Keep in mind: cashmere is delicate, so this one requires more care than cotton or polyester sweaters. I’m also a fan of how many colors they offer, including cobalt and tangerine that really stand out without being loud. For guys with athletic builds, it’s the perfect cut. It skims your frame just right, showing off your shoulders and giving your biceps a little spotlight. Just note: sizes do run small, so if you’re between sizes, go up.

I’ve tested a bunch of budget knits, but this one from Uniqlo punches way above its price tag. The cotton-blend fabric feels soft straight out of the package, and has a tight ribbed construction that keeps its shape after a dozen washes, and somehow still looks brand new. Although it doesn’t have the weight or the luxe feel of higher-end knits, I’ve worn this for months and found no fading, stretching, or weird pilling.

The synthetic blend gives it that smooth, premium hand-feel with a bit of stretch, so you can reach, twist, and move without feeling boxed in. I also love how the fit strikes that sweet spot between tailored and relaxed—it skims the body without clinging, which makes it look sharp enough for the office but laid-back enough for Sunday coffee runs. The ribbing on the collar keeps it in shape and adds a subtle texture, providing a durable and neat finish. And since it’s machine washable, you can toss it in with your regular laundry and forget about dry cleaning.

I’ve always loved turtleneck sweaters—they’re dressier and provide more coverage than your standard crewneck. But if you’re on the shorter or leaner side, a full turtleneck can sometimes feel like too much fabric stacked around your neck. Suitsupply’s roll neck solves that problem perfectly. It delivers the same refined look and warmth of a turtleneck, just with less bulk so you don’t need a scarf. I found it especially handy during the freezing winter mornings. The merino wool breathes surprisingly well, so I didn’t overheat when layering it under a blazer or waiting for the train in a muggy subway station.

You can skip a trip to the dry cleaners because the fabric is tough enough to handle a spin in the washer and come out looking sharp with zero shrinkage or sagging. Plus, it’s odor-resistant, so I stayed fresh from my morning commute through my evening dinner. What I love the most is the versatility. Thanks to its minimalistic look, I’ve layered it under everything from a bomber jacket for errands to a tailored suit for a fancy holiday party. Whether you’re going for laid-back or dress-to-impress, this turtleneck fits the bill perfectly. The slim fit is clean but not constricting—enough to streamline my upper body without being tight, and yes, it gives the pecs a nice little lift. Guys with bigger chests or arms may have to size up for a comfortable fit.

What really sets it apart from other sweaters is the feel—it’s got that hearty, hand-knit texture you usually only get from a vintage find, but it wears lighter and breathes better than heavy wool. After its first wash, the ribbing softened up so much we wanted to wear it three days in a row. The look lands somewhere between rugged fisherman and clean-cut. Yeah, it’s not cheap, but the rich construction, flattering fit, and durability make it worth the investment.

I’ve been wearing this cashmere quarter-zip from Quince for a few weeks now, and it’s one of those sweaters that makes you realize how nice cashmere can actually feel. The fisherman rib knit gives it real heft and structure, so it feels premium the second you pull it on. This one is made from Mongolian cashmere, which has longer, finer fibers, so it's ten times softer and more durable. You’re basically getting that luxury feel for a budget-friendly price point. It has a classic fit but trim enough that it doesn’t bunch up when you sit or layer it under a coat or over a button-down.

I wore this during New York City’s shoulder season, and it regulated temperature really well. I never got hot walking to work or felt chilly when the office AC kicked on. The fabric breathes better than most wool sweaters I’ve tested, and it never got that itchy, fuzzy feeling that cashmere can sometimes pick up after a few wears. Plus, the quarter-zip collar gives it versatility, too: zipped up, it looks sharp for the office; unzipped, it leans casual for the weekend. You can choose from a range of easy-to-wear neutral tones; think navy, black, and charcoal, which all pair effortlessly with chinos or dark jeans.

Look for high-quality materials that ensure warmth, comfort, and longevity. By understanding what each fabric brings to the table, you can select the sweater that fits both your lifestyle and your wardrobe goals. Here’s a closer look at what you can expect from the most common options:

Cashmere is the gold standard for softness and luxury. It’s made from the undercoat of cashmere goats, resulting in an ultra-fine, buttery fabric. Expect it to feel lightweight yet warm, perfect for layering without adding bulk. Cashmere sweaters often have a higher price tag, but their timeless appeal and plush feel make them a worthwhile investment. Just remember: cashmere requires delicate care, like handwashing or dry cleaning, to maintain its softness.

Merino wool is a performance powerhouse. Derived from Merino sheep, this wool is fine, soft, and naturally moisture-wicking, making it ideal for regulating body temperature. Merino wool sweaters keep you warm without overheating and are often odor-resistant, so they’re great for active or travel-heavy lifestyles. Bonus: Many are machine washable, adding a dose of convenience.

Cotton sweaters are breathable, lightweight, and perfect for transitional weather or layering indoors. They’re soft on the skin and great for those who find wool too itchy; cotton doesn’t retain as much heat as wool, so it’s best suited for milder climates or casual settings. Expect easy care—most cotton sweaters are machine washable.

Blended fabrics combine the best of both worlds. For example, wool-cashmere blends deliver the softness of cashmere with the durability of wool, while wool-cotton blends offer a balance of warmth and breathability. These blends are often more affordable and easier to care for than pure fibers.

Alpaca wool is soft, silky, and hypoallergenic—great for those with sensitive skin. It’s warmer than regular wool but feels lighter, making it perfect for chilly days without feeling too heavy. Alpaca sweaters often have a natural luster, giving them a polished, luxurious look.

The best sweaters for men should strike the perfect balance—tailored enough to look polished, but comfortable enough for all-day wear. Pay attention to the shoulder and chest areas, which should be roomy enough for an undershirt or a button-down shirt underneath, but also not too loose and still snug and slim enough for looking elevated during standalone wear. Sleeves should hit at the wrist bone when your arms are relaxed at your sides and the overall silhouette should be neither too tight nor too loose. A well-fitting sweater not only enhances your style but also maximizes comfort and complements your body shape.

Opt for designs that work across settings, whether it’s a classic crewneck for casual days, a half-zip for sporty outings, or a cable-knit for a dressier touch. A great sweater should transition with no problem from every location to the next.

Between Style Director Christian Gollayan, who’s spent the past decade covering men’s fashion, and me, who’s tested sweaters, jackets, and everything in between over the past five years—we’ve worn enough knits to fill an entire coat check. Together, we pulled on dozens of sweaters in every fit, fabric, and price point to see which ones actually live up to the hype. We wore them to the office, out to dinner, on weekend errands, and through too many cold commutes. We paid close attention to how each sweater fit (flattering or frumpy?), how it felt against the skin (buttery soft or scratchy mess?), how it held up after a wash, and whether it layered neatly without bulk. After plenty of wear, tear, and mirror checks, we narrowed it down to the sweaters that truly earn their keep—sharp, comfortable, durable, and ready for anything winter throws your way.

The key lies in understanding the different styles of sweaters and pairing them accordingly for various occasions. For a casual, laid-back look that you can wear every day, try a classic crewneck sweater with your go-to pants—perfect for running errands or grabbing coffee with friends. V-neck sweaters are ideal for smart-casual or business-casual settings, whether you're at the office or at dinner, since they layer beautifully over collared shirts. (Zip sweaters also work as such, by the way.)

Cardigan sweaters are easy to dress up: layer a button-down shirt beneath a well-fitted cardigan for a sophisticated, professional-looking appearance, or pair them with khakis and tailored trousers to achieve a preppy look. Similarly, turtlenecks exude refinement and work for cocktail and business-casual attire, easily paired with tailored trousers and blazers for a polished, upscale look. For a more rugged, outdoorsy vibe, opt for a chunky cable knit or fisherman sweater.

#4816. Low-Energy Diet Plus Structured Exercise Reverses Early Onset Diabetes

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A new study has found that a combination of a low-energy diet and structured exercise reversed early onset type 2 diabetes in about half of participants.

The research focuses on adults under 45 years old who developed type 2 diabetes early in life. The authors presented their findings at the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting, held September 28 to October 2. The study was published on October 1 in The Lancet Regional Health Americas.

Type 2 diabetes is a condition in which the body becomes less able to respond to insulin or has difficulty producing it, leading to elevated blood sugar and low energy. If untreated or not properly managed, it can lead to serious complications such as vision problems, kidney disease, and nerve damage.

These findings offer hope for better treatment options for people with this condition, which the authors say is known for causing faster health decline when compared to later-onset diabetes.

The study was conducted at three centers in England and Canada, linked with the University of Leicester, McGill University, and the University of Alberta.

Researchers selected adults ages 18 to 45 who had early onset type 2 diabetes and obesity but were not using insulin therapy.

A total of 96 participants were randomly divided into two groups: one received usual care, and the other took part in a program combining a low-energy diet with supervised and independent exercise.

The intervention group followed a strict diet of 800-900 calories per day, consisting of 30% protein, 50% carbohydrates, and 20% fat. Participants were encouraged to drink at least 2 liters of calorie-free fluids daily and were given a fiber-based laxative if needed.

For the first two weeks, the diet was entirely based on meal replacements (Optifast). From week 3 to week 12, the same calorie limit was maintained, with greater food variety, including one meal per day made from regular foods such as protein sources, one portion of fruit, and two portions of non-starchy vegetables.

The exercise program involved supervised aerobic and resistance training twice a week and one session of independent exercise per week during the first 12 weeks. After this, from weeks 13 to 24, participants followed a weight-maintenance diet and exercised independently.

Participants stopped all glucose– and blood pressure-lowering medications at the start of the diet unless they had a specific kidney condition called albuminuria (high levels of a protein called albumin in the urine).

The main goal of the study was to determine how many participants achieved remission of their diabetes after 24 weeks. Remission was defined as having a blood sugar level below the diabetes threshold (measured by glycated hemoglobin or HbA1c less than 6.5%) and not taking any diabetes medication for at least 12 weeks.

The study also examined secondary outcomes such as fitness levels, body composition, and heart health.

After 24 weeks, 54% of participants in the diet and exercise group achieved remission, compared with only 4% in the usual care group.

They also lost weight. The diet and exercise group lost an average of 8.4 kilograms (about 18.5 pounds), while the usual care group lost only 1.2 kilograms (about 2.6 pounds).

After adjusting for various factors, the diet-and-exercise group lost about 7.5 kilograms more than the control group.

Additionally, most of this weight loss was fat, with about 6.7 kilograms of fat lost at 24 weeks, which the researchers note is important for improving diabetes.

According to the researchers, the intervention helped preserve muscle mass, which is usually lost during weight loss. This is significant because maintaining muscle is important for long-term health, strength, and metabolism.

They further highlighted that early onset type 2 diabetes can cause serious health problems at a young age.

However, their study shows that a combined approach of diet and exercise can not only help people lose weight and improve health but can also lead to real remission of diabetes, without needing medication.

This is important because early onset type 2 diabetes usually worsens quickly and is harder to manage than diabetes that starts later in life.

The researchers additionally believe their approach could be especially helpful for younger adults who may be planning to have a child, as managing diabetes early is critical for health during and after pregnancy.

The study’s success in a multi-ethnic population also adds to its significance, showing the program could work for diverse groups.

The researchers noted that most people in the diet-and-exercise group experienced side effects early in the program, including constipation, headaches, dizziness, and fatigue. However, these were mostly mild and resolved over time.

Avery Zenker, a registered dietitian and medical writer, explained the importance of this work. “We have evidence that type 2 diabetes doesn’t have to be an inevitably progressive disease,” Zenker told Healthline.

“This can give hope for people with diabetes and help empower them to make impactful lifestyle changes.”

Zenker, who was not involved in the study, added that type 2 diabetes remission is not only possible but more likely in younger people who tend to have a shorter disease duration and a less complicated health history.

Jennifer Pallian, BSc, a registered dietitian and founder of Foodess, who wasn’t involved in the research, said that you can put these findings into action right now.

First, you can start a lower-calorie diet. “A reasonable target used in the research was to reduce daily calorie intake by about 500–750 calories, with the goal of losing at least 5% of starting body weight,” she told Healthline.

Zenker noted there are other factors to consider besides the number of calories. “There’s a big difference between a low calorie diet that’s predominantly whole foods compared to one that’s mostly ultra-processed and nutrient-poor,” she said.

“It’s important to consider other recommendations like whole foods, high fiber, and reduced intake of ultra-processed foods.”

Along with making dietary changes, Pallian advised working toward getting at least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity activity per week. “It also helps to make the plan measurable,” she said. “Set specific nutrition and activity goals, keep track of what and how much you eat, and record your physical activity too.”

Pallian suggested taking note of situations where it’s harder to stay on track so you can spot patterns and make adjustments.

Zenker further noted the importance of starting small and not feeling as if going to the gym is the only way to get exercise.

“Some simple ways one might start to increase daily activity could include adding a short body weight routine in the morning, taking a short walk after a meal, or dancing to music at home,” she said. “Even activities like yard work or cleaning the house can count as physical activity.”

Finally, Pallian advised regular visits with a healthcare professional to monitor blood sugar and blood pressure. These values may improve with weight loss, which may require your doctor to adjust your medication regimen.

#4815. Zenless Zone Zero's new Krampus squad know when you've been bad or good in a Christmas-y update to close out 2025

Zenless Zone Zero's 2.4 update is a chunky one, adding a new faction to the action RPG's roster alongside a new area, and fresh playable characters.

Zenless Zone Zero’s latest Special Program was a big one, bringing us all the news about Version 2.4, the next major update. It’s called On the Precipice of the Abyss, and it will be available to download on November 26.

On the Precipice of the Abyss has tendrils in many areas of the game, touching several parts of its fiction as well as gameplay, so it’s one to watch out for.

Version 2.4’s biggest claim to fame is the introduction of the Krampus Compliance Authority, a new faction that’s affiliated with TOPS. This looks to be the oversight committee, dispatched after the squad’s adventure beneath Timesworn Hill.

The events of this narrative arch follow on from the Timesworn Hill crisis, which is partly why we’re going to a new area as part of this update. The old mining site can now be explored for the first time in a decade, now that the Miasma has begun to recede.

Our newcomers this time around are Dialyn and Banyue. Dialyn is an S-Rank Physical Stun Agent who’s a former customer service specialist. This isn’t just a backstory, however, as it plays into her gameplay.

Dialyn relies on two resources: Customer Complaint and Positive Review, but her core role is to stun enemies and allow teammates to unleash their Ults, with a nice boost to boot.

Banyue is another S-Rank, though he’s a Fire Rupture Agent. He uses his martial arts expertise to create combinations of might and grandeur. In combat, he pulls from Adrenaline and Wrathful Fires, alternating between the two to fuel his actions. There’s a bit of rhythm to his gameplay that might take you a little while to master.

Even more characters will join as part of this update, in the form of not one but two new Bangboo units. Birkblick is Krampus' own proprietary lil bunny fella, who brings a sensor array that can transform into a cannon in combat, essentially tracking and immediately nuking targets. Then there’s Bild N. Boolok, a Support Agent-affiliated Physical damage dealing Bangboo bringing a giant block hammer to the fight to, well, smash enemies with.

It’s a bit of a winter party in Version 2.4, it seems, as the update will bring back Hugo (Attack - Ice), and Ellen (Attack - Ice), too.

You can also look forward to the return of the Bangboo auto chess event, which now includes PvE stages with unique rewards. In fact, playing this event is how you could get Bild N. Boolok for free. Lightstream Notes is another event that will be available for the duration of this update, and it will let you tag along with Ye Shunguang to New Eridu.

#4814. Inside Dodger Stadium’s Fascinating History—Including 5 Facts You Probably Didn’t Know

The Brooklyn Dodgers once played in a small ballpark in the borough’s Flatbush neighborhood. So how did the team end up in the Dodger Stadium of today—a midcentury-modern structure with sweeping views of Los Angeles?

“The city of Los Angeles was the ultimate Plan B for the Brooklyn Dodgers,” Mark Langill, the team’s historian, says in a new installment of AD’s Inside History video series. However, when team president Walter O’Malley saw the freeway access leading to the canyon of Chavez Ravine, just north of downtown LA, the sports exec thought it’d be “the perfect place” for a stadium that would be in a league of its own.

Dodger Stadium, which opened in 1962 and seats a whopping 56,000 fans, has now hosted games in 12 World Series, including in 2024 and 2025 when the Los Angeles Dodgers won the championship. Though the team hoped for a historic three-peat this year—a third consecutive World Series win would have marked the first time a team achieved this since the New York Yankees came out victorious in 2000—they were eliminated by the Milwaukee Brewers in the playoffs.

Ahead of this year’s World Series, AD visits the third-oldest Major League Baseball stadium—behind Fenway Park (home to the Boston Red Sox) and Wrigley Field (home of the Chicago Cubs)—to learn more about its history. Below are a few highlights.

Dodger Stadium is known for its colorful seating, and architect Emil Praeger had a specific reason for arranging the seats in a gradient from yellow at field level to orange, turquoise, and sky blue at the top. “The choice of the colors was selected to mimic the sunset,” says Janet Marie Smith, executive vice president of planning and development for the LA Dodgers.

Every entrance of Dodger Stadium “is marked by something different,” says Smith, adding that landmarks like oversized Dodgers bobbleheads can be especially helpful when getting back to your car after a game. And of course, the stadium wouldn’t be complete without paying tribute to Jackie Robinson, who broke Major League Baseball’s color line in 1947. Alongside a bronze statue of the baseball great are some of his profound quotes chosen in consultation with his widow, Rachel Robinson, and his daughter, Sharon. They are displayed out front because his “power and impact on America wasn’t just on the playing field,” says Smith.

Players heading straight to Dodger Stadium from LAX may think they ended up exactly where they started. A hallway that serves as the entrance to the clubhouse is inspired by the tile mosaics at the airport that have appeared in movies like 1967’s Point Blank and 1997’s Jackie Brown. “We wanted to give honor to Janet Bennett, who designed the mosaics at LAX,” says Smith.

Because Dodger Stadium was in need of advertisement space, hexagonal signs bordering the field were created to “mimic the scoreboard,” says Smith. The six-sided polygon is found in various places at the venue—though some didn’t take shape until 2013, when additions to the venue were crafted from old sketches laid out in 1959. “We’re really pleased to have finally finished things that were authored half a century ago,” says Smith.

The outside of Dodger Stadium is just as important as the inside. In 1963, O’Malley invested in landscaping that would grow into lush botanical gardens just outside of its gates. “We’re so proud of having the only botanist on staff of any Major League team,” says Smith, noting that the green grounds have become one of the stadium’s “defining” features. Outside are more than 100 concrete planters shaped like oversized martini glasses, or champagne bowls, that the Dodger landscaping crew “lovingly restored” over the years.

#4813. Hayes details FLOP’s AI compute derivatives vision after selling his altcoins

Arthur Hayes revealed the derivatives market design for Flop Network and how he expects its native token could transform AI compute into a tradable commodity.

BitMEX co-founder and Flop Labs CEO Arthur Hayes shared new details about the Flop Network and how he expects its native token could turn AI computing power into a tradable commodity, two days after selling his altcoin holdings.

Hayes proposed measuring the computing power delivered for each AI inference task and using transaction data to derive a spot price for compute denominated in Flop (FLOP) tokens. He cited a lack of a universal benchmark price for AI computing power.

“If Flop becomes the payments network for AI agents, then it follows that the $FLOP/compute exchange rate will be the global benchmark for spot compute,” wrote Hayes in a Monday Substack essay, adding that a liquid spot compute market would enable derivatives based on that price.

Analysts at BCG in a July 2026 report considered how soon AI computing power could become a commodity. The consultancy projected that the market for AI compute will reach roughly $2.3 trillion by 2030 climbing from $360 billion in 2025.

Flop Labs is developing a network that allows AI agents to pay for computing and decentralized memory services. In August, Hayes said Flop was planning a “massive airdrop” in the fourth quarter of 2026 and announced that he would be the company’s CEO.

Hayes’ Flop explainer came in the wake of his statement on Saturday that he “dumped it all” and took profits from the Ether (ETH), Ethena (ENA) and Uniswap (UNI) token holdings in Maelstrom’s directional trading book, citing “dicey” macroeconomic and geopolitical conditions.

Lookonchain later flagged about $22 million worth of tokens sold by Hayes-linked wallets, including $15 million in ETH, $2.7 million in ENA, $2.2 million in UNI, $1.2 million in Ether.fi (ETHFI), $398,000 in Aerodrome Finance (AERO), $244,000 in Pendle (PENDLE) and $214,000 in Zama (ZAMA) tokens.

#4812. Baby manta rays stick to the shallows to avoid sharks and orca

Published Oct 22, 2026 12:00 AM EDT

Before giant manta rays (Mobula birostris) can grow to up to 30 feet long and 4,409 pounds, they must start small like most living things. Giant manta ray babies (called pups) in Central America don’t have their size to protect them from orca whales (Orcinus orca) and the numerous sharks in the open ocean. Their solution—sticking to the coast, where there is plenty of food to help them grow and defend themselves. A new study published today in the journal Frontiers in Fish Science details the baby rays’ behavior.

“We show that manta rays’ lifestyle isn’t a one-size-fits-all behavior. Giant manta rays in Central America undergo a dramatic shift in space use as they mature,” Jeffry Madrigal-Mesén, a study co-author and project leader of Mantas Costa Rica, said in a statement. “Juveniles remained strictly within shallow nearshore waters that serve as coastal refuges, but most of the tracked adults executed long-distance transboundary migrations along the continental shelf break into Central America.”

In the study, the team used multiple methods to track and identify mantas at five locations between 2018 and 2025. Acoustic telemetry uses underwater sound pulses to monitor fish movements and allowed the team to track 59 individual rays. With satellite telemetry, transmitters track movement and behavior from a distance and helped the team track large-scale movements of 12 individuals. Lastly, photo identification helped to identify individual manta rays.

“If we hadn’t used all three technologies together, we simply wouldn’t have been able to piece this puzzle together,” explained Madrigal-Mesén. “Combining these tools turned fragmented data into a unified, undeniable story.”

The team ended up with a dataset of over 12,000 detections. The data showed that young mantas (those with a body about 10 feet or less) stick to shallow coastal refuges. In these more shallow spots, the pups have a safe place that doesn’t overlap with their predators’ hunting grounds. They have plenty of food and places to hide, and even get essential services like cleaning from reefs.

“Mature adults can rely on their massive body size and their ability to dive deep to evade threats. But juveniles don’t have that luxury,” said Madrigal-Mesén. “What is truly amazing is seeing how strictly young manta rays stick to coastal waters as long-term refuges. We see the same individuals more often than once a year. In fact, just recently, they were sighted again at the exact same tagging location. It is always a joy to see them again—they have truly become a part of the family.”

By comparison, nine mature mantas in the study went on expansive migrations along the continental shelf break. One manta ray nicknamed Mickey Mouse because of a spot pattern on his belly had movements that suggest the fish also continue to display loyalty to a particular site once they grow up. Unusually approachable for a manta, Mickey Mouse allowed the team tag him multiple times over several years, which provided a continuous dataset. In 2020, he logged 462 acoustic detections at a coastal hub called El Secreto. He returned to the same shelf in 2023, and stayed there for 38 days.

“Nearshore cleaning stations transcend basic hygienic function; they act as primary social hubs where courtship and reproductive interactions take place,” said Madrigal-Mesén. “Returning to a predictable coastal depot allows mature rays to prioritize social and cleaning needs before undertaking large-scale regional migrations.”

Once they leave coastal regions, adult manta rays face different dangers. Coastal areas are often legally protected marine reserves, but the open ocean is another story. They no longer have legal protection and are exposed to dangers from industrial fisheries and vessel strikes.

According to the team, this shows that marine protected areas alone are not enough to protect this highly mobile species as it swims across Central America. They say that multi-national “conservation swimways”  that link the domestic juvenile coastal refuges with adult migratory corridors could help keep the endangered rays safe.

“This study opens a new chapter for manta ray research in Central America,” Madrigal-Mesén concluded. “We hope it sparks fresh enthusiasm and encourages future collaborators to join us in executing the new ideas and research projects we have planned for the conservation of this species.”

#4811. Crypto wallet maker Keystone to destroy almost 19,000 devices after security breach

Hardware wallet maker Keystone said it will destroy almost 19,000 unsold devices and suspend shipments after a website breach exposed customer information and a key that could let counterfeit wallets pass authenticity checks.

An attacker gained access to the company’s website on Oct. 20 through a flaw in a third-party data monitoring tool, Keystone said Monday in a post on X. The attack exposed 25,964 buyer records from 2024 through 2026, including names, contact details, shipping addresses and order information. Another 12,873 unpaid checkout records may have been affected.

The breach adds to a string of attacks on hardware wallet users this year. At Ledger, criminals fitted devices sold through resellers in Southeast Asia, with hidden components capturing recovery phrases during setup. Similarly, a flaw in Coldcard’s seed-generation software let attackers reconstruct private keys and drain wallets remotely.

The stolen Keystone key is built into devices during production and cannot be changed. It allows wallets to pass online authenticity checks, raising the risk that fake devices could appear genuine and put users' funds at risk, according to the firm. The company said it will destroy 18,835 unsold units.

Keystone said the breach did not expose recovery phrases, private keys or wallet credentials.

Some customer records should have been deleted under Keystone's 180-day retention policy, but an automated deletion tool failed without the company detecting the issue, it said.

“We failed to fulfill our commitment to erase customer data on time, and we accept responsibility for this failure,” Keystone wrote.

Keystone’s update came a day after hardware wallet maker Foundation disclosed a separate breach in which attackers sent phishing emails that appeared to come from its official domains.

Foundation said it had identified more than 23,000 phishing email attempts. It found no evidence that Passport devices or private keys were compromised.

Foundation also found older database copies that its 60-day data deletion process had missed. In an update, it said attackers could access records containing about 5,600 mailing addresses and 5,200 phone numbers, though it could not confirm how many were accessed.

Keystone said devices delivered before Oct. 20 remain safe to use if they pass authentication, while customers who received devices using the old key after that date should seek further checks before use.

The company said users do not need to move funds due to the breach alone and warned against fake recall or asset migration messages.

Keystone will replace the authentication key in new devices, shift website sales to preorders and cut customer data retention to 90 days, it added.

#4810. AI Agents Are Creating a New Layer of Cybersecurity Spending

AI Agents Are Creating a New Layer of Cybersecurity Spending

AI-focused cloud service provider Akamai launched a sandbox for AI agents on Tuesday, a sealed environment where code an agent writes can run without touching the rest of a company's systems. Agent Sandbox is in beta, alongside Akamai Inference, a service for running open AI models on Akamai's cloud.

The launch may be small but the problem behind it is not. Software is no longer just answering questions—it logs into accounts, writes code, moves money and acts on its own, and every one of those powers is something a hacker, or the software itself, can abuse.

This is what’s known as agentic AI, and it’s taking over the internet. A 2026 Palo Alto Networks survey of 2,930 security leaders found an average of 109 machine identities for every human, the logins and keys software uses to access other software. Of those, 79 are AI agents.

Agentic AI comes with a lot of perks, but also some serious dangers, especially when those agents have too much freedom, or are so intelligent they can bypass their own restrictions.

For example, Hugging Face, a major hub for open AI models, disclosed an intrusion on July 16, and OpenAI confirmed five days later that its own models were responsible. The research group METR later investigated the incident and found about 1,200 OpenAI agents had coordinated on a hidden message board, with roughly 700 joining the attack.

Turns out the agents escaped their sandbox and chained a previously unknown software flaw with stolen credentials to reach live systems. OpenAI called it a "warning shot." It said a monitor that reads a model's written reasoning was not running during the tests.

In simple terms, AI safety usually ends up being a tug of war game between alignment (the guys in charge of making sure AI is safe) and development (the guys in charge of making sure AI is powerful), with each team wanting the other to play a minor role in order to secure its own goals.

That is what Akamai wants to treat. “Enterprises should not have to choose between speed and control when they put AI into production,” Jon Alexander, Senior Vice President of Cloud Technology at Akamai said in a press release.

“Some teams want to ship an AI feature this quarter on a managed platform. Others need dedicated GPUs to run frontier-class open models on their own terms. Every team running agents needs a boundary around the code those agents write,” he said.

Hijacking is another fear. In July, Zscaler found web pages hiding instructions meant for AI agents, a trick called indirect prompt injection. One fake documentation page told agents to pay a $3 "license fee" in about 0.0012 Ethereum to a hardcoded wallet, and four of the 26 models Zscaler tested complied.

Back in May cybersecurity firm Bugcrowd saw submissions more than quadruple in three weeks in March, most of them fake. HackerOne and Nextcloud suspended paid programs in April.

The machines also find real bugs. Mozilla said an early version of Anthropic's Claude Mythos helped find 271 vulnerabilities in Firefox, all patched within the week. That flood of real and fake findings is what security companies are now racing to manage.

Palo Alto closed its CyberArk purchase on Feb. 11 to extend privileged-access controls to AI agents. In April it bought Koi, which secures AI coding agents running on employee machines. Cisco announced a planned acquisition of Astrix Security in May, a firm that governs the keys and tokens agents use, and completed the deal June 29.

Akamai's piece is the fence around the code itself. Its sandbox uses WebAssembly, a format that runs programs in a tightly walled box, to cap an agent's computing power, memory and outbound connections. Akamai's Alexander said every team running agents "needs a boundary around the code those agents write."

Palo Alto's survey found organizations expect AI agents to grow 85% over the next 12 months, against 56% for human identities.

#4808. Battlefield 6 gets a new map today alongside major changes to controller aim assist, and even more weapon dispersion tweaks

Right on schedule, the latest Battlefield 6 patch has arrived to kick off the next stage of Season 1.

Battlefield 6’s first season has entered its second phase. The game received a major update earlier today, version 1.1.2.0, which delivers a host of changes to the game, many of which have been expected.

The update, of course, also adds new content to the game in the form of the map Eastwood, as well as new hardware and fresh features for the multiplayer sandbox.

We’ve known about the new content side of today’s patch for quite some time. The update kicks off the California Resistance stage of Season 1, which is the second of three. The new Eastwood map takes place in California, and it’s a map cut from the larger Battlefield Redsec battle royale map of Fort Lyndon.

There’s a new limited-time mode called Sabotage - a tactical, 8v8 mode where two teams attempt to destroy as many bomb sites as possible. The other big new feature that arrives today is Battle Pickups.

These are powerful weapons which can be found in specific game modes (and in Portal), scattered around the map for players to pick up and use. They have limited ammo that cannot be replenished, and deliver incredible firepower.

Redsec players have a new Gauntlet mission to look forward to that joins the existing modes. It’s called Rodeo and it involves a lot of vehicular action. Finally, everyone gets to unlock two new weapons: the the DB-12 shotgun, and the M327 Trait revolver sidearm. There’s also a new angled grip attachment.

The fixes and tweaks side of the patch is arguably the more interesting. Controller aim assist has received a big overhaul that essentially resets everything to open beta settings. The goal is to make infantry targeting more consistent.

Controller players actually get the bulk of the changes in this update, which also improves input latency and stick response. Several issues that reset aim assist values, or make it behave erratically, have been addressed.

The conversation around weapon bloom/spread doesn’t look like it’s ever going to end, because Battlefield Studios is yet again tuning gunplay to fix unintended dispersion behaviour, and decrease its intensity across the board.

Certain Challenges have had their description rewritten so their requirements can be better understood. Beyond that, you can expect several deployables to behave much more consistently, including several bug fixes there, too.

#4807. Letter From the Editor-in-Chief: Is Worrying About Sleep Keeping Us Awake?

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

As a busy, sleep-deprived, middle-aged father of two young kids, including an irrepressible 8 year old ball of energy who is, without fail, up and ready to take on the world at 6 o’clock every morning (usually with a steady stream of questions about dinosaurs, the natural world and the origins of the universe in his back pocket), do I really need another set of headlines informing me that not only am I falling short in getting the requisite 40 winks a night but I’m also at risk of 83 diseases into the bargain?

Another day, another headline-grabbing sleep study lands, this time linking a greater amount of sleep – specifically sleep of the rapid eye movement (REM) variety, also known as dream sleep – to a lower risk of dozens of diseases.

The recent study published in PLOS Medicine found that REM sleep is linked to a lower risk of 83 diseases, including dementia, Parkinson’s, and multiple sclerosis (MS). As an added bonus, the study also found that a higher amount of deep sleep was associated with a lower risk of seven health conditions, including depression and type 2 diabetes.

So far, so alarming for a self-confessed “bad sleeper”.

Yet also fairly unsurprising. The accepted science, combined with anecdotal experience, has long told us that the right amount of good quality sleep corresponds directly with improved short and long-term health outcomes, both mental and physical. Evidence suggests that 7-9 hours of sleep per night is typically the sweet spot for a healthier life. Where this particular study differs from those that have come before is that scientists used wrist trackers to gather much more detailed information over a multi-year timeline about participants’ sleep stages, including irregular sleep patterns and variations in sleep duration. The findings were drawn from real-world data from more than 95,000 participants in the UK Biobank database.

Now, as the aforementioned and self-proclaimed VBP (very busy person – if you know, you know), who works long hours across UK and US timezones for the day job whilst serving as a sub-par encyclopedia for the real boss (the aforementioned 8 year old) outside of work, the general demands and pressures of daily life are more than sufficient to make consistently good sleep something that is all too often out of reach.

I know this to be true because the wearable I use reminds me of the fact with cold, hard data on a daily, if not hourly, basis. One of the first things my wife and I do each morning (after fending off existential questions from a third grader) is hawkishly plug into our Oura app to trade sleep scores and discuss what did or didn’t contribute to our invariably bad or, very rarely, good night’s sleep. Deep sleep, REM sleep, resting heart rate, heart rate variability, latency, even sleep debt – a laundry list of data points that we pore over in a futile attempt to make our sleep consistency optimal whilst living a life that doesn’t readily allow for it. Scientists have even given this phenomenon a name – Orthosomnia – where the anxiety of analyzing and perfecting sleep scores actually disrupts rest and intensifies poor sleep, and in the most extreme cases, insomnia.

It turns out we’re not alone. Results of a Gallup poll from 2024 – with the rather on-the-nose headline “Americans sleeping less, more stressed” – showed Americans report averaging 6.5 hours of sleep per night, comfortably below the recommended baseline of 7+ hours. Perhaps more tellingly, in a survey from 2024, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine found that nearly 70% of Americans report losing sleep due to anxiety.

Which brings me back to my original point. Anxiety-induced stress makes for the perfect bedfellow (pun intended) for a bad night’s sleep. The kind of anxiety-induced stress that can be both caused and amplified by obsessing over wearables data and a steady torrent of sleep-related content that aims to scaremonger rather than inform.

In fairness to the UK Biobank study, it is unequivocally important and unique research steeped in actionable real-world data and peer-reviewed medical science. And yet we live in a world increasingly awash with AI-generated content, sporting sensationalist headlines and powered by algorithms designed with an inherent imbalance to engage rather than empower. In that environment, gold-standard scientific work like this is all too often reduced to the lowest common denominator.

If you’ll indulge a minor tooting of our own horn here, this is precisely the inverse of how the team of wonderfully talented editorial misfits at Healthline Media thinks about and creates health information. We take research-backed science and our embedded clinical rigor and apply it to every aspect of our work. But we never lose sight of the fact that behind every study, there’s someone lying awake in the early hours running the math on their own risk factors, and that what they need in that moment is recognition that we are all at different stages on that journey, and a helping hand along the path to better health.

Life isn’t linear, sleep isn’t linear, and health is certainly not linear. Some nights I’ll get my seven hours. Plenty of others I won’t. And that is ok. Because either way, my alarm clock has a favorite dinosaur and a nonnegotiable 6 a.m. start time, and no peer-reviewed study or scary headline is going to change that. So I’ll take the data and the empathy, leave the anxiety, and see you all again at dawn.

If there’s something keeping you awake at night, or you just want to learn more about the wonderful world of sleep, we have a wealth of science-backed information at our dedicated Sleep Health hub. There you can also find Healthline’s Better Sleep Challenge – a three-week series designed to help you sleep better, feel more rested, and wake up ready to thrive each day.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#4806. Crypto could outperform AI stocks: Wall Street veteran Jordi Visser

Former Morgan Stanley and Weiss executive Jordi Visser says AI agents are boosting demand for crypto as he expands his holdings beyond Bitcoin, Ether and Solana.

Cryptocurrencies could deliver a better payoff from the artificial intelligence boom than AI stocks themselves, according to Visser Labs founder Jordi Visser.

Explosive gains in AI stocks are becoming harder to repeat, Visser tells Cointelegraph’s Trade Secrets. At the same time, AI agents are creating demand for crypto payments and tokenized assets.

“Without AI, I don’t think crypto would be going through this bull market,” he says.

Hyperscaler capital spending and free cash flow (left), and forward valuations compared with the S&P 500 (right). Source: JPMorgan Asset Management

As a result of his thesis, he has expanded his own holdings beyond Bitcoin, Ether and Solana into a broader basket of tokens.

Visser’s three-decade career includes stints as managing director at Morgan Stanley and chief investment officer at Weiss Multi-Strategy Advisers. He now heads 22V’s AI Macro Nexus Research.

The Wall Street veteran’s broader bet is that AI agents will allow people to instruct software to execute trades and other financial transactions on their behalf.

“AI agents are effectively the same as the App Store or the smartphone for crypto,” he says.

In that scenario, users would direct their agents while the software handles execution. The growing use of those agents will drive more activity in tokenized assets and crypto payments, extending the investment opportunity beyond the companies supplying computing power.

Related: AI potential to drive crypto demand remains ‘underappreciated’: BlackRock

But Visser’s crypto outlook still carries exposure to the AI trade he has grown more cautious about. He identifies a severe AI downturn that drags down equity markets as one threat to his bullish case.

“I don’t think that would end the bull market, but I definitely think it would retest the lows that we just saw,” he says.

Visser says he began reducing his exposure to memory chipmaker Micron in mid-May after concluding that hedge funds and retail investors had crowded into the AI trade.

“One of the reasons that Bitcoin and Ethereum were having such a hard time is because the focus was completely on AI,” he says.

By mid-May, Bitcoin was down 10.85% for the year and Ether about 25.86%. Source: TradingView

AI offered investors potentially greener pastures backed by rising business revenue. Microsoft’s quarterly report on April 29 claimed that its AI business was generating revenue at an annualized rate of $37 billion, more than double a year earlier. Meanwhile, Micron reported in March that its fiscal second-quarter revenue had nearly tripled to $23.86 billion.

But AI investors now face a less forgiving market, according to Visser. The 100-stock AI portfolio he tracks is up about 40% this year, but has become more volatile than Bitcoin on a rolling 126-day measure.

Related: Agents can trade 24/7 — but Yat Siu warns you could lose access at any time

Financing concerns have since hit AI infrastructure, as building them costs more than companies generate from their existing businesses at present.

For example, Oracle spent $55.7 billion on capital expenditures in fiscal 2026 against $32 billion in operating cash flow, and raised $43 billion in debt financing.

“I didn’t say AI was over. AI fireworks show is over,” Visser says.

He still sees several years of growth ahead in computing demand from AI agents, but says investors should temper their expectations for stock returns.

Until August, Visser’s crypto holdings consisted solely of Bitcoin, Ether and Solana. While Bitcoin remains his largest position, he has since started to acquire a broader range of tokens.

Dogecoin is a clear signal he watches for returning retail enthusiasm, which he considers essential to sustaining a broader rally.

“They’re the energy of the space and they’re the ones that are going to fuel this as other people get involved,” he says.

To track that broader market, Visser maintains an equally weighted index of 42 tokens and four publicly traded stocks. He says it has outperformed Bitcoin since June and plans to hold all its constituents.

Privacy is a theme he has already invested in.

“I’m not betting on one company, but I believe in privacy,” Visser says.

He says AI will make privacy and identity protection more important as fraud and other forms of abuse grow. Zcash and Monero among projects that fit that investment thesis.

#4805. Behind the Black Box Summit: The Event That Foreshadowed CrossFit’s Explosive Future

This exclusive excerpt from The Glass Box documents the early days of CrossFit—and its conflicts.

The following is an excerpt from The Glass Box: The Shocking True Story of How CrossFit Broke the Rules, Made Millions, and Changed Fitness Forever, a new book about CrossFit’s history that features reporting and interviews with the people involved, including Greg Glassman.

AT 1:45 PM on Saturday, November 21, 2009, a few dozen trim, healthy-looking men and women in their twenties and thirties were gradually returning to the aluminum folding chairs that had been arranged in the middle of CrossFit Central, having just finished a light lunch provided by the local Austin health-food chain My Fit Foods. A roughly five-hundred square-foot gym housed in a former body shop on Burnet Road in Brentwood, a flat, oak-lined neighborhood a few miles north-northwest of the Texas capital, CrossFit Central was founded in 2006 by Carey Kepler and Jeremy Thiel, siblings and former collegiate athletes who had been running boot camps in parks around the city before opening a place of their own. The air was mild but the slate-gray sky looked ominous—like a storm was coming.

Most of the people present were themselves gym owners operating CrossFit gyms—known in the business as “boxes”—in cities all around the country. Impressively muscled and swathed in athleisure, they had come to Austin at Thiel’s invitation to attend what had been dubbed the Black Box Summit—a three-day gathering of CrossFitters intended “for affiliates, by affiliates,” in the program’s official description. It was like an Apple keynote with an audience of Arnold Schwarzeneggers.

Thiel had put together the weekend with Dutch Lowy, a fellow Texan who owned a CrossFit box across from Texas A&M University in College Station. The event was inspired by CrossFit’s weekend seminars—conducted by CrossFit founder Greg Glassman and held at the original CrossFit box in Santa Cruz, California—which certified coaches in the methodology. The idea was to embrace the brand’s “open source” philosophy by pooling resources and sharing information, like a fitness-based meeting of the minds. “It was all centered around the CrossFit mentality of finding the best people, bringing them together, and figuring out what we can do to make this better,” Lowy explained. “That was the way CrossFit was initially developed: Take from the best in the business, develop a program that’s superior in every way, and always be changing.”

The desire to gather experts and showcase their unique expertise was one motivation for organizing the summit. The other was that CrossFit gym owners desperately needed help. Glassman was a dyed-in-the-wool libertarian with ties to the Cato Institute, the free-market think tank founded by Charles Koch. In business, he favored the “least rents” model in which a company would claim “a shrinking slice of the pie in a rapidly growing ecosystem.” CrossFit, Inc., charged prospective gym owners only a nominal fee for the rights to use the CrossFit name—just $500 in the early years—which Glassman boasted was a fraction of the capital required to open a Planet Fitness. But in exchange for this miniscule payment, box owners got nothing: no merchandising, no marketing materials, no business plan. Other than having a link on the CrossFit website, these budding entrepreneurs were on their own.

Granted, most people involved in CrossFit in those days appreciated the hands-off approach, at least conceptually. The price was so low that it was easy for boxes to justify, and the informality only added to the brand’s laid-back, renegade air. “There were these affiliates popping up all over the place, and there was really no regulation on what was going on,” said Michael Rutherford, a strength coach from Kansas City who was one of the summit’s plenary speakers. “It was like the old mining days in Leadville, Colorado. People were going out and sticking a flag in the ground and—boom— they were CrossFitting.” At the same time, the total absence of support meant that box owners—many of them former coaches without any real experience running a business—were struggling with standard operating procedures and best practices. It was a crisis that the Black Box Summit was designed to solve. “The original agenda was let’s help people get the answers they’re not getting as a CrossFit affiliate,” Rutherford said. If CrossFit wasn’t interested in providing this kind of information, they would have to provide it to themselves.

It was shortly before the summit, in mid-November, that Thiel arranged a call with the weekend’s speakers, including Rutherford and Lowy, and the box owners James FitzGerald, Greg Everett, Aimee Anaya, and Robb Wolf. He let them know that he’d called Glassman as a courtesy, to extend an invitation and to reassure him that they weren’t trying to steal any of CrossFit’s thunder. Glassman hadn’t seemed to have taken the news so well. “We’ll be there, because we have to keep an eye on what’s going on, not because this is fantastic and we want to be involved” was the gist of the response, in Lowy’s recollection. “It was very standoffish. Us against them,” Lowy said. Or as Rutherford put it, “CrossFit was clearly offended that this was going on. As if we were going to hijack the whole company and take everybody away.”

Everett was not surprised that Glassman was distressed, because this attitude was exactly what he had come to expect of him. Co-owner of the fourth-ever CrossFit affiliate, which he opened with Robb Wolf and his wife, Nicki Violetti, in Northern California, Everett often spent his weekends in Santa Cruz helping facilitate the certification courses. Though he was never as close with Glassman as Wolf, who thought of Glassman as a father figure, Everett had seen enough over the years to develop a clear impression of CrossFit’s mercurial founder: “It was very apparent to me that he was simultaneously this really intelligent, really charismatic guy, but extremely erratic, unreliable, and unpredictable,” he said. “We would joke constantly about how little we wanted to be legally associated with the CrossFit name, because we never knew what he was going to do. Was he going to bomb a building at some point? That was a running joke.”

To the people on the call with Thiel, word that Glassman knew about the summit was alarming. It wasn’t only Everett who had experience with his volatile temperament: Everyone involved in CrossFit knew that Glassman could be both charming and capricious, capable of both extravagant displays of generosity and sudden flashes of venom. “Greg is going to lose his mind,” one of the speakers on the call wagered. “He’s going to freak the fuck out. He’s going to think this is some kind of insurrection.” Thiel was adamant that it would all be okay. “No, no,” he assured them with characteristic brightness. “It’ll be fine. Everything is cool.”

Nevertheless, when Everett stood before the crowd as guests returned from lunch and took their seats at CrossFit Central that Saturday afternoon, he began to feel some serious trepidation.

It started the previous morning, when Everett walked into CrossFit Central right after dawn for the event’s 7 a.m. registration and found a familiar face already there waiting for him: Tony Budding, CrossFit’s longtime head of media and Glassman’s de facto right-hand man. Glassman had warned Thiel that he might send an emissary to attend the summit on behalf of the brand, someone who could represent the company’s interests and report back on whatever matters were discussed over the course of the weekend. But Everett had not been given a heads-up about this particular attendee, and to him it seemed like an ambush. “I was kind of like, what’s the story here?” Everett said. “This is not supposed to be anything to do with CrossFit HQ. Like, is he here to babysit? Is he here to report back to Glassman? It was a little weird.”

Both Budding and Dave Castro, a former Navy SEAL who had quickly become one of Glassman’s top lieutenants, had flown in from California for this event, though Budding maintained that they were welcome guests, or in any case, “certainly didn’t crash it.” Budding shared Glassman’s enthusiasm for debate and vigorous autodidacticism, and to his mind, the entire appeal of CrossFit was the intellectual stimulation its ideas could provoke. “You have to have a pretty involved background in the core questions of science, which is almost indistinguishable from epistemology, in order to understand why things were conveyed in a certain way rather than in other ways,” Budding explained. “I’ve always loved passionate debates. You get the best results from that. And if you’re a skilled debater, you make it about the ideas, not the person.”

The debates were harmless enough, in the beginning. At 11:15 a.m. on the first day of the event, Nicki Violetti gave a talk under the title “Introducing New Clients to a Program and Dedicated Points of Entry,” which touched on “On-Ramp,” a brief introductory session for new members designed to teach them the basic principles of CrossFit and ease them into the program’s intense regime. The idea, Violetti explained, was to mitigate the risk of injury and reduce the likelihood that someone might be overwhelmed: Don’t throw newcomers into the deep end, she advised; instead, let them slowly wade in. But this was starkly opposed to how the CrossFit methodology was taught at its own seminars. “The needs of Olympians and the needs of our grandparents differ by degree, not kind,” Glassman often declared, touting the accessibility of the program. From his seat in the crowd, Budding raised the issue and fervently disputed the point: Throw them in the deep end, he argued. How could anyone who coaches CrossFit not understand?

But it was during Wolf’s talk on “Nutrition in a Box” on Saturday morning that the mood in the room took a notable shift. A successful nutritionist and an early proponent of what he referred to as “the Paleo solution,” Wolf had frequently found himself in opposition to Glassman and CrossFit’s most loyal adherents, butting heads with his friend and mentor over the issue of the ideal diet for someone practicing high-intensity exercise. Glassman had long advocated for a low-carbohydrate, high-fat diet, and at the time had partnered with the dietician Barry Sears to promote his faddish Zone diet as part of CrossFit’s program. Though both diets involved cutting out grains and sugar, Paleo emphasized whole, unprocessed foods, akin to what might have been eaten by our ancestors millions of years ago, while the Zone focused on food categories—ratios of carbohydrates, fat, and protein—and was ambivalent about food quality. There were those within CrossFit who believed that it didn’t matter which specific diet someone followed so long as they were making predominantly healthy choices. But many at the top—including Castro—saw this quibble as a seismic fracture.

Though Castro had only been involved in CrossFit for a couple of years, he was rapidly becoming one of the most prominent figures in the growing organization, in large part because of his role as the frontman of the CrossFit Games, the now annual competition the brand had organized three years running on Castro’s family ranch. But among those present at the Black Box Summit, Castro had a different reputation: He was regarded as Glassman’s heavy. “He was a racehorse that was beat down and groomed and shown what to believe and how to talk. He was honestly Greg’s enforcer,” Lowy said. “Greg needed somebody like that, who could go in and be an asshole so that he didn’t have to. He would step into places where Greg didn’t want to be and he would cause a disruption.” If the speakers were apprehensive about Budding’s presence at the summit, Castro’s presence was enough to make them fear the worst. Wolf in particular was all but certain of it: Glassman had sent Castro to cause a scene.

Excerpted from THE GLASS BOX: The Shocking True Story of How CrossFit Broke the Rules, Made Millions, and Changed Fitness Forever by Calum Marsh. Reprinted with the permission of the publisher, HarperOne, an imprint of HarperCollins. Copyrighted © 2026 by Calum Marsh.

Calum Marsh is a journalist and the author of The Glass Box: The Shocking True Story of How CrossFit Broke the Rules, Made Millions, and Changed Fitness Forever. His bylines have appeared in The New York Times, The New Yorker, and The Guardian, among others.
