#4804. Some people really are werewolves—at least, in their minds

Published Oct 22, 2026 9:02 AM EDT

Dr. Nick Medford’s patient had plenty of reasons to be terrified of a simple blood test.

His body had shrunk down to a fraction of its size, or so he believed, and the physicians looming over him were enormous. Then there was the needle about to pierce his vein: to the patient’s eye, it was larger than his entire arm.

It might sound like something out of Alice in Wonderland. But this patient’s story belonged to a different genre entirely. Horror, namely. As far as the patient was concerned, there was no point in taking his blood. It was no longer human. Neither was he.

Medford’s patient was suffering from clinical lycanthropy, defined as the delusional belief that one is turning into a werewolf. And unlike the flesh-tearing beasts of folklore, who terrorize the unsuspecting by the light of the full moon, patients like Medford’s are often the ones haunted, trapped inside a body-horror nightmare.

According to Medford, his patient knew something was wrong, but was as confused about his condition as anyone. “Not only did he think he was a werewolf, but he would also talk about things like wanting to realign the cosmos and wanting to kill God,” says Medford, a neuropsychiatrist with the South London and Maudsley NHS Foundation Trust.

“And then you would say, ‘Do you believe in God? Are you religious?’ And he’d say, ‘No.’ His ideas didn’t really make sense even to him, which was a part of his distress.”

So, what causes this rare condition? And why does it take the form of one of Halloween’s classic monsters?

If you’ve never heard of clinical lycanthropy before, there’s a good reason: Hollywood’s werewolves easily outnumber the real-life cases of this condition. Only about 25 instances have been reported in the medical literature.

Because there are so few cases, Medford says it is difficult to identify a common cause or predictable disease course. However, he says that typically it is seen in patients already experiencing mental illness.

“In around 40 percent of published cases, it occurs in the context of schizophrenia,” Medford says. “There are also reports of it occurring in the context of bipolar disorder or psychotic depression. So, in the context of severe mental illness, basically.”

Medford’s own patient had been diagnosed with Huntington’s disease, an inherited, fatal brain disease that causes psychiatric symptoms and uncontrolled body movements. The case, which Medford documented in a 2023 report, was the only published instance of clinical lycanthropy associated with Huntington’s. Although Medford says it is uncertain what then led his patient to believe he was a werewolf, he says the physical changes associated with Huntington’s may have fed this idea.

“There may be some change in the person’s body or in their perception or their experience of their body,” Medford says. “That might be, if you like, the seed from which this idea can develop.”

This seems possible in the case of Medford’s patient, who exhibited involuntary writhing movements and other strange physical sensations.

“He did not seem to be aware that he was having these abnormal movements, but he was aware of some weird sensations,” he says. “He described freezing sensations and electric-shock-like sensations. He had some sense that something strange was happening in his body.”

But physical changes alone don’t explain why the patient imagined he was turning into a werewolf. Which raises the question: Why do these delusions so often focus on lupine metamorphoses?

They don’t always, Medford notes. There’s a broader term—therianthropy—for the delusion that one has turned into any animal. But most of these cases involve canines, he acknowledges.

“Most of them are wolves or dogs or something of that type,” he says. “And then there’s a whole range of other strange things. There’s a report of someone who thought they’d turned into a bee.” That would make for an underwhelming horror movie, to be sure.

So, is it a coincidence that many of these patients report delusions ripped straight from a creature feature?

Probably not. In their case report, Medford and his co-author, neuroscientist Natasha Sigala, note that “delusions are influenced by culture,” and few cultural images of human/animal shapeshifting are as deeply embedded as the werewolf.

“The werewolf folklore and werewolf mythology goes right back to antiquity,” Medford says. “And it’s kind of interesting that the werewolf or something like it seems to pop up in a lot of different cultures and different times and places.”

Why is the werewolf legend so potent and enduring? Medford suspects it may tap into something primal.

“The werewolf symbolizes something about the human condition,” he says. “We live in civilized societies, and we adhere to social norms, but there’s a sort of animal part of us that perhaps chafes against that.”

That internal conflict may explain some of the patient’s other fixations. He described urges to strangle his wife and to defecate in a church. Medford notes that Huntington’s may also have impaired his sense of disgust, which can happen as the disease progresses.

Yet the patient also fought against his own impulses. At times, he lunged at staff, only to pull back and apologize.

“You could see the conflict in that moment,” Medford says. In that sense, the patient wasn’t so far from the werewolves of fiction. Like a movie werewolf beholding his mirror image in horror, he looked at his reflection and saw himself changing in ways he couldn’t control.

For Medford’s patient, that dreaded blood test ultimately was part of a turning point in his treatment. The bloodwork allowed Medford to keep the patient on clozapine, a potent antipsychotic. As Medford increased the dose, the patient’s delusional beliefs dwindled, and although the underlying Huntington’s disease remained, the werewolf soon was gone.

Medford hopes patients like his will be treated with compassion rather than sensationalism. “These cases, they’re rare, but when they do happen, they tend to arise in the context of serious psychiatric or neurological disturbance,” Medford says. “They are people meriting sympathy and treatment, rather than romancing about it.”

#4802. The Tiny Chair Has Always Had Big Ideas

The Children’s Wishbone Chair is a design icon made for someone too young to know it’s an icon. The fun-size version of the 1949 classic, released by Carl Hansen & Søn in 2024, maintains the signature curved back and Y-shaped support of the original design as well as the chair's meticulous, 100-step production process.

The mini Wishbone is one recent example of adult favorites being remade for a younger (and smaller) customer, a practice that goes back decades but has gained fresh attention in recent years. Ligne Roset’s Mini Togo, introduced in 2007, shrinks Michel Ducaroy’s foam lounge chair for five-to-12-years old. Soon to follow was a child-size version of Mies van der Rohe’s Barcelona chair and Faye Toogood’s Roly-Poly. Paola Lenti premiered a full Kids Collection of downsized sofas, poufs, chairs, and tables at Milan Design Week in 2025. This year, Hamburg’s Museum für Kunst und Gewerbe places these launches within a much longer history. “Mini Furniture: Chairs for Children,” on view through the first of November, gathers more than 130 children’s chairs from two centuries, including high chairs, miniatures of adult classics, and furniture meant to support children’s motor, social, and creative development.

Hamburg’s Museum für Kunst und Gewerbe features an exhibit called “Mini Furniture: Chairs for Children,” on view through the first of November, gathering more than 130 children’s chairs from two centuries.

A chair is one of the first objects that asks a child to arrange their restless body to fit adult architecture. And across two centuries, children’s chairs have been used as a means to carry out competing adult agendas: They encourage the toddler to sit properly, do things independently, or use their imaginations.

By the late 1800s, Thonet had added child-size cane seating and high chairs to its catalog. A 1931 English model in the MK&G exhibition bears the straightforward name Correction Chair. It was used to straighten posture and enforce discipline in homes and schools; a small chair meant to prepare children for the larger, meaner world.

On the other end of the spectrum we have Erich Dieckmann’s 1925 children’s ladder chair, also included at MK&G, which doubles as a set of steps for reaching higher shelves. Montessori philosophy, meanwhile, preaches the virtues of the “prepared environment”—a child-centered space designed to encourage independence. Here, low, lightweight furniture allows children to move objects and begin activities without asking an adult for help. n each case, the furniture changes scale not simply to accommodate a smaller body, but to give the child greater independence.

Designers eventually accounted for the reality that a seated child rarely stays seated for long. Charles and Ray Eames’s molded-plywood Elephant—as seen in this modular home outside Madrid—designed in 1945, could be sat on, ridden, or treated as an animal. The idea that a child’s chair can do more than offer a place to sit continues to shape new designs. When industrial designer Henry Julier developed his children’s chair for USM, the Swiss furniture maker known for its modular system of steel tubes and ball joints, he couldn’t simply shrink an existing design. He had to find child-friendly proportions using those same parts. "At that scale, sitting, climbing, leaning and playing can become pretty interchangeable,” he says. The finished chair behaves more like a stool, while its “back” doubles as a handle that makes it easier for adults to move.

For many of the adults buying them, miniature chairs are design objects as well as children’s furniture. Lora Appleton, founder of children’s design gallery Kinder Modern, has seen interest in collectible children’s furniture grow as design-conscious parents, influencers, actors, and other high-profile families embrace living stylishly with children. In designer Abigail Turin’s Carpinteria kitchen, for example, children’s Panton chairs share the room with Eero Saarinen’s Tulip chairs—child-size furniture absorbed into the family’s design vocabulary rather than confined to a separate children’s world.

"Naturally, the purchasing decision is made by an adult, and many parents appreciate children’s furniture that integrates harmoniously into the overall aesthetic of the home,” says Knud Erik Hansen, CEO and third-generation owner of Carl Hansen & Søn. While children's furniture has "often been viewed as something transitional," to be discarded when the youngster grows, he says, the Children's Wishbone is designed to become an heirloom that can carry memories between generations.

A mini Jens Risom chair sits in front of custom laminate intarsia cabinetry in this San Francisco home by AD100 interiors firm The Archers.

Scaled down versions of adult designs, while beautiful, aren't always practical, Appleton says. Unlike furniture conceived around children’s needs, such as the simple bent-plywood pieces architects historically designed for schools, an adult icon made smaller can cost nearly as much as the original to produce, be outgrown quickly, or lack the stability and proportions needed for a child to use it independently. “Often, it’s just cute, but not functional,” she says. A miniature chair can succeed as an image while failing as a chair, eventually becoming, in Appleton’s experience, a catchall for clothes and miscellaneous bedroom debris.

Appleton evaluates children’s furniture according to safety, size, function, and play-ability: Can the child move it without help from a grownup? Can small fingers get caught? Will it remain useful as dexterity and interests change? She has watched children turn a miniature Panton chair on its side to support a fort, hide behind its curves, and wedge stuffed animals into its crevices. When she tested prototypes with her son, he often crawled beneath them and ignored the expected use entirely. Those detours would often be the inspiration that produced new design ideas.

Every tiny chair begins as an adult proposal for what it should be. It may be meant to teach posture or independence, invite play, cultivate an appreciation of craft, or eventually become an heirloom. The child makes a counterproposal: Turn it sideways and build a fort. The best tiny chairs leave room for that possibility.

#4801. Anchorage Positions Itself as Custody Backbone of US-Mexico Stablecoin Corridor

Anchorage Positions Itself as Custody Backbone of US-Mexico Stablecoin Corridor

Anchorage Digital is planting itself at the center of one of the world’s largest money-transfer routes, expanding a custody-and-listing arrangement with Latin American crypto platform Bitso to hold the stablecoins moving between the United States and Mexico.

Announced Tuesday, the deal puts Anchorage Digital Bank’s custody beneath both sides of the corridor. MXNB, Bitso’s Mexican peso stablecoin, is now custodied by the federally chartered crypto bank across six blockchains: Arbitrum, Base, Ethereum, Polygon, Avalanche, and Arc.

In turn, Bitso has listed USDPT and USAT, the U.S. dollar stablecoins from Western Union and Tether, both of which Anchorage issues and custodies. The companies did not disclose financial terms.

The target is the U.S.-Mexico remittance corridor, which moves tens of billions of dollars a year and ranks among the biggest cross-border payment flows on earth. Much of that money still travels over correspondent banking, wire transfers and cash-pickup networks that are slow and costly, while about 15% already runs through Bitso’s infrastructure.

Anchorage is betting that stablecoins, which settle onchain in seconds around the clock, will increasingly replace those legacy rails, and that doing so requires custody that is regulated and sits wherever the liquidity is.

“The US-Mexico corridor is where stablecoins stop being a thesis and start moving real money at scale,” said Nathan McCauley, Anchorage’s CEO and co-founder, arguing the firm is now “the institutional custody layer on both sides of that flow.” Ben Reid, Bitso’s head of stablecoin, said clients can now access the full dollar-peso corridor fully onchain, backed by a federally chartered bank.

The move extends a broader race to wire stablecoins into Latin America’s payments plumbing. Circle recently pushed deeper into the region with a USDC settlement pilot in Colombia, part of a wider expansion into dozens of emerging markets as stablecoins go mainstream.

For Anchorage, the deal builds on its deepening stablecoin footprint. The bank issues Tether’s U.S.-regulated USAT stablecoin, while Bitso has been pushing its MXNB peso coin across networks like Arbitrum. By anchoring both ends of the corridor, Anchorage is aiming to become the default custodian for any issuer or bank entering the route.

#4799. Battlefield 6 is getting a free trial next week with... a questionable selection of maps, but at least the modes are good

If Redsec got you hooked on Battlefield gameplay but you never experienced traditional multiplayer in Battlefield 6, there's an upcoming moment you might want to pay attention to.

Presumably as a way of marching ever forward towards its (alleged) 100 million player goal for Battlefield 6, EA has announced that the multiplayer shooter will be getting its first free trial later this month.

This is, of course, specifically for players to sample the core, paid component of the game, as the recently launched Redsec remains free-to-play. Redsec, however, offers modes like battle royale and Gauntlet, which aren’t quite the traditional Battlefield experience.

The Battlefield 6 trial arrives next Tuesday, November 25. It will run for one week, ending the following Tuesday, December 2. The trial will be available on all platforms, but won’t include all content from the full game.

Instead, players will have access to three of the game’s maps and five of its modes. Siege of Cairo will be there alongside Season 1’s two new maps: Eastwood, and Blackwell Fields. It’s not the strongest line-up of maps, considering Eastwood is already cut from the larger Fort Lyndon battle royale map that Redsec players should be quite familiar with, but it’s something.

As for modes, you can pick from Conquest, Breakthrough, Sabotage, and Team Deathmatch. Battlefield Studios didn’t say what the fifth mode is going to be, but a dedicated blog post with more information is set to arrive next week.

Beyond the trial's content itself, the timing is certainly great. Battlefield 6 just received one of its biggest patches, kicking off the second phase of its first season. So far this season, we’ve seen two new maps, several new weapons - but more importantly, solutions to problems the game’s had since launch, as well as fixes to longstanding bugs.

The miserable XP progression Battlefield 6 launched with is far more tolerable. Challenges and Assignments are much easier to understand and actually finish. The often maligned weapon dispersion has been toned down. Some of the wildest vehicle bugs have been fixed. And Portal has a host of experiences that go beyond just bot farms.

In essence, Battlefield 6 feels like its reached its 1.0 status, which makes this a great entry point for new players, even if the trial could've used more of the game's better maps.

#4798. FDA Issues Class II Blood Pressure Medication Recall, Over 13,000 Bottles Affected

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Some people taking a common blood pressure medication may have pills that don’t dissolve the way they should.More than 13,500 bottles of 25 mg chlorthalidone tablets are under a nationwide recall after failing lab tests, according to the Food and Drug Administration (FDA).Inventia Healthcare Limited voluntarily recalled the tablets on September 3, 2026, according to the FDA’s enforcement report.

The report lists the reason for the recall as “Failed Dissolution Specifications.” That means the tablets failed a test that measures how the medicine is released from the pill, according to the FDA.“Chlorthalidone is a diuretic, or ‘water pill,’ commonly used to treat high blood pressure and reduce excess fluid retention in the body,” Cheng-Han Chen, MD, a board certified interventional cardiologist at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, told Healthline.This is the second time this year Inventia has recalled its 25 mg chlorthalidone tablets.

The first recall was initiated on June 5 and covered 11,460 bottles. Both were for the same reason: failed dissolution specifications, according to FDA enforcement reports.

Together, the June and September recalls cover 25,027 bottles.

The FDA classified both recalls as Class II.

The two recalls cover three batches of the chlorthalidone tablets, USP, 25 mg, according to the FDA enforcement reports. All three expire in April 2027.

The 25 mg tablets are light yellow, round and flat, with “103” stamped on one side and nothing on the other, according to the drug label.

Recall initiated on September 3, 2026; 13,567 bottles affected.

Recall initiated on June 5, 2026; 11,460 bottles affected.

Patients whose supply is part of the recall “should contact their pharmacy to discuss obtaining a replacement and ask whether they should continue taking the medication in the meantime,” Chen said.

For Class II recalls, people can generally keep taking the medicine unless the recalling company gives other instructions, according to the FDA.

In some cases, stopping a medication may be more harmful than continuing to take the recalled medicine, the agency said.

If you’re concerned that your medication has been recalled, here’s what you can do:

A Class II recall means using the drug may cause temporary health problems, but the chance of serious harm is unlikely, according to the FDA.

“The voluntary recalls cited are classified as Class II, at the retail level,” an FDA Spokesperson told Healthline.

A retail-level recall means the company is “removing the product from retail stores and pharmacy shelves,” Ileana Elder, a branch chief in the FDA’s Office of Compliance, said in an FDA podcast. When a recall doesn’t extend to consumers, stopping a medication unnecessarily can be more harmful, and patients should contact their pharmacy with questions, Elder said.Healthline reached out to the companies for more information about the September recall, but had not heard back by the time of publication. The FDA referred further questions to the companies

However, based on a preliminary assessment of the batches recalled in June, Rising Pharma Holdings, a distributor of one of the recalled lots, stated that “no impact on patient health and safety is anticipated.”

“If a medication does not dissolve at the expected rate, it may not be absorbed by the body as intended,” Chen said. “This can reduce the amount of medication that reaches the bloodstream and potentially decrease its effectiveness.”

In a dissolution test, a lab places a tablet in a controlled liquid medium to determine whether and how quickly the active ingredient is released, according to FDA guidance on dissolution testing.

A failed result means the batch didn’t meet the release standard set for that product, according to FDA guidance on drug quality standards.Manufacturers propose and justify these standards, and regulators approve them as part of drug approval, according to the same guidance.

In the United States, the official standards stay the same from the time a drug is released until it expires, the guidance said.The September FDA enforcement report doesn’t say how the recalled tablets failed. A notice from Rising Pharma Holdings about the June recall provides more details.

Rising found two problems at the 18-month mark of ongoing quality testing of its 25 mg tablets, the company said in a notice posted by the California State Board of Pharmacy:

According to the drug label, chlorthalidone treats:

The label recommends that most people with high blood pressure start with one 25 mg dose per day. That’s the strength involved in both 2026 recalls.

#4797. USDC overtakes USDT in euro stablecoin trading as EU rules reshape market: Kaiko

USDC’s share of euro stablecoin trading has climbed to 56% since January 2024, with deeper liquidity as exchanges restrict USDT under MiCA.

Circle’s USDC (USDC) has overtaken Tether’s USDt (USDT) in stablecoin trading against the euro, as European Union regulations increasingly favor bloc-compliant stablecoins, according to a new Kaiko report.

USDC now accounts for 56% of stablecoin trading against the euro, up from just 10% in January 2024, according to Kaiko data. The shift accelerated after major exchanges restricted USDT trading for customers in the European Economic Area under the Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA).

USDC has also overtaken USDT in euro-denominated trading liquidity, with its order books now roughly 40% deeper than its rival’s. Tether remains the larger stablecoin globally, with a circulating supply about 2.5 times that of USDC.

Kaiko described stablecoin-to-euro trading pairs as a “main on-ramp and off-ramp for European customers,” allowing them to exchange euros for US dollar-pegged stablecoins when entering or exiting cryptocurrency markets.

USDC-to-euro trading pair overtakes USDC-to-USDT. Source: Kaiko

The findings come about two weeks after the European Banking Authority designated USDC as the EU’s first “significant e-money token” under MiCA, a classification that places the stablecoin under direct EBA supervision and subjects its issuer to additional regulatory requirements.

Related: Germany leads MiCA crypto authorization race as Europe’s deadline looms

Kaiko said the US is taking a similar approach to stablecoin regulation under the GENIUS Act, raising the possibility that American exchanges could see the same shift in trading activity observed in Europe.

On Oct. 21, the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency issued its first final rule under the law, establishing reserve and redemption requirements for stablecoin issuers under its supervision, effective Jan. 18, 2027.

Beginning in July 2028, US platforms will also face restrictions on offering stablecoins from issuers that do not meet the law’s approval requirements.

The regulatory changes could favor Circle, which has pursued authorization in both Europe and the US. Tether has taken a different approach, launching a separate US-focused stablecoin, USAT.

Related: Treasury Secretary Bessent likens GENIUS Act to Lincoln-era banking reform

#4795. What night is van Gogh’s ‘The Starry Night’? New analysis says it’s in July.

Published Oct 22, 2026 11:00 AM EDT

The swirling bluish-white mistrals, towering cypress trees, and glittery stars in Vincent van Gogh’s “The Starry Night” (1889) have captivated our imaginations for generations. Despite never selling a painting during his lifetime, van Gogh’s “The Starry Night” has since inspired a hit song in 1972, draws visitors to the Museum of Modern Art in New York City, and adorns everything from journals to scarves and tote bags.

But what particular night above Saint-Rémy-de-Provence, France, did the troubled post-impressionist painter actually put on the canvas? A new analysis points to July 22, 1889. During the early morning hours at that time of year, a waning crescent moon, the planet Venus, and the Hyades star cluster in Taurus appeared close together—all of which are seen in the painting. A study published today in the journal Scientific Reports details the findings.

According to the Metropolitan Museum of Art, van Gogh was inspired by the view from his window at the Saint-Paul-de-Mausole asylum in southern France. He spent twelve months there 1889–1890, seeking reprieve from his mental illnesses and was also diagnosed with a form of epilepsy.

Like many of his paintings that year, “The Starry Night” was created in several sessions during the day, under different weather conditions. The village nestled below the hills was also based on other views, and could not be seen from his window. Although van Gogh reconstructed some of the features in the night sky as he saw them, he also altered the celestial shapes and added a sense of glow.

“Van Gogh assigned an emotional language to night and nature that took them far from their actual appearances,” the museum writes. “Dominated by vivid blues and yellows applied with gestural verve and immediacy, ‘The Starry Night’ also demonstrates how inseparable van Gogh’s vision was from the new procedures of painting he had devised, in which color and paint describe a world outside the artwork even as they telegraph their own status as, merely, color and paint.”

Using astronomy to pinpoint which day inspired the painting began with UCLA art historian Albert Boime in 1984 and Harvard University astronomer and astrophysicist Charles Whitney in 1986. The two scholars reached conflicting conclusions, and neither of them displaced the prevailing religious and symbolic interpretations of the painting.

Historically, the painting has been dated based on a letter van Gogh wrote to his brother Theo around June 18, 1889. However, the waning gibbous moon from mid-June does not account for the slender crescent seen in the painting. By July 22, 1889, the Hyades star cluster rose in the eastern sky alongside Venus, and the moon’s shape looked closer to the crescent on the canvas. This new study uses observational geometry to find an answer.

“To an observational astronomer, the cluster of stars in ‘The Starry Night’ is the Hyades in Taurus,” said study co-author Elijah Jeong-hyun Kim.  “But the moment I read the earlier studies explaining it as Aries, I heard the painting calling to me.” Kim is the CEO at South Korean robotic telescope company SLLAB and a PhD candidate at Kyung Hee University.

For this research, the team combined astronomical simulations and measurements of star positions, with historical weather records, and van Gogh’s own letters. They also used earlier sketches of the landscape to reconstruct the narrow field of view represented in “The Starry Night.”

According to the team, the crescent moon, Venus, and the Hyades star cluster in the constellation Taurus would have been visible around dawn on July 22, 1889. This configuration generally corresponds to what is portrayed in the painting. Weather records suggest that the night sky around July 22 would have been clear enough for this astronomical arrangement to be visible. Additionally, the frequent mentions of cicadas in van Gogh’s letters suggest that the region was seeing a period of hot weather, which matches the recorded temperatures in July 1889 better than in June.

The team also verified that the painter’s field of view in another painting titled “Starry Night over the Rhône” (1888) with photographs taken on site was painted when the sky was in Aries. Using the same method, the team also believes that the stars in “Café Terrace at Night” (1888) may be the Summer Triangle.

“I hope this work prompts a renewed, multidisciplinary look at the sequence and working methods of the Saint-Rémy paintings, and at Van Gogh’s movements that summer,” Kim said. “Our conversation with Van Gogh’s other skies is still ongoing. We plan to tell those stories one by one.”

#4794. The 86 Best Movies of 2026 So Far (and 17 More We Can't Wait For)

With Christopher Nolan, star-studded epic romance, and major Marvel events, this list is a behemoth. And it also features a movie called Behomoth!.

AS A HUGE fan and follower of both movies and sports, the former has one major advantage: There's always something to look forward to. As a fan of the New York Jets, I don't always have that luxury. As a fan of watching films, though, not only is there always something worth my time on the big screen or streaming, but there's always several flicks down the line that will sound great or intriguing for one reason or another. Will they all turn out great? Of course not. But with sports and movies alike, excitement tends to be all about hope.

After a particularly strong 2025—movies like One Battle After Another and Sinners proved to be true standouts—how does 2026 pick up? With strength and energy, that's how. The year has been filled with all sorts of great movies, from horror (like 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple and Obsession) to epics (like The Odyssey), to some pretty great comedies (like Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie and The Invite). It’s been a good year.

But we always like to look at not only what’s been good to date, but also what we can still be excited for—and that includes everything. We’re talking stuff on the calendar already, stuff we suspect could come out this year, and even stuff making noise in places like the Sundance Film Festival. Throughout the year, this list evolves from our most anticpated movies into our favorite movies, and always looks quite different in December than it did in January.

For now, we've got a lot we’ve loved, but still a lot to look forward to as well. Below, we've listed nearly 100 movies on our radar that we think you'll be really interested in seeing.

If you're interested in a killer chimpanzee B-movie slasher, then Primate is for you. The movie delivers what it promises. Ben the chimpanzee does some very terrible things on screen but it makes for quite an enjoyable experience.

28 Years Later: The Bone Temple is a direct sequel to last year's 28 Years Later. And while the great Danny Boyle doesn't return to direct, the movie is helmed by the more-than-capable Nia DaCosta and once again counts Alex Garland as its screenwriter. The real assets, though, are the movie's leads: Ralph Fiennes and Jack O'Connell are sensational, magnetic every minute they're on screen amidst all sorts of post-apocalyptic chaos. Bring on part three.

Here's a quick pitch for The Rip: Matt Damon and Ben Affleck. The longtime friends team up once again for The Rip, a cop thriller in the vein of movies you've probably seen several times before... but done particularly well. This twisty crime story involves a police team finding a ton of money in a stash house (the titular rip), and figuring out who may or may not be corrupt. In addition to our beloved duo, the cast is shockingly stacked: Steven Yeun, Kyle Chandler, Scott Adkins, and One Battle After AnotherAcademy Award nominee Teyana Taylor are all on board.

SAM RAIMI FANS, ASSEMBLE! For the first time since 2009, the master of gross effects and frenetic cameras is back with an original horror story in Send Help. Rachel McAdams plays a Survivor superfan who gets stuck on an island with her dickhead boss (Dylan O'Brien), and then the antics begin. Think Cast Away meets Misery meets Drag Me to Hell. It's great stuff.

Jason Momoa and Dave Bautista are a pair of cool dudes who, with The Wrecking Crew, set out and did exactly what they intended to do: Make a badass buddy action comedy. The movie finds the pair playing estranged brothers who reunite in Hawaii to investigate their father's mysterious death; From there, we get lots of jokes, and a lot of (very violent!!!) action. It's a good time! Honestly, if they want to make a couple sequels to this, I'd be her for it.

Part This Is Spinal Tap, part Josie and the Pussycats, and part Curb Your Enthusiasm, The Moment is Charli XCX's movie for anyone who enjoys her music, persona, or both. A really fun movie about music, stardom, and celebrity in 2026.

Pillion was technically released initially in 2025 (and was also included on our list of favorite movies of that year), but we didn't want to miss an opportunity to recommend it to people once again. This unique film follows a man (Harry Melling) who engages in an unexpected BDSM relationship with a biker he meets at random (Alexander Skarsgård). The movie is quite the tonal balancing act, at times funny, intense, raunchy, and heartbreaking, but it all comes together by the end. A delightful movie, and a great showcase for both leads.

The classic novel comes to life on the big screen once again, this time from Promising Young Woman and Saltburn director Emerald Fennell, and with Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi in the lead roles. Fennell, an Academy Award winner for her work on Promising Young Woman, has both big fans and big detractors at this point—but her take her, while book purists haven't been thrilled, is a big, visually stunning epic romance. Robbie and Elordi are both up to the task as well, bringing a charged energy to roles that really need it. Alison Oliver, who recently shined on HBO's Task, is another major highlight in a supporting role. An original soundtrack from Charli XCX helps to set the anachronistic mood and feels like a real cherry on top.

This action-thriller, based on a book by Don Winslow, looks like a very solid entry in the genre that we're going to call "Somewhat Resembles a Michael Mann Film." And reviews have confirmed as much—the movie has often been compared to Heat, and while its tough to compare to Mann's 1995 masterpiece, we'll take just about anything in that vein. Look: If someone tells you there's gonna be a crime thriller in the vein of Heat with Chris Hemsworth, Mark Ruffalo, Halle Berry, and Barry Keoghan leading the cast, how could you not be on board?

One of our coolest directors, Gore Verbinski, makes his grand return with Good Luck, Have Fun, Don't Die—his first movie since 2016's A Cure For Wellness. If you don't know Verbinski by name, you should know several of his movies: the original Pirates of the Caribbean trilogy, The Ring, and Rango are among the standouts. Good Luck follows Sam Rockwell as a man who claims to be from the future and fighting off a rogue artificial intelligence. While the movie takes some pretty tough and labored tangents into on-the-nose satire, Rockwell's performance and the practical effects keep the movie afloat as a fun sci-fi romp.

Despite the title, you don't need to know anything else about Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie before going to see it. Sure, this story of two inept dudes in a band who just want to play music at a bar—and go through all sorts of ordeals, including time-travel, to do it—has existed before in both web series and television series form. But you can just go in and laugh and the absolute madness of the movie. Trust us—you'll have a good time with this one if you're into "madness that makes you laugh." This is a long-term passion project of Matt Johnson, who became well known recently for directing the underrated BlackBerry. His brash talent is again on display here.

We're always in on Glen Powell, and How to Make a Killing marks his first foray into A24's world as a leading man. This black comedy neo-noir comes from Emily the Criminal director John Patton Ford and follows Powell as a man who decides to take out a few members of his family in order to increase his chance at making a whole bunch of money via inheritance. The supporting cast joining Powell isn't too shabby: Margaret Qualley, Jessica Henwick, Ed Harris, and Topher Grace are all along for the fun.

The Napa Boys is a very specific cut for comedy fans. Remember when all those nondescript American Pie direct-to-video sequels were coming out? "American Pie Presents:.."? The Napa Boys imagines itself as one of those: self-impressed raunchy comedy with little tact—crossed, naturally with the wine bro nature of 2004's Sideways. As a result, we get a movie that presents as a sequel to a franchise that both has long been running out of juice, and, in our world, never was. If you get the truly absurd brand of parody being presented here, you're going to get a real kick out of it.

Hoppers feels like a throwback to the golden age of Pixar. Not only is it an original story (at a time when the legendary animation studio has often been leaning into sequels, prequels, and more), but it's a sweet and funny one at that. Following an animal-lover who uses Avatar-style technology to transfer her consciousness into a robotic beaver body that can discover what animals are thinking and saying, Hoppers actually turns into something of a conspiracy mystery story. With a voice cast that includes Jon Hamm, Dave Franco, and Meryl Streep among others, it's a fun one for the whole family.

Look. This is one of those movies that you really don't have to overthink. It's Alan Ritchson—Reacher himself—taking on a giant alien robot monster thing. This is as much of a throwback to '80s-style action filmmaking as we're ever going to get, and it delivers as promised. The fact that the rest of the cast includes staples like Jai Courtney, Esai Morales, and Dennis Quaid is just a bonus.

Undertone has been positioning itself as A24's next huge horror hit—and we'll see how it lands. The filmfollows a supernatural podcaster who gets sent some particularly spooky recordings; Its soundscape has brought tons of hype, with many calling it the scariest experience of the year. A simple premise like that sometimes makes for a very solid horror movie.

Based on the novel by Andy Weir(who also wrote The Martian), Project Hail Mary is a story of space exploration and a regular guy (played by Ryan Gosling) who gets involved. Phil Lord and Chris Miller (who are responsible for the Spider-Verse movies and directed 21 Jump Street) are behind the camera for this one, which has already proven to be a big, big hit. Gosling is basically on his own out in space for most of this, but it also includes a fun little alien guy named Rocky and a nice supporting performance by Anatomy of a Fall star Sandra Hüller. The visuals here are worth seeing on the biggest screen you can find.

2018's Ready or Not was one of the most fun horror films of the last decade, and now eight years later we get to see Samara Weaving's lead sucked back into the same twisted, bloody, violent games. The supporting cast—including David Cronenberg (!!!), March MH Flex star Elijah Wood, Sarah Michelle Gellar, and The Pitt's Shawn Hatosy—is fantastic. The movie kind of keeps with the form of the first Ready or Not, so if you liked that one, you'll certainly have a fun time with this one.

Forbidden Fruits follows a secret coven of witches operating in a shopping mall. Like, guys, some premises you just have to accept for how good they are and see them out. The movie is a campy, fun, twisty turny ride. Understand what you're getting into—a little bit of horror, a little bit of satire, and some fun twists—and it's going to be a good time.

Zazie Beetz (Atlanta, Deadpool 2) gets to lead her own horror/thriller movie with They Will Kill You, where she plays a woman who answers a help-wanted ad for a housekeeper in a luxury apartment building, only to find herself fighting for her life. The movie is pretty similar in tone to fellow horror/comedy Ready or Not 2, which we just talked about. But They Will Kill You leans more into its John Wick-style action and Kill Bill style plot, which sets it apart. The supporting cast, which includes Patricia Arquette, Heather Graham, and Industry star Myha'la, make it extra worth it.

#4793. Battlefield 6 streamlines some of the most annoying Challenges yet again, takes out a few of them entirely

The easier and more accessible Battlefield Studios makes those Battlefield 6 Challenges, the better.

The work to continue streamlining Challenges in Battlefield 6 is clearly not done. Having just released a new patch - which already included several updates to Challenges and Assignments - Battlefield Studios has gone in and made even more changes.

This latest round includes far fewer tweaks, but quite surprisingly, it also removes a few of the more annoying ones from the game.

The updated list, which Battlefield shared on Twitter, includes changes to the dreaded Recon 2 headshots beyond 150m Challenge. Now, any headshot beyond 75m will count. Recon Expert 3 is another headshot-themed one that’s been updated, and now requires getting headshot kills beyond 125m, rather than the practically impossible beyond 200m that it required previously.

The Rapid Fire 2 Challenge, which required getting kills beyond 50m with Assault Rifles, has been changed so it now tracks anything beyond 40m. The painful ‘Inflict damage under 15m while hip-firing with Assault Rifles’ has been entirely replaced with one that requires - the far more sensible - inflicting damage while in ADS with Assault Rifles.

A couple of Challenges, such as the one that required suppressing enemies with LMGs, and another that wanted you to deal damage to laser-designated vehicles using lock-on weapons, have both been removed.

The journey to make Challenges and Assignments easier to complete has been a long one. Progression has been one of the key areas of criticism Battlefield 6 received at launch. The issue encompassed more than just slow player and weapon XP earn rates, it also prevented players from accessing new Training Paths for the various classes, and several of the game's weapons.

Battlefield Studios attacked the problem from multiple angles, initially boosting the XP players and their weapons would earn, before later reducing the requirements of the Challenges themselves. The developer continued to streamline those requirements over multiple patches, including majorly overhauling the system earlier this month.

It seems the studio is more confident than ever in the game, as it recently announced that Battlefield 6 will be getting its first free trial next week, offering players who do not own the full game access to iconic modes like Conquest, and Breakthrough - though on an unusual selection of maps.

#4792. Robinhood Taps Ripple's Stablecoin, Treasury Software and Trading Loans

Robinhood Taps Ripple's Stablecoin, Treasury Software and Trading Loans

Robinhood, the trading app with 28.6 million funded customers, is adding a digital dollar built by Ripple, the company whose founders created the XRP cryptocurrency. Ripple's RLUSD token went live Tuesday on Robinhood's own blockchain and on its Bitstamp exchange, and is expected to reach the main Robinhood app later this year, according to Ripple's announcement.

RLUSD is a stablecoin, a crypto token designed to always be worth one U.S. dollar, which lets money move across a blockchain without waiting for banks to open. It launched in December 2024, and Ripple said its market capitalization, meaning the combined value of every token in circulation, now tops $2.5 billion.

Robinhood will also run its global treasury operations on Ripple Treasury, software that manages a company's cash, from payments to cash-flow forecasts and risk. That includes intercompany netting, which offsets what a company's own divisions owe one another so fewer payments have to move.

Ripple Prime, a prime broker that lends money and clears trades for hedge funds and other large institutions, already provides Bitstamp with lending and margin financing, meaning borrowed money for trading, across roughly 20 digital assets. Ripple paid $1.25 billion for the firm in a deal announced in April 2025, when it was still called Hidden Road.

Ripple Prime clients also get access to spot trading on Bitstamp, which means buying at the current market price, and later this year Prime will support perpetual futures there, contracts that bet on a coin's future price and never expire. Robinhood closed its purchase of Bitstamp, one of the world's longest-running crypto exchanges, in June 2025 for about $200 million, and most of the exchange's volume comes from institutions.

Robinhood will use RLUSD on the XRP Ledger, the blockchain behind XRP, for institutional settlement, the step where a large trade is finalized and paid for, across Bitstamp's institutional business.

“Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations, and Ripple Prime for financing,” said Johann Kerbrat, senior vice president and general manager of Robinhood Crypto and International. “That is what a connected, global financial system looks like.”

Robinhood, which pared back its crypto listings during the SEC crackdown on Ripple and other crypto companies, added XRP for U.S. customers in November 2024. Now Ripple's software will help run Robinhood's own treasury department.

Robinhood cut about 10% of its staff in June, after revenue fell 34% in its crypto transaction business in the first quarter to $134 million. It will report its third-quarter results after the market closes on Tuesday, the first report to cover a full quarter since the July 1 launch of Robinhood Chain.

#4791. Janus Henderson, Centrifuge launch tokenized Nasdaq-100 fund on Stellar

The blockchain-based fund offers eligible non-US investors exposure to the Nasdaq-100 as Janus Henderson expands its institutional tokenization business.

Asset manager Janus Henderson and tokenization platform Centrifuge have launched a blockchain-based fund tracking the Nasdaq-100 Index on Stellar, offering eligible investors outside the United States exposure to the benchmark.

Called JQQQ, the fund uses Centrifuge’s tokenization infrastructure and seeks to track the Nasdaq-100 under a licensing agreement with the exchange, according to a Tuesday announcement.

The launch expands Janus Henderson’s onchain investment offerings beyond US Treasurys and structured credit into equity-index exposure. The asset manager said its institutional onchain strategies now exceed $1 billion.

JQQQ is structured as a segregated portfolio of a British Virgin Islands investment company and provides indirect exposure to the Nasdaq-100 rather than ownership of the underlying stocks. The fund targets eligible professional investors outside the US, according to the Wall Street Journal, which reported a minimum investment of $100,000.

The launch follows Nasdaq’s decision in April to expand licensing of its Nasdaq-100 Index to additional asset managers, including BlackRock and State Street, while maintaining its longstanding partnership with Invesco, which operates the $499 billion market cap Invesco QQQ Trust exchange-traded fund.

Related: US Bank tests proprietary stablecoin in cross-border Stellar transaction

The Janus Henderson launch expands Stellar’s growing tokenization business. The Layer-1 blockchain currently ranks fourth among blockchains by distributed real-world asset value, with approximately $3.8 billion in tokenized assets, behind Ethereum, BNB Chain and Solana, according to RWA.xyz.

The network hosts tokenized assets from major issuers including Spiko, Franklin Templeton and Ondo. In July, tokenization platform Tradable announced plans to bring up to $1 billion in private credit assets to Stellar, with $500 million expected at launch.

Stellar is also part of a planned expansion by the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), which announced in May that it would connect its tokenization service to the network. DTCC-tokenized assets are expected to become available in the first half of 2027, potentially including US Treasurys, major index ETFs and Russell 1000 stocks.

Despite the expansion of Stellar’s tokenized asset market, its native token, XLM, has fallen about 16% year to date, trading near $0.17 on Tuesday, according to CoinGecko data.

#4790. Japan's top yen stablecoin falls short of its target in a disappointing first year

Japan's JPYC stablecoin has fallen well short of expectations in its first year, reaching just a quarter of its issuer's circulation target despite a string of corporate partnerships.

“Frankly, I don’t think it’s growing,” CEO Noritaka Okabe told NADA NEWS in an interview, acknowledging that the yen-backed token had failed to gain the traction the company expected. He said trading volume was “not bad,” but wasn’t satisfied with the amount in circulation.

Only 3.2 billion yen (around $20 million) was in circulation as of Oct. 23, compared with cumulative issuance of 12.1 billion yen, Nikkei reported. That data is near the $20.85 million DeFiLlama shows, and reflects tokens that have since been redeemed, underscoring the challenge of keeping JPYC in circulation.

Circulation briefly topped 4.2 billion yen in September after South Korean exchange Upbit listed JPYC. The listing pushed its market price above 1 yen and sent traders rushing to mint new tokens for arbitrage.

Okabe estimated that about 35 billion yen changed hands across Upbit and Uniswap in a single day, before circulation fell back to around 3 billion yen.

That matters for JPYC's business model as the stablecoin is backed by bank deposits and Japanese government bonds, with its issuer earning interest on those reserves. Less money in circulation means less potential interest income.

Okabe blamed the disappointing results in part on a 1 million yen daily issuance limit tied to Japan's Type II funds-transfer rules. JPYC eased its restrictions in May, allowing 1 million yen per request rather than per day, but still restricts rapid repeat transactions.

The cap has frustrated corporate adoption. Software company Asteria, which plans to hold 1 billion yen in JPYC, has been forced to build its balance through installments.

The weak results also contrast with JPYC's growing list of partners. Logistics firm AZ-COM Maruwa signed an alliance in July to develop payments for about 2,300 business partners.

SBJ Bank and LINE NEXT also agreed earlier this month to test bank-linked stablecoin wallet top-ups, though the project remains in the trial stage. Meanwhile, Japan's Financial Services Agency is reviewing stablecoin regulations, with a roadmap expected in early 2027.

Okabe now wants circulation to reach tens of billions of yen in JPYC's second year, a roughly tenfold increase from current levels.

#4788. Cutting Back on Ultra-Processed Foods May Help Ease Depression Symptoms

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Emerging research has linked high consumption of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) with an increased risk of depression. Now, a small study published September 23 in JAMA Psychiatrysuggests that reducing UPF intake may help ease depression symptoms.

There is no universally accepted definition of UPFs. However, a widely used classification system known as NOVA defines them as industrial formulations made primarily from substances extracted or derived from foods, often with additives not typically used in home cooking. Examples include soft drinks, packaged snacks, candy, sausages, hot dogs, and pastries.

Previous research has linked UPFs to an increased risk of depression, anxiety, eating disorders, and other mental health conditions. In the new study, participants reduced their UPF intake by more than 85% and had significantly lower depression scores across three standard measures while following the low-UPF diet.

This small study included 20 participants who:

The participants also had to agree not to change medications, therapy, exercise, or sleep during the study.

Of the 20 participants enrolled in the study, 19 completed it. The average age was 44. Participants followed both their usual diet and a low-UPF diet during separate phases of the study, with the order randomly assigned.

Participants generally found the low-UPF diet acceptable and manageable. They also had lower depression and anxiety scores during the low-UPF diet phase compared with their usual diet.

On one commonly used depression measure, the PHQ-9, average scores declined from 8.68 during the usual diet phase to 5.53 during the low-UPF phase.

The study had several limitations, including its small sample size, reliance on self-reported dietary data, and uncertainty about which aspects of reducing UPFs may have contributed to the changes in symptoms.

The researchers also said that larger studies are needed to determine whether reducing UPF intake can improve depression symptoms.

Michelle Routhenstein, Preventive Cardiology Dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the study, agreed, noting that more research is needed to understand whether reducing UPFs directly improves depression symptoms or whether other aspects of the overall diet may be contributing to the observed benefit.

Dara Houp, PsyD, a licensed clinical psychologist with Pathways Psychological Services in NYC, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that it’s important to distinguish ultra-processed foods from processed foods more broadly.

Routhenstein agreed that the distinction is important. “Processing is not inherently negative. Many nutritious foods are processed in some way.”

“I would not encourage people to simply label a food as ‘processed’ or ‘unprocessed’ and make decisions based on that label alone. I prefer to look at the overall dietary pattern, the nutritional value of the food, and what role that food plays in the diet. A packaged food can still provide meaningful nutrients and be part of an overall nutritious eating pattern,” she said.

There are various proposed mechanisms for how UPFs may affect mood.

For example, UPFs are often low in fiber, vitamins, and minerals, which can play important roles in brain health.

Houp noted that some ingredients found in UPFs, including artificial sweeteners such as aspartame, may affect the gut microbiome.

“Our gut is a primary location for producing neurotransmitters, responsible for manufacturing the vast majority – upward of 90% – of our body’s serotonin. When the gut microbiome is disrupted, it directly interferes with healthy neurotransmitter production,” she said.

Houp added that diets high in UPFs may be lower in antioxidant-rich foods, such as fruits and vegetables, which could contribute to oxidative stress. Research suggests that people with depression may have lower antioxidant levels and higher markers of oxidative damage.

Routhenstein also noted that researchers are investigating how UPF consumption may influence inflammation, metabolic health, and the gut-brain axis, all of which may have implications for mood.

Reducing your UPF intake can start with small, sustainable changes.

Routhenstein suggested starting by looking at the foods you eat most often rather than trying to overhaul your kitchen.

“Identify one or two foods that are frequent sources of UPFs and consider what you could realistically replace them with. For example, you might swap a daily sugary drink for water or sparkling water, or choose fruit, nuts, yogurt, or another minimally processed option in place of a packaged snack,” she said.

Other ways you can help reduce UPFs include:

Routhenstein added that you don’t have to eliminate convenience from your diet. Frozen vegetables, canned beans, canned fish, oats, plain yogurt, tofu, and other minimally processed foods can make healthy eating much more practical.

“The goal is not to eat a perfectly unprocessed diet. It is to gradually shift the overall pattern toward more nutrient-dense foods that provide fiber, protein, healthy fats, vitamins, and minerals,” she said.

#4787. This Colorful 400-Square-Foot Apartment Makes Room for Everything Its Owner Needs to Live a Vibrant Life

“At first glance, it looks composed and serene, but the more time you spend in the apartment, the more unexpected details you discover,” says interior designer Mila Gench, founder of Gench Design, of the 400-square-foot apartment in Moscow that she recently designed for a young professional in tech.

Gench’s description of the apartment could also be applied to the person who lives there: He’s a reserved and thoughtful man who, in his free time, creates objects with a 3D printer and crafts small lamps and decorative objects out of wood. “He wasn’t looking to create a simply contemporary and functional interior, but instead a space that would truly interest him and reflect his personality,” she says. And within this 400-square-foot apartment, every inch must have a purpose. The result is a small and very functional interior that is also a journey through different colors and shapes.

Artwork and textiles define the character of the living room; the pillow is by Izzi Decor, the rug is by Dovlet House, and the sofa is by Gench.

Before the renovation, the apartment, which is located in a modern residential development in Moscow, had a small, separate kitchen next to a larger living room. “For someone living alone, this layout didn’t seem logical to me,” explains Gench. “So we combined the kitchen and living room into a single open space, while separating the bedroom with a small wall to create a sense of privacy.”

A custom desk, shelves, and room divider integrate the workspace to the home. Artpole gypsum panels add texture to the surfaces, while items from Woon Stores decorate the shelves.

The bathroom was expanded, and given the owner’s creative interests, a workspace was a must. All of this was done, however, without taking away space from other parts of the home: “Even in the living room, there’s still enough space to exercise,” Gench says. In fact, every choice serves a specific purpose: “Everything the owner needed had to be easily accessible, without making him feel constrained by the apartment’s size.”

Designed by Mila Gench for this apartment, the mustard-colored sofa is the centerpiece of the living room.

Central to Gench’s design, however, are her bold choices when it comes to color. She explains that the color palette was inspired by the client’s preferences: “It was clear that he was drawn to a cool color scheme, but not a monochromatic one. He also wanted warm, saturated accents.” Royal blue details run through the entryway and hallway, berry red dominates the kitchen, and the living room is lighter in tone. “The mustard sofa has become the heart of the living room,” says Gench. “Its color echoes that of the yellow pendant lamp in the kitchen and reappears in small accents throughout the rest of the apartment.”

In the bedroom, the VitSet bed is complemented by textiles from LanoHome and Izzi Decor, along with accessories from Woon Stores on the nightstands. Factura wallpaper, moldings, and a Europlast frame define the space.

The dark, cozy bedroom, on the other hand, creates an atmosphere that is “slightly more mysterious, like something from a fairy tale.” The color scheme helps to tie all the spaces together: “It was important to me that the different colors appear not as isolated patches, but that they flow throughout the apartment.” This continuity is also functional, as the color palette defines the different areas of the living space without having to rely on walls.

In the living room, visual touches like a vase from Woon Stores, DzenDiz curtains designed by Mila Gench, and handmade ceramics from the Plakart Ceramics website accent the space.

“I think of this apartment as a little jewelry box,” says Gench. A checkered arch frames the kitchen, while frescoes, relief plaster panels, and brass details catch the eye. It can feel like being immersed in a Pop art piece. “I wanted the apartment to reveal itself little by little, with new details to discover in every corner: a metal object, a relief, a pattern, or an unusual surface.”

The custom-built kitchen combines a layout planned down to fractions of inches and with a bold color scheme. The backsplash is covered with Equipe tiles; the decorative items are from Woon Stores.

One choice the homeowner was particularly keen on was the perforated metal doors on the upper cabinets in the kitchen and bathroom. “At one point, I’d considered not using them. They were complicated to install and drove up the cost,” says Gench. “But the client insisted.” Their graphic precision contrasts with the painted surfaces and soft shapes in the apartment, reflecting two different sides of the home’s owner.

The cabinets reflect Gench’s broader approach to the project: “I chose to highlight them and make them part of the apartment’s decorative language, almost like architectural elements.” She points out, “Art isn’t a final touch added at the end of the project, it should be part of the concept from the very beginning.”

The design of the desk, shelves, and room divider were calculated to create a workspace that fits the size of the apartment.

“The workspace had to be as professional as possible, with a large desk to accommodate the latest technology and multiple monitors,” says Gench. The area’s height and storage solutions were carefully planned, along with the proportions of the furniture. “At the same time, I was concerned with not turning the living room into an office.”

The desk echoes the room’s colors and materials, blending seamlessly into the space. And once work is done, it takes on a new function: “A screen on the desk can be seen from the sofa, so the same space can easily transition from working to relaxing and watching a movie.”

A view of the custom wardrobe in the bedroom.

“The more compact the space, the less it tolerates rough approximations: Sometimes a single centimeter is enough to determine whether a corner will be comfortable or whether a cabinet door can open fully without hitting the wall,” the designer explains. Custom designs took into account the precise width of the sofa, the amount of room necessary to move around the bed, the size of storage units, and the dimensions of the kitchen—which is equipped with a refrigerator, dishwasher, oven, microwave, and wine cooler. Most of the furnishings come from contemporary Russian manufacturers.

In the dining area, a painting by Yaroslav Shein, from the Sample Gallery, hangs above a Delo table and custom chairs. The pendant light is by Elena Mikheeva, with furnishings from Woon Stores and moldings and baseboards by Europlast.

This attention to detail gives the owner the freedom to relax and live in his home without ever feeling limited. “I wanted all these constraints to go unnoticed in everyday life,” Gench concludes. She sums up the essence of the project and her approach: “Don’t pretend that 400 square feet is more than it is, but instead find a way to make it truly sufficient for daily life.”

The entryway features wall sconces by Freya.

#4786. Deer mating season can be deadly for drivers. Solar powered roadsigns may help.

Published Oct 22, 2026 11:19 AM EDT

Fall is here, and with it comes very determined deer. Mating season for the hoofed ruminants typically begins once temperatures dive and the days grow shorter, but the annual event also poses problems for humans. The deer have one thing on their mind, which means they pay even less attention to vehicles and roadways. In New Jersey, roughly 41 percent of all annual animal collisions occur between October and December, while the AAA Foundation for Traffic Safety routinely tallies over 275 fatalities from similar accidents nationwide every year.

Driving gets even riskier in rural areas, which frequently host higher deer populations and much darker roads. However, a recent pilot program in Virginia offers a promising tool that may keep commuters safe while also allowing the animals to do their thing. At the Virginia Tech Transportation Institute (VTTI), researchers designed an automated digital roadside sensor system that warns drivers of large jaywalkers like deer and elk.

According to VTTI engineer Shreyas Bhat, the year-long trial demonstrated a “measurable change” in how local drivers responded when alerted to oncoming wildlife. Bhat and his colleagues tested the system with help from the state’s Department of Transportation along a 14-mile section of Route 460 in southwest Virginia. The array was composed of eight portable, solar-powered trailers and customizable digital message signs. Each station included thermal cameras analyzed by onboard machine learning software, which triggered the sign alerts whenever it flagged nearby medium– to large–sized animals.

Over the course of the trial, researchers amassed driving data on over 10,000 vehicles, while the detection system maintained an estimated accuracy of around 97 percent. This is particularly impressive given the region’s environmental and infrastructural challenges.

“This system is unique because of the requirements of the area—it needed to be solar powered, portable, and be able to withstand all weather conditions,” explained VTTI engineer Jean Paul Talledo Vilela.

The team reported an average reduced speed of about three miles per hour thanks to the roadside warning signs. That may not seem like much, but even a slight slowdown can save lives. It also proved people took note of the system.

“Speed is an important data point to understand if drivers were actually paying attention to the message board,” said Talledo Vilela. “Even if they are not braking aggressively, some reduction in speed indicates that they may be paying more attention to their surroundings because they got that alert.”

While Talledo Vilela, Bhat, and their collaborators are still parsing through data from the program,(it ended in September) the initial findings remain promising. From here, the team hopes to refine the overall system while organizing future trials in other regions of the country. If successful, the relatively simple installations could also be adapted for other year-round road safety situations like construction or real-time weather hazards.

#4785. Arc Raiders patch nerfs the Deadline mine, ends the menace of the Venator

The first post-Stella Montis patch for Arc Raiders has arrived, and it predictably touches much of what people brought to the latest map.

Arc Raiders has received a new update today that’s almost entirely dedicated to balance and economy tweaks. Patch 1.3.0 arrived earlier across all platforms, coming in at just under 1GB. This is an especially interesting patch because many of the changes it makes can be seen as a response to player behaviour on Stella Montis, the game’s latest map, which just joined the roster last week.

Stella Montis is available for players who’ve put in a decent number of rounds, and its close-quarters, enclosed environments have essentially turned it into the new “PvP map”, where constant action can be found.

That, of course, also means that the map has exposed a few balance issues with some of the game’s weapons and tools. Case in point: the Venator, a pistol that saw a lot of use recently. The weapon is especially powerful when fully-upgraded, something Arc Raiders developer Embark said pushed it beyond what had been originally intended.

In today’s update, the Venator's fire rate is the thing getting hit the hardest, with each upgrade now offering slower gains to the weapon's rate-of-fire compared to the way things were. The weapon’s weight is also being increased to five from two.

Another powerful item players have been getting a lot of mileage out of recently has been the Deadline mine. Unlike the Venator, the Deadline is mainly a threat to Arc enemies, and it’s proven incredibly powerful against the largest and most fearsome, such as the Bastion, and Leaper. In fact, the mine was considered essential when taking down a Queen.

The patch vastly increases its purchase price, and makes it more difficult to craft by upping the amount each of its components requires. Perhaps the most effective nerf, however, is the reduction to the Deadline’s stock at traders from three to just one, which means you’re going to be seeing a lot less of it topside.

Beyond those specific changes, Embark has also tweaked explosive damage across the board. According to the developer, explosive damage was being applied to multiple parts at once, which trivialised some encounters.

This change mainly affects larger Arc units, such as the Bastion, allowing them to withstand more explosive damage before bits start coming off of them. The Hullcracker, one of the best explosive damage weapons, has also caught a nerf as part of that.

Check the official blog for the full change log for patch 1.3.0. If you’re just getting started with the hottest extraction shooter ever, our Arc Raiders tips offer plenty of help. You should also learn what all the different Map Conditions do in Arc Raiders, and how to best make use of each of them.

#4784. Terry Bradshaw Will Never Tell You He’s Retiring

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The 78-year-old NFL legend opens up about depression, cancer, and why he has no interest in a farewell tribute.

TERRY BRADSHAW IS feeling achy today. But it’s not because of his 14-year NFL career, his 40-plus-year career as a broadcaster, or the daily work of manning his Texas ranch. No, what’s really been wearing him out is all the autographs he’s been signing.

“I signed 4,300 stickers in two weeks. I've got 2,500 more sent to me, and I've signed 750 since last night and this morning,” Bradshaw tells Men’s Health. “My finger on my index hand is turning to the left.”

Unfortunately for Bradshaw, people want your signature when you’re a Hall of Famer, stage performer, Fox Sports analyst, and—once again—author. Looking for Terry, Bradshaw’s second memoir, arrived earlier this month, 25 years after his last book. And the 78-year-old has experienced plenty in that span.

He got married (for a fourth time) to his longtime partner Tammy in 2014, survived two cancer diagnoses and even launched his own bourbon. Bradshaw’s book also gets into his struggles with depression and his “dumb” public image—which has followed him since his playing days.

“If you think you know me, you really don’t,” he says. But as the curtain begins to close on his broadcasting career, Bradshaw isn’t interested in taking a victory lap.

The Steelers legend told us that he recently signed a two-year extension with Fox, but expects to be replaced after the network shows the Super Bowl in 2029. He’s hoping that there’s no fancy send-off—not that he expects that wish to be honored.

“I don’t go for tributes,” he says. “I don’t need that in my life.”

For now, Bradshaw remains in the public eye, and he was happy to talk through his new book after a much-needed dose of rainfall to his farm.

MEN’S HEALTH: This is your first memoir since 2001. Why was now the time to put out another one?

TERRY BRADSHAW: I enjoy doing books, unlike probably a lot of people. The first couple of books were fine. The last three I've really enjoyed doing [with coauthor David Fisher].

You kind of park the vanity a little bit, and you say to yourself, "You know what? I’ve got a few things that have happened to me in the last 20-plus years. I'd like to share it with people.” And I'm thinking those that buy the book will learn from the book. Those dealing with depression, those who have had failed marriages… As my wife says, I'm an open book.

MH: A main theme of the book is opening up, and how there’s been this perception of you that isn’t accurate. What’s the biggest way people misunderstand you?

TB: That's my fault. Look, first of all, I know what this question is [laughs]. It's my fault, because I play into the dumb image. You want to have a shocker here? I enjoy it! It's fun for me.

I choose to do hardline football analysis, but with a smile on my face, and I try to entertain. And maybe sometimes I try too hard to entertain. Sometimes you can go too far, you can definitely be too much, and people get tired of that. So I measure my steps, I kind of let things come to me.

MH: You wrote about endearing yourself to people with humor, and how you want them to have good interactions with you. How much of a personal responsibility do you feel to make other people happy?

TB: It's of paramount importance. I hate to use the word, Jake, celebrities. I enjoy people. I enjoy making them smile. I enjoy making their day, because a lot of times, giving an autograph and taking a picture in an airport makes their day.

To tell you the truth, too many celebrities value themselves a little bit too highly. When we're all stripped of our titles and the movies and the songs we've written, the Super Bowls we won—we’re all just people.

MH: You’ve also talked at length about your experience with depression. How important was it for you to be known as someone who spoke about mental health?

TB: The depression [diagnosis] explained all the dark, dark, dark times, even now still, that I go through and experience. And you go through all the times, what started it, what kicked it in. Divorce will kick it in, injury will kick it in, things that are important to you'll kick it. Plus, let's be honest, I take medication, and even with the medication, I still will [sometimes] start sinking.

You have no idea how big a difference Tammy has made. Because if I didn't have her, I'd be by myself. I'd drink too much, I'd get drunk, or I’d get sad, and I'd buy a horse, or I'd purchase something. I was always looking for some fix, and now that's not the case. But to answer your question, I'm not afraid to share my illness or my weaknesses—as some people would want to say it is, but it isn’t a weakness, it's an illness—with other people.

I know I’ve made a difference in men's lives because I have them come up to me. They come up quietly. It's mainly in a grocery store, and I'll be going down the aisle shopping, and they'll slip over to me [quietly], "Hey man, I know about your depression. It got me to a doctor and my wife, and I really want to thank you for sharing all that.”

MH: How difficult was it to accept help for it?

TB: Well, like I told you earlier, events in your life will trigger it. I had gone through a divorce, and I had two little girls. That sent me in a hole because I felt like I lost my kids.

I was in a hole for almost two years. The emotional drag that it had on my life got to a point where it was unbearable, and that's when I decided, I said I gotta figure out what's going on here.

MH: Regarding your cancer diagnoses (which came in 2021 and 2022), you wrote that you weren’t actually sure if surviving skin and bladder cancer made you appreciate life more. Has your stance changed on that?

TB: Getting a report you had cancer was like: “Alright, there's a challenge.” But it didn't scare me. Of course, I thought about it. I didn't understand how serious bladder cancer was. My approach to it was always this way: as a Christian, if I die, I'm going to heaven. So, I'm good. I don't want to die, and I don't know how good I'm going to be at dying.

Let me tell you this: the hardest thing about cancer for me is going back for the checkups and waiting for the call that says you're okay. Now you’ve got another six months before you do it again. But you're okay. And everybody I talk to that are cancer-free, they all say the same thing. The scariest thing now is, “Oh man, going back, what's the report gonna be?”

MH: There’s a feeling you describe in the book of having to constantly prove yourself on the field. Do you feel more confident now as a broadcaster than you did at the end of your playing career?

TB: Oh, I am totally confident on the air, absolutely. Supremely confident. I think I'm going through now what everybody that's in their 70s goes through. I'm 78. I just signed a two-year extension, and I'm pretty sure no matter how well I perform over the next two years, they're going to want to replace me.

I’m not going to announce my retirement, because in two years when we do the Super Bowl, after my contract is expiring, I don't want anybody to do a tribute. So I'm never going to say I'm going to retire. Because, look, they may say, "Hey, you're still doing a good job. We'll sign you for another year.” That could easily happen. They're pissing into the wind if they think I’m gonna tell them I’m retiring.

TB: What if I say, “I don't know why you're doing that for, I'm not retiring.” But I mean, I'm not going anywhere either. What am I going to do, at 80? Go to ESPN? [laughs].

MH: When you reflect on your post-playing career, is there anything you regret having not done?

TB: I’m afraid of heights. I used to be afraid of flying. I’m not a daredevil. I will not paraglide, parasail, whatever you call it. I'm not jumping out of a plane. I'm not snorkeling. I'm not going underwater with a tank of air. Sharks are down there, they will bite you. I don't go in the water [laughs]. I live a really… it's not an uneventful life, certainly, nowhere near. But everything in the entertainment world, I have done.

MH: What does a successful last couple of years at Fox look like to you? Just continuing to make people smile?

TB: Absolutely. Keep entertaining, give strong opinions when I feel it.

I have another book in me, and I'll say more in the next book. I'm already planning it. It won't be a tell-all book. But it's a strong opinion book.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.
