#4783. Majority of Americans want candidates who support US-based financial technology: Poll

The September survey offered a possible window into US voters who could consider fintech and digital asset policies on Election Day.

A new poll from the DeFi Education Fund and Ipsos found that 72% of people surveyed said it was “somewhat” to “extremely” important for politicians to be supportive of policies encouraging financial technology built in the US.

On Tuesday, Ipsos released the results of a poll of 1,545 people conducted in September, showing that 35% of respondents said it was “very” or “extremely” important for political candidates to support the idea of “ensuring that those who are building the next generation of financial technology are encouraged to do so in the United States.” An additional 37% of those surveyed said that the fintech policy was “somewhat important.”

Notably, only 9% of the Ipsos poll respondents said that they were familiar with crypto, and 5% with stablecoins. A majority, 87%, said that they had not held any digital assets in the previous 12 months. Only 13% said it was “very” or “extremely” important for a political candidate to encourage growth in blockchain, crypto, and DeFi.

“Ownership of crypto and openness to DeFi are largely consistent with 2025, and interest looks consistent across party lines,” said Ipsos.

The poll was conducted about two months before Americans were set to vote on the next session of Congress in the 2026 midterm elections, in which crypto policies and laws could be a factor at the polling stations. Political action committees backed by crypto companies have already spent tens of millions of dollars in an attempt to influence voters in key races.

#4782. Robinhood Misses Q3 Earnings as Crypto Revenue Rises and Prediction Market Bets Grow

Robinhood Misses Q3 Earnings as Crypto Revenue Rises and Prediction Market Bets Grow

Robinhood, the trading app where more than 28 million people keep money to buy stocks and crypto and now bet on sports and elections, reported third-quarter revenue of $1.42 billion billion on Tuesday, missing the roughly $1.43 billion Wall Street expected

Earnings came in at $0.51 per share, missing the $0.55 analysts projected and down from $0.61 a year ago. Earnings per share is profit divided by the number of shares outstanding, and it is the figure investors use to call a quarter a beat or a miss.

Robinhood is the app that brought commission-free trading to a generation of first-time investors, which makes its quarterly report one of the clearest readings of what ordinary people are doing with their money. When customers trade more, deposit more or pile into new products, the shift shows up in these numbers.

Net income was $476 million, down 14% from $556 million a year earlier and 17% from $573 million in the second quarter. The quarter included a $67 million unrealized loss, meaning a drop in value on paper, on Robinhood Ventures Fund I, which cost $0.07 per share. Adjusted earnings per share, which exclude certain items, came to $0.60, down 3% from a year ago.

Event contracts, the yes-or-no bets that pay out $1 if a game, an election or an economic report goes a certain way, brought in $173 million, up from $156 million in the second quarter, when they overtook crypto revenue for the first time. Customers traded a record 16.4 billion of them, more than seven times a year earlier.

“I believe we’re at the very beginning of a prediction market supercycle,” CEO Vlad Tenev said in December last year, when Robinhood added NFL player contracts and parlay-style combos that pay only if every outcome hits.

Crypto trading revenue rose to $120 million from $100 million in the second quarter, but remained down 55% from the $268 million Robinhood earned in the third quarter of 2025. Customers traded $50 billion in crypto during the quarter, down 38% from a year earlier, with $21 billion on the Robinhood app and $29 billion through Bitstamp, the crypto exchange it acquired

Robinhood Chain went live on July 1 as a layer-2 network, meaning a blockchain that runs on top of Ethereum to make transactions cheaper and faster. Its first weeks were driven by meme coins, joke-themed tokens, rather than the tokenized stocks it was built for, which are digital versions of company shares that trade on a blockchain.

By the end of August the chain had earned about $3.13 million in fees over 30 days, more than Ethereum and Base, which came in at $2.47 million and $2.89 million.

Hours before the report, Robinhood announced a deal with Ripple that brings RLUSD, a stablecoin (a crypto token designed to stay worth one dollar), to Robinhood Chain and Bitstamp, with an in-app rollout planned later this year. RLUSD has a market value above $2.5 billion, and Ripple will also supply Robinhood’s treasury software and lending for Bitstamp traders.

Robinhood shares closed Tuesday at about $94.61 and slipped in after-hours trading- The stock is down roughly 18% this year and about 37% below its 52-week high of $150.47.

Robinhood narrowed its 2026 outlook for adjusted operating expenses and stock-based pay to $2.675 billion to $2.75 billion, trimming the top of the $2.675 billion to $2.775 billion range it set in July.

#4781. Can the BPC-157 ‘Wolverine’ Peptide Really Help With Pain and Recovery?

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Wellness seekers are increasingly turning to BPC-157, the wildly popular “Wolverine” peptide for injury recovery and pain relief, despite scant human evidence.

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) advisory panel voted in July to ease restrictions on it and several other peptides, recommending the injectable substances be made eligible for pharmacy compounding.

But before restrictions were eased, preliminary research suggests a rise in the use of the Wolverine peptide in recent years.

A September 2026 preprint analyzed more than 15 million medical records and identified 1,039 people with documented BPC-157 use.

The researchers found a roughly 33-fold increase in new users between 2020 and 2026.

The results, which were based on clinical records, have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

“The study found that BPC-157 use is largely hidden in clinical narrative rather than conventional prescription data,” said lead study author Venky Soundararajan, PhD, co-founder and Chief Scientific Officer of nference.

“The proportion of newly documented BPC-157 users who were male rose from 57% in 2020 to 70% in 2026, a statistically significant trend,” he told Healthline.

“The user population for BPC-157 peptide is increasingly male in proportion and increasingly younger with each passing year.”

BPC-157 is a synthetic 15-amino-acid peptide marketed and sold online for wellness.

Health claims about it and other peptides have spread on social media and in fitness circles and wellness communities, with high-profile advocates like Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. touting their benefits.

Proponents of BPC-157 claim the injectable peptide promotes injury recovery, pain relief, and tissue healing. Some advocates claim that “stacking” the Wolverine peptide, particularly with TB-500, may enhance its effects.

The “Wolverine” peptide got its nickname from claims that it facilitates rapid healing, echoing the regenerative abilities of the fictional X-Men character of the same name.

This promise of pain relief and recovery may be what gives the Wolverine peptide its appeal.

Soundararajan’s study found that nearly 33% of patients reported using BPC-157 for pain relief. By comparison, just 4% of patients reported using BPC-157 for performance enhancement.

Across the cohort of 1,039 patients, 27% reported improvements.

“Pain relief was the dominant reported benefit, documented in 183 patients,” Soundarajan said. “Wound or tissue healing was reported in 38 patients and functional return in 34 patients.”

“Part of BPC-157’s appeal appears to be to patients who remain symptomatic after chronic, complex or previously treated musculoskeletal problems, signaling a clear clinical unmet need,” he said.

Bert Mandelbaum, MD, a sports medicine specialist, orthopedic surgeon, and co-director of the Regenerative Orthobiologic Center at Cedars-Sinai Orthopaedics and Sports Medicine in Los Angeles, said that everyone experiences pain, but warned about the risks of using the Wolverine peptide to treat it.

“There is zero indication that BPC-157 has any efficacy for pain,” he told Healthline. “There’s zero evidence that shows that it’s good for clinical healing, and we don’t really understand the safety profile of it.”

Human evidence of the possible benefits and safety of the Wolverine peptide is limited.

A 2025 systematic review of current BPC-157 literature found that it was dominated by preclinical research and low-grade human evidence. The review authors said there was insufficient clinical safety data to determine the peptide’s risks.

A small 2021 retrospective study on the effects of injectable BPC-157 on knee pain suggests the peptide could help repair tissue and regrow cartilage. However, the study followed just 16 participants, lacked a control group, and did not assess impacts on function or quality of life.

An even smaller 2025 intravenous safety pilot on BPC-157 use involved 2 participants. While no adverse effects were reported during the short study, the researchers acknowledged that larger studies are needed to confirm safety.

The July 23–24 FDA advisory vote added BPC-157 to the Section 503A bulk drug substances list, allowing pharmacies to include it in compounded medications.

The FDA’s 8-6 vote, with one abstention, greenlit BPC-157 in both free-base and acetate forms.

The recommendation did not make BPC-157 an FDA-approved drug, nor is it a component of an approved product. The federal health agency has yet to make final regulatory decisions on peptides.

“RFK Jr. policies are hurting Americans,” Mandelbaum said.

“This decision is ridiculous. It’s another step in the wrong direction. There’s no basis for any of this stuff.”

The safety profile of the Wolverine peptide for use in humans is largely unknown.

As such, BPC-157 is banned by the World Anti-Doping Agency as an unapproved substance for athletes both in and out of competition.

The FDA notes that peptides like BPC-157 (free base) may be sensitive to product formulation, processing, environmental conditions, and impurities, which could degrade the products and reduce their effectiveness.

The health agency adds that it currently has limited safety information for how peptides like BPC-157 are administered.

Soundararajan explained that his study was unable to estimate the incidence or overall safety of BPC-157 because adverse events were captured retrospectively from routine clinical notes and are likely underreported.

“That said, the medical records examined contain both positive and negative patient experiences,” he said.

“Mood, sleep, skin, injection-site, and gastrointestinal symptoms are concrete safety outcomes among the few examined patient records with available evidence that deserve prospective measurement and discussions by healthcare providers with their patients who report using BPC-157.”

Some experts warn of short-term risks like infection at the injection site, but the long-term effects are not known.

“We don’t have publications of infections yet, but there are anecdotes of this,” Mandelbaum said.

“People are injecting their joints with this stuff, and you have no idea what it is. It’s not standardized. It’s not being regulated by the FDA, and I think people should recognize that. And although we haven’t proven it’s a safety hazard, we know it has no efficacy. So why do it? Why waste your money?”

Experts urge anyone considering BPC-157 or another peptide treatment to discuss it with their doctor first.

You might ask for evidence supporting its use, information on its quality, and whether there are alternatives with established safety and effectiveness profiles.

Mandelbaum noted there are many scientifically proven and safe alternatives to consider.

“We have spent the last 20 years developing an arsenal of biologics from PRP to bone marrow aspirate to adipose tissue to understanding glucosamine and hyaluronic acid,” he said. “We’ve done an amazing job at studying these things. We’re getting to the point where we are understanding the complexities of biologics. It’s exciting, and it’s impactful.”

Mandelbaum vehemently opposed the use of unapproved peptides like BPC-157.

He likened its rise in popularity to a “Ponzi scheme” promoted by “gym rats” and “internet influencers” who “leverage and exploit the gap between hope and knowledge.” Just because the FDA eased restrictions, he said, doesn’t make them any safer or more legitimate.

That said, any use of unapproved peptides should also be disclosed to a doctor.

“A short, open and empathetic clinical conversation can turn invisible consumer use of products like BPC-157 into useful clinical information that can critically help inform current and future care for such patients that ensure the best possible outcomes,” Soundararajan said.

BPC-157 should not be combined with other experimental peptides until research determines the potential for adverse reactions or events. Long-term use of BPC-157 and other peptides marketed for wellness is not advised.

#4779. The best cameras under $1,000, according to experts

Updated Oct 22, 2026 11:45 AM EDT

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more ›

The best camera under $1,000 for most people is the Canon EOS R50 ($849.99 with an 18-45mm lens at Canon as of October 2026), because it pairs a 24.2-megapixel sensor and uncropped 4K video with Canon’s current lens mount and an assisted auto mode built for backlit and night scenes. If you’re mostly filming yourself, the Insta360 Luna Ultra ($769.99) puts a 1-inch sensor, a 3x telephoto, and a mechanical gimbal in your pocket. Plenty has changed since our last update: price increases, many tied to US tariffs, have pushed favorites like Sony’s a6400 past $1,000 for the body alone, Canon has discontinued the EOS RP in Japan, GoPro’s new flagship is the Mission 1 Pro rather than a Hero 14, and DJI’s newest pocket gimbal hasn’t officially reached the US. Here are your options if you’re looking to get started under a grand.

I have been a professional photographer and camera journalist for nearly two decades. During my time at our sibling publication, Popular Photography, I shot with almost every major camera released in the past decade. For this guide, I looked for cameras with clear strengths that fit specific kinds of shooting, and weighed durability, performance, and less tangible things like access to a deep catalog of lenses and accessories.

For this update, we started from our previous picks and the cameras released since, then checked every finalist against its maker’s spec page and a live US retailer listing on October 20, 2026. That price pass cut more cameras than any spec comparison did: Sony’s a6400 now costs $1,098 for the body at Adorama and the Fujifilm X-E5 is $1,699.95 for the body. We also checked each maker’s product lifecycle and business news, which is why the Canon RP section says “while stock lasts” and the GoPro section mentions the company’s sale process.

The picks below run from about $620 to $1,000. Five are interchangeable-lens cameras; the other three have built-in lenses, for people who want one device that does everything. Camera prices have moved a lot this year in both directions, so check before you buy. While we always make specific picks for specific needs, there’s plenty of overlap, so read the whole list before you set your heart on one.

Canon’s EOS R50 is still the camera we’d hand to someone trading up from a phone. The kit with the RF-S 18-45mm IS STM lens costs $849.99 at Canon as of October 20, 2026 (a sale price against a $899.99 regular price), which leaves room in a $1,000 budget for a memory card and a bag. If that’s still too much, Canon’s refurbished store had this same kit for $529 with a one-year Canon USA warranty in late September (per CPriceWatch), though restocks sell out fast. Y.M.Cinema Magazine reported in late August that it was still the best-selling mirrorless camera on Amazon, more than three years after launch.

The video spec has aged better than the price tag suggests. Canon rates the R50 for 4K at 30 fps using the full width of its 24.2-megapixel APS-C sensor, oversampled from 6K, so you don’t lose your wide angle when you switch from stills to video. Subject-detection autofocus locks onto people and animals and follows them across the frame without any fiddling, and there’s a mic jack for when the built-in microphone stops cutting it.

For beginners, the Advanced A+ Assist mode is the feature that matters. It works like a full auto mode that reads the scene, so backlit portraits and night shots come out better than they would on plain auto, and you can graduate to aperture priority when you’re ready. Canon’s spec page lists no in-body stabilization, so stick with stabilized lenses for low-light handheld shooting, or budget for Canon’s inexpensive RF 50mm f/1.8 STM, which gathers far more light than the kit zoom. If you mostly shoot video, the R50 V below is the same camera reworked for creators, without the viewfinder.

A phone on a gimbal gets you steady video. A pocket gimbal camera gets you steady video from a much bigger sensor, with the screen facing you and the stabilizer built in. The Insta360 Luna Ultra is the one we’d buy in that category, at $769.99 for the standard bundle at Insta360’s store. Its main camera puts a 1-inch sensor behind a Leica Summicron lens, and a second camera with a 1/1.3-inch sensor handles 3x telephoto. Insta360 rates the pair for 6x lossless zoom and 12x total.

That second lens is why we’d pick it over a single-lens pocket camera. You can frame yourself at the wide end, then punch in on a street performer across the plaza without walking over. Insta360 rates it for 8K at 30 fps, 4K at up to 120 fps, Dolby Vision HDR, and 10-bit I-Log, and the gimbal is a mechanical 3-axis unit, so most of the steadying happens physically instead of by cropping the frame. I have been using this for the past few months to shoot everything from personal videos to footage for work and I’ve been impressed with everything from battery life to image quality.

The 2-inch OLED screen pops off and works as a wireless monitor and remote from up to 20 meters, per Insta360, which is handy when the camera is on a tripod and you’re in the shot. It’s not the quickest to zoom or focus, but you’re trading a bit of performance for convenience. The bigger story for US buyers is the competition: DJI’s Osmo Pocket 4 launched in April with its FCC authorization still pending, per DroneXL, and Amateur Photographer reported in June that DJI’s products remained off the US market. The $969.99 Creator Bundle adds a wireless mic transmitter, a battery handle, and a wide-angle lens; start with the standard bundle unless you already know you need the mic.

The Canon EOS R50 V takes the R50’s 24.2-megapixel sensor and rebuilds the body around video. The viewfinder and flash are gone. In their place you get a headphone jack next to the mic jack, a red tally lamp on the front so you know you’re recording, and a second tripod thread on the side for shooting vertical video without an L-bracket.

It’s the camera for the creator who wants interchangeable lenses on a desk or a tripod rather than a gimbal in a pocket. DPReview lists full-width 4K at 30 fps oversampled from 6K, the same clean 4K as the R50, and 4K at 60 fps with a 1.56x crop. Get the 14-30mm kit if you film yourself: on APS-C, that’s roughly a 22-48mm equivalent by our arithmetic, wide enough to hold at arm’s length.

There’s no in-body stabilization, so walking shots depend on a stabilized lens, a gimbal, or Canon’s electronic stabilization, which crops further. Battery life is the other limit. Plan for about 70 minutes of 4K/30 per charge, so grab a spare battery or USB power for long sessions. If you’d rather have a viewfinder for stills, the regular R50 kit above costs $50 more than the R50 V kit and does both jobs.

The T30 III runs the same X-Processor 5 as the X-E5. It trades the X-E5’s 40-megapixel sensor for a 26.1-megapixel X-Trans CMOS 4 chip, which is plenty for prints and cropping, and it still has a built-in viewfinder, which Fujifilm’s cheaper X-M5 doesn’t.

The new top-plate dial is the reason to want this camera. It switches between 20 film simulations, and three of its positions store custom recipes, so you can build a Classic Chrome look with your own grain and white balance settings and call it back with a thumb. Fujifilm’s straight-out-of-camera color is why many people choose the system in the first place, and the dial moves it from the menus to the top of the camera. Video is better than an enthusiast stills camera needs: DPReview lists 6.2K at 30 fps in open gate and 4K at 30 fps from the full sensor width, with 10-bit 4:2:2 internal recording.

It’s still an entry-level body. There’s no in-body stabilization, so handheld low-light shots depend on a stabilized lens or a steady grip, and the battery offers roughly 315 shots per charge under good conditions. Stock is the other catch. On October 20, Glazer’s Camera showed the body sold out in all three colors and Hot Rod Cameras listed it as a preorder. The kit with the XC 13-33mm lens, at $1,149.95, breaks this guide’s budget, so pair the body with one of Fujifilm’s compact primes instead.

The Nikon FM2 is one of the great film SLRs, and the Z fc copies its top plate closely enough to fool people at a distance. Shutter speed, ISO, and exposure compensation each get their own engraved aluminum dial, so you can read every exposure setting with the camera switched off. I love setting exposure with real dials, and so do many of the working photographers I know. The price has come down too: Adorama had the body at $756.95 on October 20, 2026, against a $1,056.95 list price, and Nikon’s own store listed it starting at $759.95.

Inside, it’s a 20.9-megapixel DX sensor. Nikon rates it for 4K at 30 fps with full-pixel readout and no crop, plus bursts up to 11 fps, and the 3-inch touchscreen flips fully around for selfies and flips in to hide, which keeps the retro look intact. The contoured front makes it easier to hold than its flat sides suggest, and the 2.36-million-dot OLED viewfinder is big and bright for a camera this size.

It’s from 2021, and you can tell in places. There’s no in-body stabilization, and the Z50 II that Nikon launched at $909.95 in late 2024 has a newer processor and better subject detection inside a plainer body. The Z fc is the one to buy if you’ll enjoy using it more, and that’s a real consideration. The short Z-mount flange also makes it a good host for adapted vintage lenses, and Nikon’s FTZ II adapter covers decades of F-mount glass.

Canon couldn’t keep up with orders for the PowerShot V1 after it launched in 2025. It’s easier to find now, and cheaper: Canon’s store had it for $849 on October 20, 2026, down from a $1,029 regular price. It replaces the Sony ZV-1 II we used to recommend here, because the Insta360 above covers that camera’s vlogging job better and the V1 is the stronger camera for everything else.

Its sensor is the reason. Canon calls it a 1.4-inch type, and at 22.3 megapixels it has roughly the area of a Micro Four Thirds sensor, per PetaPixel, which by our arithmetic is about twice the area of the 1-inch sensors in most premium compacts. More area means cleaner low-light shots and easier background blur from a camera that fits in a jacket pocket. The zoom covers 16-50mm equivalent at f/2.8-4.5 (17-52mm in video mode), wide enough to hold at arm’s length and long enough for a head-and-shoulders portrait.

The V1 is also a better video camera than most compacts because it has a fan. PetaPixel’s launch report says Canon rates it for more than two hours of 4K/60 in typical conditions with the right settings, and the fan settings lift the time limits on 4K/30 and Full HD. 4K/60 crops the frame by 1.4x, though, so use 4K/30 when you need the full width, and expect the lens to get slow at the telephoto end indoors. Canon lists optical image stabilization in the lens plus digital Movie IS, and Galaxus’s hands-on review notes a built-in ND filter, which helps keep motion natural in bright daylight. The same review called the lack of a viewfinder the camera’s biggest weakness, and the screen is dim in direct sun, so this is a camera you’ll frame at arm’s length.

Canon listed the EOS RP as discontinued in Japan in June. In the US, Canon’s store still sells it, at $899 for the body as of October 20, 2026, which is $250 off a $1,149 regular price through November 1, per Canon. It was $799 at Canon and B&H in early October, per CPriceWatch, so a holiday sale could bring that back. Canon U.S.A. told PetaPixel in June that the RP remained on sale in the United States, with no end date announced, so treat this as a while-supplies-last pick.

I’ve shot with the RP as a backup body, and it’s a capable camera for what it is: a small, light way into full-frame for people who mostly shoot stills. The reason to buy it is the sensor. A full-frame sensor is the size of a frame of 35mm film, so lenses behave the way their focal lengths suggest: a 50mm lens looks like a 50mm lens, wide angles stay wide, and it’s easier to get a sharp subject against a soft background. The RP’s 26.2-megapixel sensor also handles dim rooms better than the APS-C cameras above, and anything you buy for it works on Canon’s newest full-frame bodies when you upgrade.

It’s a stills camera first. Canon’s spec page lists bursts of about 5 fps and 4K at 23.98 fps, and Digital Camera World’s review measured about a 1.76x crop in 4K, with Canon’s Dual Pixel autofocus unavailable in that mode, so the R50 is the better Canon for video. The kit with the RF 24-105mm f/4-7.1 lens runs $1,199 at Canon, past our budget; buy the body and add the RF 50mm f/1.8 STM for portraits.

GoPro hasn’t released a Hero 14 Black. GoPro’s 2026 flagship is the Mission 1 Pro, a camera the company pitches at filmmakers, but it keeps everything that makes a GoPro useful for action: it’s waterproof to 66 feet without a housing, it has mounting fingers built into the body, and it has screens front and back. The difference is the sensor. It uses a 1-inch, 50-megapixel chip, much larger than the 1/1.9-inch sensor in the Hero 13 Black, which should mean cleaner footage at dusk and indoors.

GoPro rates it for 8K at 60 fps, 4K at 240 fps for slow motion, and 1080p at 960 fps in 10-second bursts, with 10-bit GP-Log2 and HLG HDR. Its native field of view is 159 degrees, per GoPro, and four microphones record 32-bit float audio, which makes a sudden shout much harder to clip.

If you just want a camera to stick on a helmet and forget, GoPro still sells the Hero 13 Black for less. If you want a waterproof camera that shoots photos first, with an optical zoom, look at OM System’s Tough TG-7 instead. Both are rugged and totally waterproof without a case, which also makes either one a good underwater camera.

Several cameras that show up in nearly every budget camera roundup didn’t make this list because their current prices are over $1,000. If one goes on sale below that line during the holidays, it’s worth a look.

The right camera depends less on the spec sheet than on what you’ll actually shoot and how you’ll carry it. Here’s what to look at before you buy.

Most cameras here land between 20 and 26 megapixels, which is plenty for prints and cropping. Sensor size makes a bigger difference. A full-frame sensor matches a 35mm film frame. APS-C sensors, like the ones in the Canon R50, Fujifilm X-T30 III, and Nikon Z fc, have roughly 40 percent of that area, and the 1-inch and 1.4-inch-type sensors in the compacts and gimbal cameras are smaller still, though larger than most phones’ main sensors. Bigger sensors generally mean cleaner low-light shots and easier background blur. APS-C makes telephoto lenses smaller and cheaper, which is why it’s a good fit for most beginners.

Compact cameras, gimbal cameras, and action cameras have built-in lenses, which keeps everything in one tidy package. Interchangeable-lens cameras let you add a fast prime for portraits or a long zoom for sports later, but you’re buying into a lens mount. Canon RF, Nikon Z, and Fujifilm X all have current lens lines from the camera makers plus third-party options, so any of the mirrorless picks here will have new glass to grow into.

A pocket gimbal camera like the Insta360 Luna Ultra mounts its lens on a motorized 3-axis stabilizer, so walking shots stay smooth without the cropping that electronic stabilization uses. The trade is that the lens is fixed and the camera is built around video. If you mostly film yourself, buy one. If you mostly shoot photos, a mirrorless camera will serve you better.

4K is standard across this list, but how a camera makes it varies. Some read the full width of the sensor; others crop in, which narrows your field of view and makes wide shots harder. Frame rate matters too: 30 fps is standard, 24 fps looks more like film, and 60 fps or higher lets you slow footage down. Check the recording format and bit depth as well. Log profiles and 10-bit recording give you more room to edit color, but they also mean more time grading.

When you’re starting out, manual mode can seem intimidating. That won’t last, and you want a camera you can grow into, which is why dial-heavy bodies like the Nikon Z fc and Fujifilm X-T30 III are worth a look even for beginners. Most cameras under $1,000 also aren’t built for harsh conditions. If you plan to get your camera wet or dirty, buy one made for it, like the GoPro, rather than worrying about a mirrorless body every time it rains.

The Canon EOS R50. It costs $849.99 with an 18-45mm lens at Canon as of October 2026, shoots uncropped 4K video, and has an Advanced A+ Assist mode that handles backlit and night scenes while you learn.

Yes. Canon hasn’t announced a successor as of October 2026, and the R50 still offers a 24.2-megapixel APS-C sensor, uncropped 4K at 30 fps, and Canon’s current RF lens mount. Y.M.Cinema Magazine reported in August 2026 that it was still the best-selling mirrorless camera on Amazon. If you mostly shoot video, consider the R50 V instead.

Around big retail events. Canon’s prices dropped during the early-October sales, then rose on October 12: the R50 kit went from $799 to $849 and the EOS RP body from $799 to $899, per CPriceWatch. The holiday shopping season around Black Friday is the next likely window.

For a new camera, mirrorless. Camera makers have moved their new bodies and lenses to mirrorless systems, and you get features like subject-detection autofocus that DSLRs largely missed. A used DSLR can still be a bargain if you already own lenses for it, but new glass for those mounts has mostly stopped.

Yes, and mirrorless cameras are especially good at it. Their short lens mounts leave room for inexpensive adapters, so older manual-focus lenses can work on bodies like the Nikon Z fc or Fujifilm X-T30 III. You’ll usually focus by hand, which is easier with the focus magnification and peaking tools most mirrorless cameras include.

Not through official channels as of our research. DJI launched the Osmo Pocket 4 in April 2026 with its FCC authorization still pending, per DroneXL, and Amateur Photographer reported in June that DJI’s products remained off the US market. The Insta360 Luna Ultra, our vlogging pick, is sold at Insta360’s US store and major retailers.

Not new. Fujifilm’s US store listed the X-E5 body at $1,699.95 in October 2026. The Fujifilm X-T30 III gets you the same X-Processor 5, a film simulation dial, and a viewfinder for $999.95, body only, with a 26.1-megapixel sensor instead of the X-E5’s 40 megapixels.

Pro cameras still cost thousands of dollars, but $1,000 buys a lot of camera, as long as you pick for what you shoot. Photographers should start with the Canon R50 or Fujifilm X-T30 III, and video-first creators on a budget should look at the R50 V. If you mostly film yourself, get the Luna Ultra. Whatever you buy, learn it well. A great camera won’t do much without the person holding it.

#4778. Frank Lloyd Wright’s Taliesin West Reopens After a Freak Accident Caused a Visitor's Death

Taliesin West, Frank Lloyd Wright’s winter home and studio, is reopening following a death at the site on Friday, October 16.

The victim, identified as Brother Rogério Renato Mateucci, the rector of Pontifical Catholic University of Paraná in Brazil, was touring the UNESCO World Heritage site in Scottsdale, Arizona, when a piece of debris from the building’s roof fell and hit his head, the New York Times reported. The accident occurred around 2 p.m. following strong winds—local weather service reported gusts up to 32 miles per hour—which dislodged the a piece of the structure. According to the Scottsdale fire department, he died instantly. No other injuries occurred.

“The Foundation mourns the loss of Rogério Renato Mateucci, who tragically died on October 16 in an accident at Taliesin West,” Joseph Specter, president and CEO of the Frank Lloyd Wright Foundation, which owns and operates the building, shared with AD in a statement.“Taliesin West has been closed to the public to allow the Foundation to conduct a thorough safety inspection of the site, including its roofing and structures, and to cooperate fully with local authorities, who have determined the incident was weather-related.” The Scottsdale Police Department confirmed to AD that the tragedy was weather-related and no foul play was suspected; however, their investigation is not complete.

Wright designed the residence in 1937 as his winter home and studio, imagining it as a desert camp that emerged from the arid grounds. Originally, the building was topped with a canvas roof, which offered protection from the hot sun while still allowing light to pass through the covering. “These were lightweight, translucent, and could be opened and closed to modulate airflow and temperature,” Rebecca Barron, director of preservation at Taliesin West, shared in a previous blog post from the Foundation.

However, the material wasn’t waterproof, and over time the building transitioned to year-round use (currently, it serves as the headquarters for Wright’s foundation in addition to other public programming), prompting the Foundation to add fixed acrylic panels in the 1990s in certain areas. According to the blog post, the Foundation is in the process of replacing the acrylic roof with a more resilient material that more closely resembles Wright’s canvas. Police confirmed to the Arizona Republic that a piece of acrylic glass hit Mateucci, but the Foundation did not comment on the condition of the roof when the accident occurred.

The Foundation says it will now monitor the weather more closely as part of the reopening plans. “Weather station technology has been added to enhance our existing safety procedures at the site, to assess temperature, wind speed, air pressure, and rain. This will offer the Foundation localized safety data to further inform the daily practice of opening our site to the public,” Specter said. “With these actions and safety assurances in place, we feel confident that Taliesin West is safe for visitors, and we will reopen the site to the public on Saturday, October 24, at 10 am. We continue to hold Mr. Mateucci and his loved ones in our hearts and continue to offer our deepest condolences.”

Following his passing, those who knew Mateucci have expressed their deep sadness at the accident and spoken highly of the late rector. “Brother Rogério spoke and acted with a rare gentleness and had a remarkable ability to listen, always making room for others to be heard,” Paulo Mussi Augusto, PhD, Kent State’s associate vice president for global education, who worked closely with Mateucci through a partnership between their universities, said in a statement. “He loved beauty and found it where art and science met. As a man of faith, he was steadfast, slow to judge, and always willing to give people another chance. His legacy will continue to inspire all of us who had the privilege of knowing him.”

#4777. CFTC sues Utah to block action against prediction markets

The US state filed a lawsuit against Kalshi last week, seeking to recover up to $60 million in profits related to event contracts on prediction markets.

The US Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) announced legal action against Utah officials after the state’s attorney general said he would sue to compel Kalshi and its affiliates to return up to $60 million in profits allegedly obtained illegally.

In a Monday filing in the US District Court for the District of Utah, lawyers for the commodities regulator sued the State of Utah and its Attorney General Derek Brown a week after officials announced legal action against prediction market platform Kalshi and its affiliates over allegedly illegal betting.

According to the CFTC, Utah was attempting to bypass congressional intent to “federally preempt state law” by “restrict[ing] the functioning of CFTC-registered exchanges.” CFTC Chair Michael Selig has been claiming for months that the regulator has “exclusive jurisdiction” over prediction markets, leading to several lawsuits between state and federal authorities.

“The event contracts targeted by Utah are ‘swaps’ under the federal [Commodity Exchange Act], and the exchanges that offer these event contracts for trading are CFTC-regulated Designated Contract Markets [DCMs],” said the lawsuit. “Utah’s efforts to prevent a CFTC-regulated DCM from offering CFTC-approved financial products intrude on the exclusive federal scheme Congress designed to oversee United States commodity derivatives markets.”

Kalshi spokesperson Jacki McGavick told Cointelegraph in response to the Utah lawsuit:

The case is another dispute between federal and state authorities over prediction markets that may be resolved only by a Supreme Court decision. In September, New Jersey officials petitioned the high court to weigh in on their case against Kalshi, similar to other lawsuits pending in Michigan, Ohio, Kentucky, Nevada, Massachusetts, Wisconsin and New York. As of Tuesday, the Supreme Court has not announced whether it will hear arguments in the case.

#4776. America Finally Approved This Sunscreen Ingredient After 27 Years. I Put It to the Test.

There’s plenty of buzz around BEMT right now. Does it live up to the hype?

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

TAKE ONE LOOK at our healthcare system, and it’s clear the US is still behind other developed nations in many ways. While affordable options, non-apocalyptic wait times, or the ability to easily speak to a human being on the phone when making an appointment are as fantastical in this country as Voldemort's horcruxes or the idea of the New York Jets ever winning the Super Bowl, this year brought a glimmer of progress toward the rest of the world. At least when it comes to sunscreen.

After the FDA approved bemotrizinol (BEMT for short) in June—a popular active in sunscreens used in Asia, Europe, and Australia since the late 90s—brands are finally now able to release products featuring it in the States. One of the first—and the best so far—is La Roche-Posay Anthelios UVDefense Sunscreen SPF 50 with BEMT, which was released early this week.

I was lucky enough to get an early look at the stuff and have been using it every day for over a month. Does it feel different than other SPFs? Is it effectively protective against UV rays? Will the Jets ever field a decent quarterback? Two of those questions I’ll answer below (and hey, who knows, maybe Arch Manning can save the New York somehow still faithful).

Obviously, the big inclusion here is BEMT, the first newly approved sunscreen filter since 1999. BEMT is a chemical UV filter, meaning it absorbs UV rays rather than blocking them as mineral sunscreen filters do. Okay, rad, but that’s nothing new. According to this writer, La Roche-Posay Anthelios Clear Skin Dry Touch SPF 60 is the best sunscreen for men—it’s also chemical-based and nearly half the price. So why all the fuss about BEMT?

Unlike many other products, BEMT absorbs both UVA rays (the ones that cause early signs of aging and can be a major contributor to skin cancer) and UVB rays (the ones that lead to surface-level damage and sunburn). BEMT can also absorb faster than other chemical actives, with a more lightweight application. Plus, it’s highly photostable, meaning it won’t break down and protect less effectively after being exposed to sunlight (a common issue with many SPF products).

La Roche-Posay tosses some classic chemical ingredients into this formula, as well, for good measure. There’s avobenzone, homosalate, octisalate, and octocrylene, which is the same quartet that highlights my go-to Anthelios Clear Skin Dry Touch SPF 60. There are even some skin-boosters in the mix, like glycerin and soybean oil for added hydration. Of course, it wouldn’t be a La Roche-Posay product if it wasn’t safe for sensitive skin, so it’s also fragrance-free.

When I first applied Anthelios UVDefense with BEMT, it was clear this was different from other sunscreens I've used. It has the look and weight of a thicker mineral sunscreen with the milky texture of a daily moisturizer—but absorbs faster than a gel SPF like Supergoop Unseen. We throw around the terms fast-absorbing and weightless a lot in skincare, but this is one of the rare occasions when a product really lives up to this claim.

I’ll start applying a dime-sized drop of this stuff, and it’s fully absorbed onto my face within 15 seconds without a trace. Not sure if this says more about how lightweight the sunscreen is or how useless my brain is before coffee, but there have been a couple of mornings when I couldn’t remember whether or not I applied the SPF—and had no way of determining if I did.

There is no semblance of a white cast after applying this stuff; it really feels and looks more like applying a lightweight moisturizer than SPF. There’s no weird chemically smell either, which is a sunscreen pet peeve of mine. The only major drawbacks are that it’s not water-resistant or sweat-resistant. But that’s what sunscreen for working out is for.

It’s always difficult to fully quantify how effective a sunscreen is at protecting against signs of aging or cancer, as these things develop over many months and years. However, I never saw any sun damage on days I applied this SPF. As a pasty dude, the biggest tell for if a sunscreen is effective for me is how susceptible my skin becomes to redness. Here, there were no signs of flushing or discoloration during the month I was using the sunscreen.

La Roche-Posay Anthelios UVDefense Sunscreen SPF 50 with BEMT takes full advantage of its newly available in the US hero ingredient with a lightweight formula that feels more like a moisturizer than sunscreen. It absorbs in seconds, leaves no white cast or chemical smell, and works especially well for everyday wear. The fragrance-free formula also makes it a strong pick for sensitive skin. The effective sun protection and quick application make a strong case for why we should have had access to BEMT in this country years ago.

#4775. Battlefield 6 may have actually done it: beaten Call of Duty

Battlefield 6 sales are setting all kinds of records, but one in particular has long eluded the Battlefield series.

If you’ve been paying any attention to the shooter market these past few months, you won’t be surprised to learn that Battlefield 6 has been an unprecedented success for EA, and the Battlefield series as a whole.

The game managed to sell over 7 million units at launch, climbing to the top of the charts anywhere you look, including setting new series concurrent player records on Steam. Thanks to new data, we now better understand the true scale of this launch.

The latest Circana report, which looks at game and hardware sales in the United States, has arrived. This iteration covers the month of October, which is when Battlefield 6 was released.

Battlefield 6 was the top-selling game across all platforms during October - which isn’t surprising. It has overtaken NBA 2K26 to become 2025’s best-selling game year-to-date, making it the best-selling Battlefield game ever.

In October alone, the game sold more than the lifetime sales of Battlefield 1, previously the best-selling entry in the franchise.

Battlefield 6 even set records beyond just its own series, having now produced the highest single-month dollar sales (physical and digital) total for any full game released in the last three years. The previous record holder was 2022’s Modern Warfare 2.

November’s report, which is set to arrive midway through December, will show, once and for all, whether Battlefield 6 has been able to overtake Black Ops 7. With one exception, Call of Duty games have, for more than a decade, topped the best-selling year-to-date chart. Regardless of the quality of the individual titles, or the competition in any given year, nothing has been able to shake that dominance.

Given all this momentum, Battlefield 6 has a shot at dethroning Call of Duty this year, beating Black Ops 7 to become the year’s best-seller. You never want to count out the sheer popularity of Call of Duty among casual players, however, which is what’s going to make December’s report all the more interesting.

#4774. CD Projekt is in "a really dangerous place", says Anthem's executive producer, pointing to ballooning costs of making games of that scope and quality

Mark Darrah predicts a "bifurcation" is coming

"The triple-A industry has become over-specialised, specialised to the point of hyper-fragility," Mark Darrah states in a foreboding interview found deep in the pages of the Summer Eternal Anthology, recently covered by Edwin. As a former executive producer at Bioware, who corralled teams on Anthem and Dragon Age, after starting his career as a programmer on Baldur's Gate, Neverwinter Nights, and Jade Empire, Darrah has seen firsthand the growing scale of video game production and the challenges it can bring.

Darrah's also been vocal about the difficult development of Anthem in the past, and the struggles he had at Bioware. However, while what he says in the interview may be familiar, I found it gained sharper definition when read in light of games industry news and some of the interviews I've had with developers this year. So let me pull together some of the choice bits with the extra context from our own RPS vaults.

I should say, I've not laid hands myself on the Summer Eternal Anthology - a 500-page manifesto/design document/making-of book for Red Rooster, the new RPG from a splinter group of former Disco Elysium developers. But Edwin, who acknowledges that he's acknowledged in the acknowledgements section, did send me some photos of the interview with Darrah that's found deep within its pages.

Anyway, back to the Darrah's warning of over-specialisation. One of the problems, he says, is that it's caused teams to balloon in size because "No one person can add anything into a triple-A game. It requires at least five people." Obviously, the immediate implication of this is that larger teams cost more to run. The more staff you have on payroll, the more you will need to pay out each month on salaries, the more office space you will need to rent, the more software licenses and equipment you will need to pay for. This isn't the first time this has been said, just last month I ran an interview with Saber Interactive's chief creative officer Tim Willits, who put some numbers on those costs. "Each one of those developers is expensive," Willits said. "You have 200 people that you're paying $15,000 per person per month, which gets you way over two million."

But Darrah then links the complexity of projects to the increased risk of a delay. "The reality is that the bigger your team, the more vulnerable you typically are to disruption in terms of time," Darrah says. "If you have a 500-person team and you go one month late, that's probably adding five, ten million to your budget right there. It doesn't take much. And it's very easy for a 500-person team to go one month late, just because of the sand in the gears of the machine."

To put that in another context, consider Grand Theft Auto VI. As Jason Schreier detailed in his Making Of feature for Bloomberg, where Rockstar's head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe had to go to Take-Two Chief Executive Officer Strauss Zelnick and tell him they needed to delay their mega game from a 2025 release until May 2026 and then go back to him a few months later to say it had to be delayed until November. "I think he said, 'You’re kidding. Please tell me you're kidding,'" she said. Now, bear in mind that there aren't 500 people working GTA 6, there are more than 4,700. Using Darrah and Willit's back of the napkin maths, that delay may have cost Rockstar more than $500m.

Darrah speculates that the growing complexity may also be encouraging larger studios to stay in the prototyping phase because "prototyping is the one place where the devs are still allowed to be creative, so they don't want to leave that phase because they don't want to get down to just executing lists." Though, as Willits told me, having a fully-staffed team working on a prototype is terrible news for a studio's bottom line – "you do not need 200 people before you have a vertical slice, you're burning money."

Even if a game does come out at the end of that long, expensive production, it now has to earn back significantly more to break even. But, more and more, we're seeing executives get scared of the mounting costs of an in-development game and cutting bait while it's still in the pre-production phase. Just look at Perfect Dark and Everwild.

Darrah touches on the risk of that long, hard-to-leave-behind prototyping phase, because while you want developers to add things to the game "you don't want them to be able to generate work for other people. And as people have gotten more specialised, the ability for people to add things to the game that doesn't generate work for other people is basically vanishingly small."

Threaded through the interview with Darrah is his prediction that the games industry is headed toward a "bifurcation", in which several games will tear away from the rest of the market and become an unreachable target for other publishers, forcing them to scale their expectations down. "GTA 6 is going to rip itself off and take five or six games with it, up into the upper echelon of 'we can spend any amount of money on our games and it's going to be all right,'" Darrah says. Along with Rockstar's crime 'em up, he suggests Assassin's Creed, Call of Duty, and Battlefield could join it. He says there are just too many games that have $100m budgets that aren't making that money back.

Darrah points to his old studio Bioware as an example of one of the victims of this split. "BioWare sat right at the point where it doesn't quite work." Shortly after Dragon Age: Veilguard's launch, EA CEO Andrew Wilson said the RPG had significantly underperformed, reaching only 1.5m players, less than the 3m the company had planned for. (Also, worth noting, that's not 1.5m sales, as the game had a free trial available and was available through EA's subscription service.)

As much as this may sound like something out of Bioshock, with all of the top-selling games breaking away and making a home under the sea where they just eat all of the money for the rest of time, Darrah paints a surprisingly optimistic picture. "If that is happening, then I think you're going to see a lot more studios the size of Sandfall Interactive's Expedition 33. These are teams that are pretty small, pretty focused – Clair Obscur was very small with unusually massive outsourcing – a roughly 40-person team."

As Nic (RPS in peace) explained in the past, the claim that Clair Obscur was made by 30 people hides the sheer number of people involved in its production. Darrah explains that the difference between Sandfall and so many other teams in the industry is that typically a studio has a representative of all the disciplines it needs in-house – designers, artists, programmers, testers, audio people – and outsourcing is used to expand the capacity of the team, providing artists to work with the art director, for instance. But, Darrah says, Sandfall is different. "It looks to me like what Sandfall did was to keep entire holes in their structure – they don't appear to have any internal animators at all, and they outsourced 100% of their animation." The nature of the game, a turn-based RPG, helps with that, but even then that's a case of the developer recognising a significant way to reduce the team's overhead.

(Another example of a studio seemingly outsourcing smartly to keep their headcount low, look at what Neon Giant's achieving with immersive sim No Law.)

I will also point out that, while outsourcing can reduce costs for a studio, for the recipients of the work it can mean low wages and a figurative straitjacket. South American developers told James earlier this year how, despite their evident expertise, it was still difficult to convince publishers they were perfectly capable to leading projects. All to often, they are only contracted to assist American studios on theirs.

If that great schism happens, with publishers giving up their dreams of making the next GTA or the next Call of Duty, it could, Darrah says, reshape the industry. "If that all goes away, then you'll get a bunch of room to make it work with titles that need to sell 3-4 million units," he says. "That's much more attractive. I don't want to be working on something like GTA, where if this thing doesn't sell 50 million units, it's going to lose money. That's insane."

Though, within this prediction there is something a smidge worrying: the future of CD Projekt. Darrah says the Witcher maker has entered that rarefied space of the mega game – "Their games now need to sell 15, 20 million units to make money." And with that capacity for success but also that necessity for success, well, Darrah says, "that's a really dangerous place to be."

Darrah doesn't offer any predictions for The Witcher 4 or Cyberpunk 2's prospects, but I can't help but think of the news in May that there are already twice as many people working on The Witcher 4 than the 240 who worked on The Witcher 3.

As I say, none of what Darrah is saying is new exactly, but when you look at what's been taking place in the last year, it does sound like a move away from the mega budgets of the early 2020s has to be on the cards for all but the very biggest studios, right?

#4773. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Circle’s USDC overtook Tether’s USDT in euro stablecoin trading as EU regulations reshaped local markets, Binance applied for a crypto license in Brazil ahead of an Oct. 30 deadline and Ripple secured final US regulatory approval to establish a national trust bank.

Circle’s USDC has overtaken Tether’s USDt in stablecoin trading against the euro, capturing 56% of the market as European exchanges adjust to the EU’s Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA), according to Kaiko.

In a new report, Kaiko said USDC’s share has surged from just 10% in January 2024, with the shift accelerating after major exchanges restricted USDT trading for customers in the European Economic Area.

USDC has also gained a liquidity advantage, with euro trading order books now roughly 40% deeper than USDT’s. However, Tether remains the dominant stablecoin globally, with a circulating supply approximately 2.5 times larger than USDC’s.

The findings follow the European Banking Authority’s decision to classify USDC as the EU’s first significant e-money token under MiCA, placing it under direct regulatory supervision.

Kaiko suggested a similar shift could emerge in the United States as regulators implement the GENIUS Act. The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency recently finalized reserve and redemption requirements for supervised stablecoin issuers, effective January 2027.

Further restrictions on noncompliant stablecoins are scheduled for July 2028, potentially benefiting Circle while Tether pursues the US market through its separately regulated USAT stablecoin.

Binance has applied for a cryptocurrency service provider license in Brazil, seeking to maintain its operations in one of Latin America’s largest crypto markets ahead of a regulatory deadline on Friday, Oct. 30.

The exchange submitted its application to the Central Bank of Brazil on Friday, Oct. 23, through its local entity, BBrasil, according to a statement shared with Cointelegraph.

The company said the filing would allow Brazilian users to continue accessing crypto assets, stablecoin trading, payments and investment services.

Binance has also announced changes for Brazilian users effective Tuesday, including restrictions on crypto lending, margin trading and other services. The exchange is also delisting 22 crypto assets in the country.

Brazil requires existing crypto service providers to submit authorization applications by Oct. 30 to continue operating under transitional arrangements. Filing an application does not constitute regulatory approval.

Ripple announced Monday that its national trust bank has secured final approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC).

The approval completes the chartering process for Ripple National Trust Bank (RNTB), which received conditional approval from the OCC in December 2025.

The federally supervised trust bank is authorized to manage reserves backing Ripple USD (RLUSD), act as collateral trustee for the benefit of RLUSD holders and provide digital asset custody services, according to Ripple.

RLUSD will continue to be issued by Standard Custody & Trust Company, Ripple’s limited purpose trust company regulated by the New York Department of Financial Services.

“We’ve always said crypto and banks work better together — this is what that looks like. Real innovation, real accountability, federal oversight,” Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse said on X on Monday.

#4770. Increasing Ozempic Dose Linked to Lower Heart Risk Than Switching to Mounjaro

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Increasing the dose of Ozempic rather than switching to Mounjaro may be associated with a lower risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE), according to a 2026 insurance claims analysis presented at the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting 2026 in Milan, Italy.

GLP-1 medications like semaglutide (Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro) have reshaped care for people with type 2 diabetes, but recent real-world evidence suggests these drugs may not offer identical heart protection.

It is important to keep in mind that this was a retrospective, observational study that showed an association, not causation.

More research is needed to fully compare the cardiovascular benefits of semaglutide versus tirzepatide.

The COMPETE SWITCH Cardiovascular (CV) analysis used Komodo Health’s Healthcare Map, a large US healthcare claims database with linked laboratory results, to examine real-world data from 636,525 adults with type 2 diabetes.

The analysis focused on 185,705 people who increased their semaglutide dose and 23,104 who switched to tirzepatide.

All participants included in the analysis were already receiving 1 mg of semaglutide (Ozempic) weekly.

The researchers found that people who increased their Ozempic dose to 2 mg had a 6% lower risk of MACE — including all-cause death, myocardial infarction (heart attack), and stroke — compared with those who switched to Mounjaro at doses up to 15 mg.

“This is an interesting study, but the findings may partly reflect medication adherence. When patients switch medications, it can take time to adjust the dose and find the right treatment level, which may temporarily affect how well their condition is controlled,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

He also noted that the study did not determine what might explain the difference in cardiovascular risk.

“It may be related to a combination of weight loss and direct effects on hormones involved in metabolism and appetite regulation,” Ali said.

Semaglutide and tirzepatide are both GLP-1 medications. Ozempic (semaglutide) and Mounjaro (tirzepatide) are approved to help manage type 2 diabetes, while each medication also has a different brand approved for weight loss.

The semaglutide approved for weight loss in the U.S. is sold as Wegovy, and the tirzepatide approved for weight loss is sold as Zepbound.

“Semaglutide and tirzepatide work in a similar way. While semaglutide is a direct GLP-1 receptor agonist, tirzepatide is a dual agonist of both the GIP and GLP-1 receptors,” Amit Blumfield, MD, with Manhattan Cardiology in NYC, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline.

“Because they work through somewhat different pathways, they can have different effects in individual patients,” added Ali.

While both semaglutide (Ozemipc) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro) work in similar ways, you may find that you tolerate one better than the other.

Ali noted that you should discuss any potential benefits and drawbacks with your healthcare professional.

“I would only recommend switching medications if there is an issue with tolerance of one or the other. If one medication is well-tolerated at increasingly higher doses and is effective in the desired weight-loss and blood sugar management, I wouldn’t necessarily switch,” added Blumfield.

These side effects are generally mild and may lessen over time. However, if side effects become difficult to tolerate, you should speak with your healthcare professional.

“GLP-1 medications can be highly effective for managing diabetes and promoting weight loss, but there is no one-size-fits-all treatment. The best medication depends on an individual’s health needs, goals, response to treatment, and potential side effects,” said Ali.

#4769. Ripple, Robinhood expand partnership to bring RLUSD to retail and institutional markets

Ripple also expanded its collaboration with BNY on digital asset custody and partnered with C2FO to bring trade finance settlement onto the XRP Ledger.

Ripple and Robinhood have expanded their partnership to bring the RLUSD stablecoin to Robinhood’s retail and institutional businesses, alongside new integrations spanning treasury management, lending and trading services.

RLUSD is already available on Robinhood Chain and Bitstamp by Robinhood, with a launch in the Robinhood app planned for later this year, according to a Tuesday announcement coinciding with Ripple’s Swell 2026 conference in New York. Robinhood also plans to use the stablecoin on the XRP Ledger for institutional settlement.

Robinhood will also adopt Ripple Treasury to manage its global cash and liquidity operations, including payment processing, cash flow forecasting and intercompany transactions. The integration extends the partnership beyond customer-facing services to Robinhood’s internal financial infrastructure.

The partnership also expands Robinhood’s existing relationship with Ripple Prime, which provides financing services to Bitstamp by Robinhood and plans to introduce perpetual futures trading on the exchange later this year.

Launched in December 2024, RLUSD has reached a market capitalization of $2.57 billion, with nearly $1 billion issued on the XRP Ledger, according to DeFiLlama data. Robinhood reported 28.6 million funded customers globally as of August, offering a potentially significant distribution channel for the stablecoin.

Related: Majority of Americans want candidates who support US-based financial technology: Poll

Ripple also announced on Tuesday an expanded partnership with financial services giant BNY, which now supports custody of XRP and RLUSD through its Digital Asset Custody platform. The companies plan to work together to allow BNY clients to mint and redeem RLUSD directly through the bank and broaden support for tokenized assets issued on the XRP Ledger.

Separately, Ripple partnered with working-capital platform C2FO to bring financing and settlement for its new Instant Pay service onto the XRP Ledger, using RLUSD. The service is designed to let suppliers receive payments against approved invoices in seconds rather than waiting days for traditional bank settlement.

A C2FO spokesperson told Cointelegraph the service is currently in testing, with a controlled launch and the first RLUSD transactions on the XRP Ledger expected by the end of the year.

The string of announcements on Tuesday followed Ripple’s receipt of final approval from the US Office of the Comptroller of the Currency on Monday to establish a national trust bank. The institution will manage reserves backing RLUSD and provide digital asset custody services, while stablecoin issuance will remain with Ripple’s New York-regulated subsidiary.
