#4749. The pleasures of Double Fine's Thank You Bus Driver are not in the destination, nor the journey, but in slapping my disobedient passengers

Showing some gratitude is the least these scribbly wretches can do. The passengers in Thank You Bus Driver – Double Fine’s new comedy public transport sim – are almost invariably rowdy, entitled hypocrites, happy to sully my single-decker by picking their toes and bellowing down their phones, while only ever being seconds away from a strop if another traveller does the same. Still, here I am, locked in symbiotic misery with them, desperate to choke down the scraps of acknowledgement they’ll spare for arriving at their stop in good time. I hate them. Please love me.

To be clear, I don’t regret playing Thank You Bus Driver. It’s a brisk three hours of chaotic wheelsmanship, dense in visual gags and amusingly exaggerated physics, in which you are both driver and disciplinarian. Catch sight of someone breaking the bus rules, and instead of detachedly tapping a sign, you leave your seat to deliver a corrective slap. This risks the still-moving bus careering off course, and thus a shift-ending missed stop, but silencing the troublemakers is the only way to ensure everyone else arrives happy. And happiness means Thank Yous, the only currency that matters.

It’s a fun little time, with just enough multitasking to stretch your attention without snapping it in frustration. I wonder, though, which Double Fine made it. Was it the newly independent, hugs and jokes, Amnesia Fortnight Double Fine? Or the Double Fine who talked themselves out of a union, and whose flight from Microsoft ownership involved jettisoning a quarter of their staff?

There are ample signs of the 'nice' one. Not that games like Keeper and Kiln were creatively bankrupt trendchasers, but Thank You Bus Driver has that endearing, rough-and-tumble quality of human beings trying something out. Passengers come in the form of Bigfoots and swimming rings and microwaves full of fish, and someone – maybe multiple people! – took the time to give them all uniquely goofy animations for dropping a coin in a slot. Your slapping arm is uncomfortably long and rubbery, a pneumatic limb whose weirdness goes completely unaddressed. Crashes and, indeed, your own acts of open-palmed violence are exclusively played for laughs – usually good ones. I confess to intentionally aiming for the local cows, just because their betrayed expression and excessively long windscreen-sliding motion make for too well-executed a joke to resist.I adore, too, how 'resetting' the bus from a stuck position drops it back on the road... from thirty feet in the air, metal groaning as it slams into the asphalt.

It can be clever, too. There is a heavy randomisation element, from the placement of stops to the timing of passenger behaviour to the vaguely roguelike-ish item upgrades you’re offered by a commuting trenchcoat oddball, but the necessity of juggling bus happiness with bus urgency breeds actual tactics. Do you move carefully, keeping a constant eye on the mirror to instantly slap down transgressions, at the cost of letting the shift clock tick down? Or do you focus on caning it through town, allowing passenger resentment to build before attempting a mass slapfest to restore harmony at the last moment? In what probably doesn’t bode well for whenever I drive my future children to Legoland, I’ve developed a taste for the latter, and have devised a kind of 'unguided missile' technique based on its principles: point the bus somewhat towards the next stop, accelerate, then spin around to beat down on farepayers as we all roll to a gentle stop. Or off a cliff.

For all of its spark, though, I can’t shake the feeling that Thank You Bus Driver has some more cynical thinking behind it. It is, ultimately, a Numbers Go Up game: there’s no real narrative, and no jeopardy beyond having to start a new shift if you run out of time. Your one and only reason to exist is a bigger collection of Thank Yous, which are delivered with slot machine chime effects, and pursued through service to arseholes. Like a less subversive Roman Sands RE:Build, on wheels.

Would it be unfair to suggest this has a lingering whiff of Microsoft about it? You can, at least, throw hands with your customers. But even a full house of swiftly punished fish-microwavers will just be replaced at the next stop with a new, possibly identical batch. The grind for approval never ends, until it does, when the game decides you’ve amassed enough gratitude points to be considered a valuable entity.

That said, Thank You Bus Driver doesn’t have much legs beyond a single playthrough anyway. While those three hours are entertaining enough, in spite of the service industry angst, there’s no getting round the fact that you’re driving the same bus through the same small town with the same handful of rules, every time. Attempts to mix this up fall flat: night shifts, rain, and 'conventions' (where the bulk of your passengers share a single archetype) are eventually thrown in, but none of them meaningfully change how a drive plays out. You’re more at the mercy of the regular randomisations, where an unlucky succession of distant stops can kill a shift even with careful passenger management. Chaining nearby stops, likewise, is the best (if equally chance-dependent) means of accumulating mad TYs.

Still, as a single, foot-down charge to the credits, it’s a decent way to chuckle your way through an afternoon. And to answer my own question, I still think Thank You Bus Driver shows off Double Fine’s creative flair, much more so than it raises worries about the new era. Hopefully that spirit endures into their future independent projects – maybe with a little less gacha ding-dinging.

#4748. In a New Production Space in the Berkshires, Ceramist Danny Kaplan is Scaling Up and Dreaming Big

Ceramists tend to have an easygoing embrace of fate. Misfirings happen in the kiln, and happy accidents do too. Last year, as the New York City–based designer Danny Kaplan began canvassing for a larger Brooklyn workshop—a search complicated by steep rents and necessary buildouts—his longtime production manager, Val Shamma, shared that he and his wife were moving to North Adams, Massachusetts. “It occurred to us that maybe the solution was actually to follow him,” says Kaplan, best known for his sculptural lighting and furniture. The Berkshires city, like so many once-bustling mill towns, had experienced a downturn in the mid-20th century before art institutions like MASS MoCA helped put it on the cultural map. Former industrial spaces beckoned. “Once the pieces started to fall into place, it made perfect sense,” he says, eventually landing on a roomy, ground-floor warehouse in a converted textile mill.

A detail of the collection's ceramic tiles awaiting application

Now, with its electrical system updated to support three kilns and a glass garage door added to let in natural light, this is where Kaplan does much of his ceramic casting and larger sculptural work. (His original Bushwick workshop still produces lamps and other hand-built pieces.) This past summer in the Berkshires, the studio was churning out slim, colorful tiles for his Delf collection—a collaboration with Vince Patti’s furniture firm Lesser Miracle. (Dutch for canal or ditch, delf refers to the grooved wood that holds the tile.) The pair have been working together since 2024, when they teamed up on a custom bed for Kaplan featuring a wraparound headboard and a border of inset tiles. This fall, Delf II extends that design language to a console, a coffee table, a desk, and a nightstand, all in oiled oak with octagonal tops. The ceramic components, made in a range of glazes, travel to Lesser Miracle’s woodshop in Brooklyn, where Patti oversees the final assembly.

While this new space has increased Kaplan’s production capability, he’s more interested in the opportunities it poses for expansive thinking. In time, he’d like to host visiting artists in need of a scene change, all while knitting in with North Adams neighbors. “It’s very serene,” he says, “and ceramic people love community.”

#4747. Battlefield 6 kicks off its first free trial post the Black Ops 7 launch to twist that knife

Activision has done its own fair share of Battlefield 6 counterprogramming prior to that game's release, and EA is here to repay the favour with Black Ops 7.

Even if you don’t care about the rivalries between the top multiplayer first-person shooters series, it’s hard to ignore how much EA wanted to stick it to Call of Duty this year with Battlefield 6.

Activision saw that coming early enough that it ran multiple free weekends around major moments for EA’s game, including its launch, seemingly in an effort to capture some of those players and keep them away from BF6.

Both Battlefield 6, and Black Ops 7 have now launched, and though exact sales figures may never be detrmined (though EA did announce 7 million sold in the first week), it’s very clear at this point from every metric we can see that BF6 came out on top this year.

The recent Black Ops 7 launch clearly hasn’t made many people happy, so just like Activision made Black Ops 6 free to play at BF6’s launch, EA has now done the same with its own game. The Battlefield 6 free trial, which was announced last week, is just an hour away at the time of this writing.

This is the first trial for the core game, and it arrives some six weeks after Battlefield 6 officialy launched, and four weeks following the release of the free-to-play Battlefield Redsec, which has battle royale and other components.

Beyond all the drama surrounding the two rival shooter franchises, the Battlefield 6 trial is itself pretty straightfoward, and is notably nowhere near as genrous as some of the Call of Duty ones we’ve seen recently.

The trial goes live today at 4am PT, 7am ET, 12pm GMT across all platforms, and will be available for a week; ending Tuesday, December 2. It includes three core maps: Siege of Cairo, Eastwood, and Blackwell Fields.

Players will have access to four game modes, played on different versions of the same maps. Conquest, Breakthrough, Sabotage, and Team Deathmatch will all be available to choose from, and there don’t appear to be any level or XP limits in the trial.

Unfortunatley, just as the free trial was about to go live, the game got hit with a major matchmaking outage that took down all matchmaking in the core game, leaving Redsec unaffected.

Hopefully EA can resolve this issue before the trial officially kicks off.

#4746. CF Benchmarks brings Wall Street pricing infrastructure onchain, including data used by BlackRock

CF Benchmarks, whose pricing indices underpin BlackRock's bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) and CME Group's crypto futures, is bringing its market data to blockchain networks as more financial firms look to offer traditional investment products through decentralized finance (DeFi).

The company, which is owned by Kraken parent Payward, has introduced CFB Oracle, a service that gives blockchain applications access to its benchmarks for cryptocurrencies, stocks, bonds, commodities and currencies.

The idea is to make the same pricing infrastructure used by major financial institutions available to developers building investment products on blockchains.

For example, a blockchain-based fund holding tokenized stocks would need reliable prices to calculate the value of its portfolio. A lending platform accepting tokenized bonds as collateral would need that data to determine how much users can borrow and when positions should be liquidated.

CF Benchmarks already provides this type of pricing data to traditional financial markets. Its indices are used by products representing more than $120 billion in assets, according to the company.

With CFB Oracle, developers can use those benchmarks to build products such as onchain index funds, tokenized portfolios and derivatives without having to develop their own pricing systems. The service is available on Ethereum ETH$2,490.55, HyperEVM and Robinhood Chain, with plans to support other compatible networks.

CF Benchmarks says the data is digitally signed, allowing applications to check that prices came from the company and haven't been altered. It also publishes each index at the same frequency for all customers, rather than charging for faster access.

The service has a planned use within Payward. The Kraken parent intends to use CFB Oracle to supply pricing data for an upcoming U.S. perpetual futures offering powered by Hyperliquid (HYPE). That product is still awaiting regulatory approval.

The launch puts CF Benchmarks in competition with oracle providers such as Chainlink LINK$12.64 and Pyth, which already supply market data to decentralized trading and lending platforms.

CFB Oracle won't issue funds or handle trades itself. Its role is to supply the reference prices those products need to operate.

#4744. The 10 Best Deodorants for Men in 2026, Tested by Grooming Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

IT DOESN'T MATTER who you are or what you do; wearing deodorant is an everyday requirement for living in a modern society. If you're spending any amount of time around other people, it's a common courtesy to manage your body odor. Sweating is, of course, completely normal, but that doesn’t mean you have to be stinky, too. Great-smelling colognes, bar soaps, or body washes are certainly a huge part of the equation, but a quality deodorant is an equally necessary component.

A good deodorant is your most important tool in the fight against B.O. Body odor is caused by two things: Naturally occurring bacteria on the surface of your skin (especially in places like your underarms) and sweat, which the bacteria feed on. Deodorants help reduce the amount of stank-causing bacteria on your skin while also controlling odor by covering it up with a much more pleasant scent.

The most important thing to note is that deodorants won’t actually stop sweat; that’s an antiperspirant’s job, thanks to the addition of aluminum, which physically blocks sweat glands. You’ll notice this list doesn’t include any antiperspirants for that reason. However, sweating is natural, and just because you sweat doesn’t mean you’ll automatically stink.

Many people avoid the aluminum in antiperspirants in favor of deodorants that still get the job done without reducing sweat. Deodorants can also be ideal for people with sensitive skin thanks to the addition of gentle, often natural ingredients—and a natural deodorant can be a great option for these instances. Choosing between a deodorant and an antiperspirant is a personal choice (and, for the record, you can switch back and forth), so find one that fits your lifestyle and give it a shot.

Oars + Alps is a powerful, natural ingredients-backed deodorant that absorbs odor, while botanical agents nourish the skin. It’s vegan and cruelty-free with no alcohol, aluminum, parabens, sulfates, gluten, or phthalates. The proprietary alpine caribous moss ingredient sourced from the Arctic Circle in Northern Finland is antioxidant-rich and boosts elasticity while guarding against environmental hazards like pollution and inflammation.

Testing Notes: One of my biggest gripes with natural deodorants—apart from when they just don’t work—is residue. Whether chalky or oily, nothing is worse than when you put on a fresh shirt only for it to be ruined by deodorant schmutz. I am happy to report that in my testing process, I found that these deodorants went on clear and did not leave any residue at all on my clothes.

I also took into account that some online reviewers mentioned needing multiple applications—I found that this largely has to do with the scent you’re using and how it reacts to your own body chemistry. Some of the longer-lasting scents were enough to last all day. It may just take some trial and error until you find the right scent for you.

If you're someone who hates deodorant marks on your clothes (or, let’s get real, on your underwear), this spray will be a game-changer for you. Its application is genuinely invisible, and its formula is designed with natural ingredients to keep your entire body (pits, groin, everything) fresh without any residue. Packed with plant-based goodies like arrowroot powder, coconut oil, and shea butter, it not only soothes your skin but also provides odor protection for up to 72 hours. Not to mention, it's free of aluminum, phthalates, parabens, and dyes, so there’s no risk of irritation anywhere on the body after applying.

Testing Notes: While I’ll admit that I didn't personally test it without showering for more than 48 hours (couldn't go three days, c'mon), I can vouch for its impressive performance during that time. Plus, the scent (bourbon leather with just a hint of vanilla) is as masculine and pleasant as they come in terms of deodorants. I was also super impressed by how quickly the spray absorbed into the skin and how little it left behind, which was truly nothing.

Sure, tons of brands offer long-lasting deodorants, but when a deodorant has been put to the test by over 1,000 U.S. Special Forces soldiers, you know it’s the real deal to combat odor. Bravo Sierra is a grooming line created with the military to provide eco-friendly essentials for men at a low price that can’t be beat. The whole line is genius and worthy of your hard-earned dollars, but if you’re going to try one item, I’d have to say this aluminum-free deodorant is just the must-have men have been looking for.

Not to mention, the unscented option is about as skin-friendly as you can get. Without any baking soda or aluminum, the plant-based formula packs a killer combo of cassava root extract, prebiotics, and aloe vera to fight odor without irritating skin. That being said, some of the oils in here may stain some clothing if you're in a hurry, but allow it to dry for a few minutes, and you’ll be fine.

When you switch from aluminum-based products, expect a short purging period. That’s just your body clearing out aluminum. (It’ll only feel like you’re sweating more.) This minty deodorant contains natural moisturizers like shea butter to keep your underarms smooth. Plus, it's formulated to be used on your entire body—pits, crotch, legs—it's all fair game to be protected with this stuff.

While I don’t usually love a solid bar, I was pleasantly surprised that this one didn’t leave any residue. Just make sure to give your pits a bit of drying time before putting on clothes to minimize the risk.

The scent is strong enough to last all day, even for heavy sweaters, and while it won’t reduce actual sweating, it absolutely prevents it from smelling.

The strength of the scent isn’t a bad thing—in fact, if you feel like your smell can be strong at times, this kind of deodorant is perfect for masking it for hours. Since there’s no fuss to the formula, it’s all about the scent with this guy, though I did also appreciate that it goes on completely clear without any chalky or greasy residue.

This easy-to-apply deodorant gel is completely clear, and a little more watery than your typical stick, which makes it way easier to apply and get full coverage with less product needed. It comes in a few different scents, including fragrance-free, but even the scented versions are subtle and not overwhelming. The best thing about it, however, is the skincare-like ingredients: niacinamide to help reduce dark spots and exfoliating alpha hydroxy acids to keep your pits smooth (it’s especially ideal if you shave or trim your armpit hair).

Testing Notes: I’ve said it already, but I’ll say it again: give this one ample dry time before putting on clothes. Because of the liquidy-serum formula, it takes quite a while to dry compared to your typical stick and definitely compared to a spray. The formula is soothing and pleasant to use, once you get the hang of the extended drying time needed.

If you’re someone who finds themselves constantly with irritated pits and redness, a deodorant balm could be exactly the type of product you’ve been waiting for, and there is no better option than AKT’s. This creamy deodorant has the consistency of a thick moisturizer but absorbs into the skin much faster. Designed to be used anywhere on the body, it offers smooth and lasting protection with minimal risk of skin irritation.

During a few regrettable days of going around 36 to 48 hours without showering, the fragrance still covered any (presumably well into development) odors that would have been emerging. Granted, these were mostly days of going into the office and avoiding (read: skipping) workouts, but it was a sight (and smell) to behold nonetheless.

When deciding on a deodorant, look for one with a scent that fits your interests. While a stronger scent might not necessarily indicate longer-lasting protection, it usually means a longer-lasting fragrance. With that in mind, consider what colognes you typically use when deciding on a deodorant to avoid clashing scents. And if you'd prefer your cologne do the heavy lifting in the smell department—or have sensitive skin—stick to lightly scented or fragrance-free options.

In terms of ingredients, the first thing to note is that deodorants only control odor, not sweat. For sweat reduction, head for an antiperspirant instead. To control odor, look for deodorants with antibacterial ingredients like magnesium or aloe vera, as well as odor-absorbing factors like tapioca starch and arrowroot powder. Additionally, if you're prone to skin irritation, opt for fragrance-free or baking soda-free (known to fight odor-causing bacteria), as baking soda has been shown to cause irritation in some people. Also, keep an eye out for moisturizing agents like shea butter or vitamin E.

Finally, you’ll want to consider the delivery mechanism. Most deodorants come in solid stick form, but they're not for everyone. Some feel the chalkier ones carry more risk of leaving stains on clothes, while others find the gel sticks leave lingering residue. Sprays, strict gels, and lotions are other options available if you’re not into the stick thing. Mostly, when it comes to deodorants and your own unique brand of odor, you may want to consider trying a variety of formulas to see what works best with your body chemistry.

As Men's Health's grooming editor, I've tested more than 35 deodorants in the past year. I tried each one for at least one week and evaluated its scent, price point, and effectiveness. I also considered how each deodorant and its scent held up over time and during more rigorous activities like exercise or high-stress situations, as well as its impact on my skin and even clothing.

I then consulted with my colleagues, including contributing editor Garret Munce and Deputy Commerce Editor Christian Gollayan, who collectively have more than a decade of experience swiping and trying deodorants, on which brands they use and recommend. Based on this extensive research and testing, below are the 10 best deodorants for men.

The biggest difference between deodorants and antiperspirants (which actually block sweat) is that they contain aluminum. To date, aluminum is the only FDA-approved ingredient to effectively block sweat, and it does this by physically plugging up the sweat glands in your armpits. However, in recent years, aluminum has come under fire as some people claim it is linked to certain diseases like cancer and Alzheimer’s disease. Because of this, there’s been a rise in the popularity of aluminum-free deodorants like the ones you see on this list.

Does that mean they’re healthier for you? "We don’t have any studies that support the claim that there are health risks to using aluminum [in antiperspirants]," says dermatologist Dr. Michelle Henry, MD. "We eat more aluminum than we actually get in our antiperspirants. The aluminum we get in our antiperspirants doesn’t affect us in any negative way." Whether or not you use deodorant or antiperspirant is a personal choice.

#4743. Penguin Colony review - the least expected Soulslike you'll play, and a cracking parody of Lovecraftian empires

Penguin Colony reviewA clever and unforgiving send-up of Lovecraftian race horror, with lumpy platforming and a surprising amount of FromSoftware DNA.

Penguin Colony is probably the best pisstake of Lovecraft and Lovecraftian horror I've played. It's the best because it places Lovecraft's universe at a creaturely remove, amplifying its absurdity without having to drop any punchlines. Entering a ravaged polar campsite, I advance among burning tents and uniformed corpses to a pioneer seated on a crate, who is busily sawing his own face off. Ice and snow shrill overhead and Lenval Brown's narration rumbles in my bones, speaking of eldritch relics and unquenchable ambitions, of "queer creatures" and inexplicable deaths, of madness and glory, of "scientific explanations" and empires from other dimensions. I consider all this, and say: Fnaaarp.

Another man standing at the top of a pile of sandbags catches my attention. I waddle up to him and his head explodes, emitting a gory spark that resolves into the word "sex". I regard the body gravely and say: urk urk urk. Then I flop on my belly and slither out of camp, chasing the whistling wires of the wind. For I am a penguin. These creatures, their flags, their words and their elder gods mean little to me. I might accidentally witness their hubris and self-deception and savage talk of racial hierarchies and destiny, but I am not some befuddled Miskatonic bookworm consumed by sweaty urges toward worlds beyond ken. I am a penguin. I just happened to be hopping through here while all this weird shit was going on.

All told, the penguins of Penguin Colony are a winningly silly source of critical distance from the carnival-mirror imperialism of, in this case, At The Mountains Of Madness, a Lovecraft novella that usefully features a bunch of monster penguins. Created by Umurangi Generation devs Origame Digital, an Indigenous team working in Melbourne on Wurundjeri Woiwurrung land, Penguin Colony retains the novella's broad arc of explorers from the global north venturing into a frozen eldritch necropolis, but otherwise gives the story a kicking.

In this adaptation, set a few years after Lovecraft's death, the explorers have become German Nazi occultists with a few English collaborators, including Brown's temporally adrift narrator. Rather than the impossible architecture of Lovecraft's stories, later areas resemble dessicated modern cities built on stolen land. The game also introduces a redemption narrative in the shape of a group of Māori warriors, who are briefly controllable between chapters. The Māori group are questing to stop the dafty fascists from waking a dormant starspawn, which they have somehow misidentified as the "one true Aryan king". Again, though, none of that's your problem. You're a goddamn penguin.

There are several playable penguin varieties in Penguin Colony, and you can switch between them freely by walking up to one and hitting Y button. Should you accidentally flop into a crevasse or a puddle of wormy Shoggoth juices, the world is full of back-up birds, dozing on bergs or in caverns. There are rudiments of a class system, which factors into optional terrain puzzles: some penguins are heavy enough to press floor switches, while baby penguins can't swim but are able to fit through tunnels, revealing new areas or shortcuts.

While the game recreates the calls of different species, I'm not sure Penguin Colony does much justice to the inner life of penguins. They're just a fun, disposable dramatic device, riffing on Pingu and Happy Feet as much as they do the six-foot dreadsquawkers of Lovecraft's story. I'm also not sure Penguin Colony is the best penguin platformer I've played, which isn't exactly a huge tent, because penguins, bless 'em, are not obvious ground-based acrobats. The penguins in Penguin Colony are, to be frank, janky as hell. They boop and stutter up inclines, often allowing you to 'cheat' the geography. They 'slide' like their bottoms are misfiring turbojets. In general, the game's presentation is a bit of a penguin right now. Tutorial tips reappear erratically, and there's a stamina circle that takes up far too much of the screen.

The penguins are overall performant in getting you from A to B, but this ain't sand-surfing in Journey. Still, one benefit of being a janky-ass penguin is that you often have to move quite ponderously, which enforces a lengthier appreciation of the game's environments - especially, the bleeding helix catacombs and blue-glared "downtown" areas of later chapters. There's some terrific scenery in Penguin Colony, beyond the opening dreary ice sheet: one enormous ruin houses a lumpy, rippled structure that could be either a brain coral or a god's petrified brain, defying both comprehension and articulation in the maze-folded contours of its squamous and primordial immensity. Or as my penguin at the time put it: HOOONK.

While I was expecting Penguin Colony to put the boot into settlers and Lovecraft, after trying the demo, I was startled to find that on some level, it's a Soulslike. Or maybe an anti-Soulslike. Later on, there's a "boss battle" featuring a giant, jerky Shoggowotsit that pays open homage to FromSoftware, complete with harrowing orchestral score and AOE gravity attacks. It's like somebody tried to rebuild the Gaping Dragon out of mops and pieces of accordion. Being a penguin, I merely flopped on my belly once again and scooched through the horror's ankles, onto the next region. I have done likewise with many Souls enemies, unless restrained by fog gates. Is there a Penguin Knight in Dark Souls? There should be.

In the wake of that encounter, I thought anew about all the non-penguin life I'd met during my four-hour Penguin Colony playthrough – all the near-dead fascists in foetal positions or kneeling before idols, their bodies mutilated, bleating in despair. You see hollow folk like that all over Lordran, the difference here being that as one in a million local penguins, I am not perceived as an intruder. No corner ambushes for me. The same likely isn't true of the Māori characters, who are essentially playing a 'real' Soulslike behind the scenes, navigating a more hostile incarnation of the same world.

The giant, jerky Shoggowotsit also features in one of the Māori adventurer sequences. The game dangles the carrot of a 'proper' bossfight, here, but fades mockingly to white when you take the first swing. Hence, 'anti-Soulslike': Penguin Colony invokes certain genre expectations in order to frustrate them. Or perhaps, to redirect them.

In lieu of combat, the FromSofty parallels appear most strongly in the torturous, Blighttown-worthy route design, and especially, the framing of pick-ups. There are collectible glowballs that bestow not gear and XP, but English and Te reo Māori words that often chime with or invite commentary on their surroundings – a really intriguing revising of the poetic distribution of item lore in Dark Souls. There are also photographs, taken by a Nazi propagandist, that self-combust to release snatches of flashback dialogue.

After the credits roll, you're shown a Zodiac constellation of all the lore you've found, a web of secrets and lies the devs offer in-game as a basis for recapturing "pre-social media secret finding and community storytelling". It's an incentive to replay, though Origame Digital's Tali Faulkner has elsewhere commented that the team would rather people don't try to 100% the lore – it's more about swapping notes with other players, like doom-sodden Miskatonic boffins trying to work out which bit of Cthulhu goes where.

Origame Digital plan to make more Lovecraftian games - they do, indeed, have their sights on Cthulhu, having begun their adventure with a Lovecraft story that is simultaneously well-known and less-explored, by virtue of starring a different faction of cosmic deities. I hope they also continue their experiments with Soulslike design, because aside from putting the H.P. in Penguin, their latest game has shed a strange new light on a genre I thought I knew all too well.

Find out how we conduct our reviews by reading our review policy.

#4742. Taking Blood Pressure Meds? DASH Diet Likely Safe for Potassium Levels

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

People who adhere to the Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension (DASH) diet do not experience significant changes in potassium levels while taking blood pressure medications or nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory (NSAID) drugs, according to a new study.

Researchers also reported that people taking these medications do not experience significant changes in their kidney function.

Both types of medications are known to increase potassium levels. High potassium levels can lead to hyperkalemia, a condition that can cause issues with the heart rhythm. People with impaired kidney function due to chronic kidney disease are at increased risk of hyperkalemia.

“The DASH diet is a well-known, proven nutrition pattern that is known to improve blood pressure,” said Marian Budu, MD, a clinical research coordinator on the GoFresh Trials at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center in Boston and the presenting author of the study, in a news release.

“Knowing that the DASH diet did not increase blood potassium levels in this population with normal baseline potassium levels provides assurance that the DASH diet can be prescribed to a wide range of people,” she continued.

The preliminary findings are being presented at the American Heart Association’s Hypertension Scientific Sessions 2026 meeting this week in Virginia. The research has not been published yet in a peer-reviewed journal.

Experts not involved in the study told Healthline that the research provides medical professionals with important information.

“While we have traditionally exercised caution before recommending the DASH diet in patients who are prescribed ACE inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers, or potassium-sparing diuretics, these results show that those patients could likely adopt the DASH diet without significant concern for harm,” said Cheng-Han Chen, MD, an interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA.

“DASH diets do not appear to increase potassium levels to any significant degree in patients taking medications that are known to potentially raise serum potassium,” added Kevin Volpp, MD, the founding director of the Penn Center for Health Incentives and Behavioral Economics in Pennsylvania and the scientific lead of the American Heart Association’s Health Care by Food initiative.

“This is important because it is a theoretical concern that diets rich in potassium could cause high levels in potassium in some patients.”

The new study was a secondary analysis of the GoFresh and GoFresh Rx randomized trials that enrolled Black adults in the greater Boston area with high blood pressure.

Some of the participants were taking medications to lower their blood pressure, and others were not taking any hypertension drugs.

The study focused on four classes of blood pressure medications: angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACEi), angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs), and potassium-sparing diuretics and beta-blockers, as well as NSAIDs.

The analysis took place in 2026 and included 356 Black adults with an average age of 53. About 70% were women.

At the start of the study, the participants had systolic blood pressure readings between 120 mm Hg and less than 150 mm Hg. They also had a diastolic blood pressure reading of less than 100 mm Hg.

The participants also had varying degrees of kidney function. In addition, they had limited access to grocery stores.

Half of the participants received three months of weekly DASH-patterned groceries and weekly nutrition counseling sessions. The other half received a cash stipend every four weeks to buy groceries, with no nutrition counseling.

The participants’ blood potassium levels were measured at the beginning of the study and again after 12 weeks.

The researchers reported that eating more potassium-rich foods in the DASH diet did not increase potassium levels to dangerous levels among participants taking blood pressure medications.

They noted no significant increase in potassium levels, regardless of whether participants were assigned to the dietary intervention or the monetary stipend group.

In a news release, the researchers acknowledged that their study did not include people with stage 4 or stage 5 kidney disease or those with higher-than-normal potassium levels.

They added that the study population comprised only Black individuals living in Boston, so the results may not generalize to the broader population.

The study authors said that future research should focus on participants with suboptimal kidney function and those with higher potassium levels.

Volpp agreed that more research is needed.

“This study was conducted in patients with non-elevated potassium levels at baseline and does not answer the question of whether they are safe in patients with elevated potassium levels or significant impairments in renal function,” he told Healthline.

Chen noted that potassium levels should be taken seriously.

“Certain classes of blood pressure medications can potentially cause high potassium levels, especially in people with kidney disease,” he told Healthline. “If potassium levels become too high, this can lead to symptoms such as fatigue, nausea, and muscle pain, or even serious heart problems such as arrhythmias.”

The DASH diet is a dietary plan that emphasizes fruits, vegetables, and lean proteins such as chicken, fish, and beans.

The plan also includes whole grains, low fat dairy products, and cooking with vegetable oils. The diet discourages the intake of red meat, salt, fat, and added sugars.

Previous research has indicated that the DASH diet can provide numerous health benefits.

A preliminary 2022 study concluded that the dietary plan can help lower blood pressure and improve heart health, but those results haven’t been published in a scientific journal. Still, the findings were consistent with a 2019 study that found the DASH diet can help lower the risk of heart disease.

In addition, a 2020 study reported that adults aged 65 and older with obesity lost body fat while following the DASH Diet. A 2023 study suggested the diet can help reduce belly fat.

A 2019 meta-analysis suggested that the DASH diet may also help lower the risk of certain cancers.

Many of these protective effects are attributed to the diet’s high fruit and vegetable content.

Chen said the DASH diet has many benefits, but the eating plan might not be for everybody.

“The DASH diet has been shown to be beneficial in lowering blood pressure, losing weight, and controlling blood sugar and cholesterol levels,” he said. “However, the amount of planning and portion tracking required to follow the diet may be too much for some people.”

Chen recommended that people ease into the DASH diet.

“The best way to start on the DASH diet is to slowly introduce the changes to your meals,” he said. “This can be as simple as including a few extra servings of fruits and vegetables each day as well as swapping some whole grain products for refined grains. Over time, more of the diet can be incorporated.”

Volpp agreed that introducing the DASH diet in increments is a good strategy.

“For many people, it is easiest to make small incremental changes sequentially,” he said. “One way to get started is to increase fruit by a couple of servings a day. Another is to pay attention to the amount of sodium in packaged foods and consciously choose products lower in sodium. A third is to make a conscious effort to eat more vegetables.”

“Make changes first that are easiest for you to make; it will be easier to make those changes stick,” Volpp added.

#4741. Maserati Is Eyeing Big Racing Opportunities, But Nothing Is Inevitable

The company’s racing plans are focused on GT2 and GT4 racing right now. Brand leadership is considering bigger opportunities a little later down the road.

For its first half-century, Maserati was one of the biggest names in motorsport. The Italian brand has been far less relevant on that stage in the six decades that have followed, but that has begun to change with new lower-level GT racing programs. That said, Maserati still does not race in the global standard GT3 class, does not compete in prototype racing, and has no known plans to race anywhere above the GT2 and GT4 categories.

In a conversation with Road & Track, global chief marketing officer Cristiano Fiorio explained that the brand is absent from GT3 racing in part because of its shared ownership with Ferrari. While Maserati parent company Stellantis and Ferrari now are different entities, the two are still both chaired by John Elkann and Fiorio notes “the large family” around Maserati already competes in the GT3 class with a Ferrari 296 variant.

Instead of running a GT3 car, a version of the MCPura supercar races in the more amateur-focused GT2 category. Fiorio says that the GT2 class “is good, but it didn’t skyrocket as we all expected,” leaving the mid-engined Maserati with limited relevancy in televised sports car racing. That means the GT2 program has not had the cultural impact of a GT3 operation, even if it did lead the company to produce the GT2 Stradale road car. While a GT3-ready MCPura is not on the docket, the company is expanding with a previously-announced plan to bring the GranTurismo into the widely-adopted GT4 category. There, it will compete against road-going competition like the Aston Martin Vantage and Porsche 911.

Head of Engineering Davide Danesin tells R&T that the GranTurismo was chosen over the MCPura for GT4 racing in part because its aluminum body was a better fit for the “spirit of the regulations” than the carbon fiber monocoque construction utilized on the supercar. It should prove more affordable to run, too.

“The GranTurismo,” Danesin adds, “with an aluminum body and already sporty design from the beginning, with few modifications, we are able to take it racing in GT4. This is also very convenient for the customer, because you can imagine... You’re breaking a lot of parts during racing, you can take spare parts from the production cars, which would be a lot cheaper than what we take from the carbon fiber car.”

The GT4-spec GranTurismo will be rear-wheel drive, which Fiorio notes is a new avenue for development of the all-wheel drive road car. Given that the brand’s GT2 car has already produced a specialized road car, work on that concept for racing could open the door for a rear-wheel drive GranTurismo with GT4 branding on the road.

Outside of GT racing, Maserati’s history is largely in single-seaters. Maserati briefly revived that idea with branding on a Stellantis Formula E team, but Fiorio says that this was done in “another time when everyone was talking about electrification, [including] at Maserati.” He calls that team, re-branded as a Citroën program last year, a “closed chapter.” He suggested that a move to IndyCar is not something that the company is seriously considering either, and that even grander options, like a return to F1, “are dreams, but that’s about dreams, not reality.”

That leaves prototype racing as Maserati’s most likely avenue for expansion. This is even more complicated for the brand, as both Ferrari and Stellantis sister brand Peugeot run an LMH-spec car in the FIA World Endurance Championship, but Fiorio does suggest that this is something the company could consider in the medium term. Entry in a championship like the U.S.-based IMSA, which currently features neither the Ferrari 499 P nor the Peugeot 9X8, would make sense, but he wants the company to right its road car operations first.

“Regarding particularly in the U.S.,” he says, “if you decide to do that step, I think that you have to have fixed all the other points in the company. Before getting to the sport, we need to give the brand the possibility to go back and launch what we made, and once we are running at a good place with production and we are selling, then we should consider [prototype racing]. I think it would not be appropriate in the moment. We are still with two years in front of us which are not going to be the easiest years, because we have to wait ... I don’t know how much, let’s say a couple of years to have the E-segment [products like the rumored return of the Quattroporte] or whatever it will be. Then once we have them on the ground and the company is performing well, then you can think about another category that is totally different in terms of budgets, expenses, development, et cetera.”

While nothing here is anything close to set in stone, the timeline does line up with the introduction of a successor to the current LMDh and LMH rule set at Le Mans in 2030. With the struggling Peugeot 9X8 likely to be out of competition by then, it opens the door for Maserati to potentially replace its French relatives as the Stellantis representative alongside Ferrari at Le Mans. If that door does not open, the brand could still run its own racing program in the U.S. as an IMSA GTP operation.

These are all still just options in front of Maserati, but they could plausibly happen in the years to come. And if the company does go prototype racing, it will be the most ambitious Maserati racing program since the brand fielded a championship-winning Formula 1 car for Juan Manuel Fangio in 1967.

Fred Smith's love of cars comes from his fascination with auto racing. Unfortunately, that passion led him to daily drive a high-mileage, first-year Porsche Panamera. He is still thinking about the last lap of the 2011 Indianapolis 500.

#4739. NHTSA’s Changing the Definition of a ‘Truck,’ Possibly Encouraging More Overseas Production

Starting in 2030, the U.S. will have way more cars than trucks (technically). It’s the other way around now.

We’ve all known that the way the government classifies trucks and cars has been a bit of a moving target, so NHTSA is finally forcing trucks to actually do truck things.

The changes—which would reclassify today’s 70/30 truck-to-car ratio into a 30/70 situation—were made as part of the government’s loosening of MPG rules.

But the different rules for cars and trucks could mean that it will make sense to build a vehicle overseas and import it, given different rules for the domestic and imported fleets.

See all results for used cars for sale near 10330

Some questions are difficult to answer. Is a hot dog a sandwich? Do crabs think fish can fly? Is a light truck a passenger car?

Whatever your responses are to the first two, the government made it clear that its legal answer to that last one will soon change. The announcement came in the Safer Affordable Fuel-Efficient (SAFE) Vehicles Rule III for Model Years 2022 to 2031 Passenger Cars and Light Trucks, which was published in the Federal Register at the end of September and goes into effect on November 30. While the change was made in the context of the Trump administration’s loosening of fuel-economy regulations, it might also force automakers to move some production outside the U.S., depending on the political winds.

The latest SAFE Rule updates fuel-economy standards for model years 2022 through 2031. Under the Biden administration, the standard had been set to a U.S. fleet average of around 50.4 mpg, but the new rules lower that to 34.9 mpg. Some of this change comes from new rules about what defines a car and what makes something a truck.

Since federal fuel-economy rules were established in the 1970s, the government has divided noncommercial road vehicles into two categories of light-duty vehicles, passenger cars (legally: passenger automobiles) and light trucks (non-passenger automobiles). How the government technically defines these vehicles has been a cat-and-mouse game between regulators and engineers. The SAFE Rule says that the back-and-forth led “manufacturers to reshape the market in unanticipated ways—such as by almost eliminating the production of station wagons (passenger cars that generally have more robust cargo capacity, adding mass and reducing fuel economy) in favor of vehicles like minivans and crossover utility vehicles (considered light trucks, and subject to less stringent standards).”

See all results for used cars for sale near 10330

Today, the rule says, “The classification system that has long divided the fleet between passenger cars (intended to move passengers) and light trucks (intended to move cargo or operate off highway) no longer lives up to its anticipated use.” It states that while 68 percent of the light-duty fleet technically meets the regulatory definition of a light truck, the majority of them “cannot realistically operate off-highway and have little value moving cargo. Instead, most of these vehicles are designed and intended primarily to move passengers but have additional features solely to meet regulatory definitions —resulting in little added functionality, reduced fuel-economy performance, added cost, and a homogenous design lacking in creativity.”

Under the updated regulatory definitions, which will go into effect for model year 2030 vehicles, NHTSA said it expects trucks to be trucks and cars to, well, not be trucks. Light-duty non-passenger vehicles will “exhibit true non-passenger capabilities that display relevant off-highway vehicle attributes such as approach angle and running clearance or include design features that provide higher payload and towing abilities for transporting cargo,” the rule states. The agency expects the current fleet mix of 70 percent light trucks and 30 percent passenger cars would be classified as 70 percent cars and 30 percent trucks under the new rules.

A vehicle’s category matters from the production side, which is where unintended side effects start to crop up. As noted by Automotive News, a “truck” today that has lower fuel-economy numbers than a car won’t have to change to meet the new, lowered fuel-economy standards imposed by the Trump administration. But if a future administration revives Biden’s 50-mpg target or otherwise raises the standards, these vehicles will need a lot of engineering effort to be compliant because they’re now considered “cars.”

One alternative that automakers might choose in that situation is to import more vehicles through recontenting. That’s because while domestic passenger vehicles need to be produced to meet a CAFE standard minimum, imported passenger vehicles do not. A company’s overall average target remains the same, but since the rules allow automakers to move credits between imported and domestic fleets, there could be benefits to building outside the U.S.

Tom Stricker, senior policy adviser at Van Ness Feldman, told Automotive News that if an automaker were to offshore part of the part supply chain or work for a model in order to get it reclassified as an import vehicle, “the net environmental impact and fuel consumption impact has not changed one bit; all you’ve done is potentially, in this scenario, offshored certain production.”

Sebastian Blanco has been writing about electric vehicles, hybrids, and hydrogen cars since 2006. His articles and car reviews have appeared in the New York Times, Automotive News, Reuters, SAE, Autoblog, InsideEVs, Trucks.com, Car Talk, and other outlets. His first green-car media event was the launch of the Tesla Roadster, and since then he has been tracking the shift away from gasoline-powered vehicles and discovering the new technology's importance not just for the auto industry, but for the world as a whole. Throw in the recent shift to autonomous vehicles, and there are more interesting changes happening now than most people can wrap their heads around. You can find him on Twitter or, on good days, behind the wheel of a new EV.

#4738. How to Winterize a Garden: Ready Your Plants to Weather the Cold With These Expert Tips

As fall winds down, you’ll pull sweaters out of storage and crank the thermostat, but what about your outdoor plants? Knowing how to winterize a garden is key to welcoming spring with an abundance of thriving greenery—as opposed to a sad brown patch of lawn. If frost, snow, rain, and freezing temperatures are in the forecast, know that your outdoor spaces don’t have to face the elements without a little assistance.

To prepare your garden for winter, you’ll need burlap; straw; garden tools like pruning shears and a pruning saw or lopper; a compost pile or ready source of nutrient-rich matter; and, ideally, some winter-ready plants that can take the place of flowering annuals and edible plants.

One more item to add to your list (in potentially large quantities): mulch. “One of the biggest things we do is mulch, and then mulch some more,” says Christina Stembel, founder and CEO of D2C e-commerce flower company Farmgirl Flowers. “We bring in what feels like an absurd amount of mulch every fall and use it just about everywhere. It helps insulate roots, suppress weeds, protect the soil through rain [and snow], and eventually adds organic matter back into the ground. We mulch our garden roses, peonies, perennial beds, [and more].”

Amy Enfield, who has more than 30 years of experience in the lawn- and garden-care industry and is currently a senior horticulturalist at Miracle-Gro, offers thoughts on a mindful first step ahead of digging in: “Before pulling out tools or buying materials, walk your garden and take stock of what you have. Note which plants are annuals that will not return, which are perennials you want to protect, where the soil looks tired or compacted, and which areas might benefit from a cover crop over winter. This assessment keeps you from doing more work than necessary or missing something important.”

Burlap adds a layer of protection against the elements for this outdoor plant in the wintertime.

“Do not begin with applying mulch,” says Jose Lopez, the founder and owner of Williams Landscaping Inc. “First, walk through your fall garden to pull up dead foliage. Pull any foliage that shows signs of disease as well. Next, apply appropriate care to the plants that will remain. Finally, cover your soil. If you do not complete these first three steps, you’re simply burying the problems of the growing season.” To cover that soil, there are three primary materials, each of which plays a different role, Lopez says. “Compost supplies nutrients to the soil. Mulch serves as insulation [and you can] use the leaves that are readily available, shredding them for mulch. Straw has no place in most flowerbeds but is suitable for vegetable gardening.” Just be mindful of where you are placing covering materials, and “do not layer large amounts of wet leaves over a newly planted shrub because the water can cause root rot at its base,” Lopez notes.

Raised garden beds tend to be used for annual flowers and edible plants, and these typically need to be removed, says Dr. Enfield. “Leaving their debris in the garden over winter creates a cozy habitat for overwintering pests and fungal disease.” Pull any spent annuals and finished vegetable plants, disposing of or composting them. “Diseased plant material should go in the trash rather than the compost pile,” Dr. Enfield says.

Once the beds are cleared, Dr. Enfield says to “amend the soil before it freezes. Working organic matter, like compost, composted manure, or a bagged soil into garden beds in fall gives it the entire winter to work into the soil structure, so it is ready to support planting in spring. Spread three inches over cleared beds and work it into the top six inches of native soil before winter sets in.”

Some plants are tough enough to make it through the winter without much support, but others can benefit from some TLC in the autumn. For those plants that aren’t hardy enough to withstand winter in your climate, you’ll need to break out some of the aforementioned materials: burlap, straw, frost cloth, and horticultural fleece. Amanda Webb, head gardener at Windy Hill Farm and Preserve, notes that “burlap is particularly useful for protecting exposed evergreens from winter wind, while straw can provide insulation around the crowns of marginally hardy perennials. I prefer breathable materials over plastic, which can trap moisture and create problems when temperatures fluctuate.”

Shrubs and other borderline hardy plants also need to take cover from the cold. “Some plants, particularly broadleaf evergreens like rhododendrons, hollies, and boxwoods, as well as any shrubs or perennials that are on the edge of their cold hardiness zone, benefit from physical protection over winter,” says Dr. Enfield. Burlap is the most commonly used material for this purpose; it can be wrapped loosely around individual plants or used to construct a simple windscreen that protects evergreens from the desiccating winter winds and sun, which can cause browning and leaf scorch even in plants that are otherwise cold-hardy. “Avoid wrapping plants in plastic, which traps moisture and heat and can do more harm than good. Burlap breathes, which is what makes it the material of choice.” For young or newly planted trees, wrapping the trunk with tree wrap or burlap protects against sun scald, “which is the cracking of bark caused by rapid temperature changes on the south and southwest sides of the trunk during winter thaws.”

Frost protection will help shield these potted plants from freezing temperatures.

Gardening doesn’t have to stop in winter; you just have to focus on plants that thrive in the cold. “I can’t stand seeing a vegetable bed sitting bare all winter,” says Mirela Bajic, senior garden designer at House Designer. “If you’re not growing anything in it, put something in for the soil’s sake. Winter rye is brilliant for that, [as] the roots hold everything together through the bad weather and then you just cut it back come spring.” She adds that many plants are more than rugged enough to make it through frosty, even snowy seasons. “Kale, cabbage, leeks, chard, [and more] all keep going through the cold if you let them.” Winter-ready crops like these help hold the soil together, provide some lovely greenery amid the gray backdrop of wintertime, and can even be used in hearty cold-weather meals.

It’s imperative that you tend to your property’s sprinkler systems and hoses, says Webb. “Drain irrigation systems and hoses if freezing temperatures are expected,” she says, or you risk catastrophic damage once water in the lines freezes and expands.

First, you’ll need to shut off the water supply to your sprinklers and hoses, removing the latter and putting them away for winter. Then, blow out sprinkler lines using compressed air to expel any residual water from the pipes. You should use a compressor set to 80 PSI or lower to avoid damaging sprinkler heads in the process. Once the lines are clear, leave the water shut off at the source inside your home (or garage or greenhouse or wherever the main water connection point is found) while leaving external valves partially open so last drips can fall and trapped water has a place to expand if it freezes.

Just because it’s too cold outside to grow colorful flowers or tasty tomatoes, peppers, or herbs doesn’t mean you can’t keep the growing going on indoors, and no dedicated greenhouse is needed, either. With a self-watering hydroponic growing system like a Gardyn Studio 2, you can grow full-sized plants indoors, including the same flowers and edible plants you’d place in a raised garden bed outdoors. If indoor space is at a premium, you can also use a smaller countertop system like a LetPot that can be used with a smaller number of flowers or food plants.

All those leaves you painstakingly rake or blow into a pile need not be bagged up and sent off to the dump; you can recycle it all to make your own mulch, which can preserve and nurture your garden throughout the winter. It’s best to use a leaf shredder to break down fallen leaves into smaller bits, rather than simply spreading collected leaves out atop your beds. You can also use a lawn mower to break down leaves, though this will dull the blade with extended use. If you have a wood chipper, you can use branches and brambles to make mulch as well. Even grass clippings can make great mulch, provided you dry it in the sun first and mix it with some thicker material, like those shredded leaves or chipped branches.

You don’t have to remove every dead flower, be it a truly finished annual or a dormant perennial, says Stembel, of Farmgirl Flowers. “One thing I’m trying to get better at is not cleaning everything up just because it looks messy. There are flowers that went from stunning to eyesores everywhere, seed heads, and so many perennials that are better off being left alone for a while so the foliage can die back naturally and the plants can store energy for next year. A lot of those seed heads and stems also feed birds and give beneficial insects places to overwinter.” Sometimes, the best thing you can do to winterize your garden is to leave it alone.

You should plant bulbs in the autumn when soil temperatures are consistently holding at around 55 to 60 degrees Fahrenheit, and about six weeks before you expect the first frost. “[It’s] my absolute favorite fall ‘chore,’ planting the bulbs,” says Stembel. “This fall we’re planting thousands of daffodils, tulips, and drumstick alliums around the farm. I know thousands sounds excessive [but] bulbs might be my favorite kind of delayed gratification. By spring, I’ll mostly have forgotten that we trudged outside on cold, rainy fall days and dug what felt like endless trenches to plant all of them until the little green sprouts start popping through the soil. Those first flowers bring us so much joy right when we need it most [after] months of cold, gray weather. In fall, I wonder why we planted so many, but by spring I’ll wonder why we didn’t plant more.”

#4737. Nasdaq-100 comes onchain as Janus Henderson and Centrifuge launch first licensed fund

Janus Henderson, a London-based asset manager overseeing roughly $500 billion, is bringing one of Wall Street’s best-known stock indexes onto blockchain rails through an issuer-sponsored fund. The launch comes amid an escalating debate over who gets to tokenize traditional securities.

The firm teamed up with tokenization platform Centrifuge to launch JQQQ, a fund tracking the Nasdaq-100 Index, home to tech giants including Nvidia, Apple and Microsoft. The fund is issued on the Stellar XLM$0.1853 blockchain, the companies said Tuesday.

JQQQ follows the introduction of a similar onchain index fund product last year, the Janus Henderson Anemoy S&P 500 Fund (SPXA), which was the first tokenized index fund licensed by the S&P Dow Jones Indices.

Janus Henderson said its onchain investment platform now includes more than $1 billion across Treasuries, structured credit and equities.

The launch marks another step in Wall Street’s push to bring traditional investments onchain, expanding beyond U.S. Treasuries, money market funds and private credit into stock-market products. It also highlights a growing divide between funds issued onchain by their managers and third-party tokens created without the underlying companies’ involvement.

JQQQ follows the issuer-sponsored model. Janus Henderson issues and manages the fund, Centrifuge provides the blockchain infrastructure and Nasdaq licenses the index to track.

That differs from third-party stock tokens, which may be backed by shares held in custody or structured as separate instruments tracking stock prices. Investors in those products don't necessarily own the underlying shares or receive shareholder rights.

The distinction has become central in the debate around tokenization efforts. Most recently, 22 companies objected to plans for blockchain-based trading of their shares on the OKX-ICE venue, adding to a debate over whether issuers should have a say when their stocks are tokenized.

“As investors explore new ways to access markets through digital infrastructure, demand continues to grow for exposure to established, transparent benchmarks,” said Sean Wasserman, head of index product management at Nasdaq.

The fund also shows how tokenization is moving beyond digital versions of existing investments toward products built to operate on blockchains from the start.

Such funds could eventually allow investors to use their holdings as collateral for loans or move them between blockchain-based financial applications, subject to regulatory requirements and platform support.

"Tokenization becomes much more powerful when the assets investors want to own can also become building blocks for an onchain financial system," said Anil Sood, chief strategy and growth officer at Centrifuge.

#4736. Bat mothers’ hearts beat faster when they hear their pups

Published Oct 23, 2026 12:00 AM EDT

A single bat colony contains hundreds or even thousands of the winged mammals. But with less-than-ideal eyesight and so many neighbors, how do mothers identify their pups amid the extremely crowded conditions? Writing in a new study published today in the journal Frontiers in Ethology, researchers say it often comes down to the subtle, unique vocalizations emitted by their individual offspring. To assess their responses, the team relied on an even more delicate biological signal—the mother bat’s own heartbeat.

The investigation began when biologists at Japan’s Doshisha University noticed a surprising—but consistent—behavior among the Japanese house bats (Alionoctula abramus) in their laboratory colony. Whenever a pup wandered too far from its roosting area, only its mother raced to the rescue.

“We knew that pups emit isolation calls—acoustic distress signals—when separated. But seeing this strict behavioral selectivity made us wonder: what is happening inside the mother when she hears these calls?” recalled study co-author and Doshisha University biologist Kazuki Yoshino-Hashizawa.

To learn more, Yoshino-Hashizawa and his colleagues monitored four mother bats who each recently birthed a litter of three pups. Some of the litters only contained females, so they decided to exclude the young males from the study to avoid sex-based variations. At two days old, the team recorded clips of the female pups’ tiny isolation calls. These function like the cries from a human baby, and each vocalization has its own unique tones.

However, Japanese house bats grow quickly. The new generation started making echolocation calls at about eight days old, and they ceased isolation calls after around 12 days. After roughly one month, they began flying like adults and set off on their own within the colony. The researchers then recorded their adult echolocation calls, as well.

Around 40 days after the pups’ birth, the team isolated each mother and played them the various audio clips while monitoring their heart rates using an ECG machine. As a control, they also played the recording of a random adult male bat. The study’s authors quickly saw that a mother bat’s heart rate sped up when they heard cries of their own pups, but not the pups that belong to other bat moms. Surprisingly, the mother bats’ heart rates boosted the most from recordings of the older, independent pups. Researchers theorize this might relate to the bats’ memory of more recent calls, but that idea requires more study to confirm.

Still, don’t get overly sentimental. The team also recorded strong heart rate reactions when the mothers heard calls from the random adult male. It’s possible the bats interpreted the sounds as a threat, but the team stopped short of categorizing the reactions as good or bad.

“‘[Heart rate measurement] cannot distinguish between positive emotions, such as maternal motivation, and negative ones, such as stress or alarm,” Yoshino-Hashizawa added.

Instead, the study authors suggest heart rates may be a solid starting point when trying to analyze bat behaviors and responses to their everyday environment. At the same time, a mother bat’s heart certainly seems to patter a bit more for her pups.

“[This] suggests that these vocalizations instantly physiologically prime the mother to engage in maternal behavior,” explained Yoshino-Hashizawa.

#4735. The latest Arc Raiders patch is almost entirely about fixing exploits, including all the clipping through locked rooms business

Embark is clearly sick of Arc Raiders players exploiting the game's mechanics to push each other around, and clip through doors and walls.

A fresh Arc Raiders patch has arrived, bringing a host of new fixes to several exploits players have recently been relying on to do things they’re not supposed to, and bypass certain mechanics to get into areas without having to go through proper channels.

The patch is fairly small, and surprisingly doesn’t include any buffs or nerfs to anything in particular.

The list of notes for update 1.4.0 is compact, with a clear focus on addressing several exploits. The biggest one, of course, is the addition of “exploit mitigation mechanisms” to stop players from glitching through locked doors.

This fix applies to all maps, and it’s intended to address an issue whereby players would not bring the required keys to access those areas of the various maps. Locked rooms, if they can be unlocked, often hide a wealth of high-tier and rare loot.

But players have also been using similar exploits to clip through walls and access areas outside the playspace, allowing them to ambush enemy players and set up traps that would be impossible for others to detect.

The game’s recent patch, which was released exactly one week ago, mainly consisted of balance tweaks to some of the more popular weapons and mines. However, it also introduced a few fixes to prevent players from clipping through walls by using barricades.

While on the subject of being in places where you’re not supposed to, the patch blocked exterior access to the locked room inside Spaceport's Control Tower, making it only accessible from inside the tower.

#4734. Cyberpunk 2077 movie "won't be a one-to-one translation of the game's story", CD Projekt Red boss confirms, and boy do I think that's the right move

A more Edgerunners-esque original tale from Night City looks to be in the offing

Since the existence of plans for a Cyberpunk 2077 live-action adaptation from devs CD Projekt Red and Paramount Pictures was revealed last week, I've been hoping it won't take the form of porting V's tale from the game to the big screen. Such a thing wouldn't necessarily be terrible, but I like my game adaptations a bit more on the original story side of things, as Cyberpunk tie-in anime series Edgerunners has been. The good news is that one of CD Projekt Red's big bosses has now confirmed that this adaptation won't be beat-for-beat retelling of 'Honey, there's a Keanu in my brain'.

There is a caveat in that the interview in question with CDPR joint-CEO Michał Nowakowski has been conducted and printed in Polish by business site PAP Biznes. A machine translation of the article suggests the exec said the following about the Cyberpunk movie:

"Our contract with Anonymous Content expired, and in the meantime, an opportunity arose to establish cooperation with Paramount Pictures, and we decided that we would conduct a live-action Cyberpunk project in a new formula. At this point, we can only say that we definitely don't want to make a film adaptation of the game. We want to tell a story in the world of Cyberpunk, but it won't be a one-to-one translation of the game's story."

Wary of relying solely on machine-translated quotes given they can result in meanings being twisted or made misinterpretable, we've run the quote by a Polish contact in order to try and root out any potential muddling of Nowakowski's words by the auto-translation. I've also reached out to CD Projekt Red for comment.

In a previous statement at the time the movie plans we confirmed, all Nowakowski said was that the studio are "excited about Paramount as a future partner who shares our creative ambitions for the Cyberpunk franchise" and would share more info on the film "at the appropriate time".

Assuming the movie does go the Edgerunners-esque route of telling a Night City story separate from the game's tale, that's definitely the right move in my eyes. Not only would that mean its creators don't have to agonise over how to make the pacing of an open-world game mesh with a film's runtime and could avoid having to decide how square messing with players' preferred headcanons about what V did or didn't do at various points, but it gives the creatives behind the film a chance to offer a fresher tale that perhaps explores some elements of the Cyberpunk world that the game didn't give as much attention to.

Then again, to avoid earning my ire, you've also got to avoid falling into the Fallout Season 2 trap of telling a fresh tale, but being desperate to hammer as many references to a game in as possible without conclusively advancing its setting in a way that makes the events of said game feel as meaningful it should. Basically, I'm saying I might be a bit too picky to be the ideal person to be trying to please with a game adaptation for the big screen.

Mac, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Stadia, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

Mac, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

Rock Paper Shotgun is better when you sign in
