#4733. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: Lung Cancer Can Look Like Anyone

  • Up to 1 in 5 lung cancer cases in the United States occur in people who’ve never smoked, but our picture of the disease hasn’t caught up.
  • Healthline Editor-in-Chief Robin Hough talks with Gini Harrison, a psychologist and his friend of nearly 30 years, whose only symptom was a sore shoulder blamed on breastfeeding. Her stage 4 EGFR-positive lung cancer diagnosis took nearly nine months.
  • They discuss testing, catching lung cancer sooner, and the documentary film, Our Lungs: Time to Breathe, which follows filmmaker Sarah Li and the family of Alex, a 24-year-old who died weeks after diagnosis.

Picture someone with lung cancer. Now picture a 24-year-old soccer player in the prime of his life. A filmmaker in her early 40s with a camera in her hand, doing what she loves best. A busy mom of two, with a shoulder so sore she can’t pick up her newborn, yet struggling to get a diagnosis. 

The gap between the picture in our heads and the people actually being diagnosed is costing time. And time is the one thing with lung cancer you cannot get back. 

We know that lung cancer is the most common cancer worldwide and the leading cause of cancer death. And while lung cancer incidence rates are falling in many countries as fewer people smoke, lung cancer in people who have never smoked is on the rise globally, both as a proportion and in absolute numbers. 

According to the CDC, as many as 20% of lung cancers diagnosed in the US are in people who have never smoked. Data suggests that non-smoking lung cancer is now among the most common causes of cancer-related deaths worldwide.

The numbers are stark. Lung cancer can affect anyone. And that includes the young – like Alex, one of the subjects of a powerful new short documentary commissioned by the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK for their Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign. 

Alex was a 24-year-old soccer player who died just weeks after receiving a diagnosis of Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) positive lung cancer, following several misdiagnoses born of the fact that no one suspected lung cancer in one so young, fit, and with no smoking history. The film was made by filmmaker Sarah Li, who herself was diagnosed with Stage 4 EGFR+ lung cancer in April 2024, just a month after Alex died from the same mutation of the disease, and charts her journey with survivor’s guilt, what an earlier diagnosis may have meant for Alex, and what that fact means for her own future. 

Ahead of Lung Cancer Awareness Month in November, I sat down with my friend, Gini Harrison, to talk about the film, her own journey, and why catching lung cancer sooner matters so much. Gini is a Professor of Psychology at The Open University, a trustee of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK, and is living with stage 4 EGFR-positive lung cancer. She’s also the mom at the top of this piece, whose sore shoulder was initially attributed to breastfeeding posture.

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity and length.

EGFR-positive lung cancer: Warning signs

Take me back to the beginning. What were your initial symptoms, and what were you told?

Gini Harrison: I’d had my son, Michael, in February 2021 and was on maternity leave. My daughter, Emily, was five. I think the shoulder pain started around March, but that was it. No cough, no breathlessness… nothing that made me think about my lungs.

Initially, they gave me painkillers, which helped a bit. But the pain kept getting worse. Eventually, I started losing movement and strength in my arm, and it got to the point where I couldn’t pick up my son anymore.

This was all happening during COVID, so my appointments were over the telephone. Eventually, after months without being examined face-to-face, I paid to see a shoulder specialist. As soon as he touched my shoulder blade and I practically leaped out of the chair, he knew something wasn’t right. He ordered an MRI, which showed a tumor in my shoulder blade and another in my upper right lung. In December 2021, I was diagnosed with stage IV lung cancer.

What do you remember about that period, and how did it feel to be reassured when your instinct said otherwise?

Gini Harrison: I wasn’t thinking, “This must be cancer.” I was just thinking, “Why isn’t this getting better?”

And I think that distinction matters. Patients shouldn’t have to identify their own disease before they can get help. You should be able to say that something is wrong and be listened to.

What went through your head when you heard “lung cancer”? Did you have any of the assumptions you now push back against?

Gini Harrison: It was devastating. So many of my first questions were about the children: “How are we going to tell them?” “Am I going to be here to see them grow up?” “Will they remember me?” Suddenly, there was a question mark over a future I’d taken for granted.

I was also completely shocked by the type of cancer. If someone had said breast cancer, I don’t think I would have been anywhere near as surprised. But lung cancer?! I’d always associated it with smoking, and I’d never smoked.

I hadn’t imagined that this particular cancer could ever happen to me. So yes, I had some assumptions that I now challenge. I understand how deeply rooted they are because I had them too.

What lung cancer ‘looks like’

When people learn you have lung cancer, what do they say? What’s the first question you get asked?

Gini Harrison: People are often shocked, and one of the first questions is whether I smoked.

And they often look healthy. That doesn’t mean everything is easy, or that there aren’t side effects. It just means you can’t tell what someone is dealing with by looking at them.

I think we’ve given lung cancer such a narrow public image that people outside it can become almost invisible.

Has anyone ever asked if you smoked? How do you answer, and how should it land?

Gini Harrison: Oh, yes! Once, I spoke to a doctor about a breathing problem after radiotherapy. He asked, “How much did you smoke?” When I said I’d never smoked, he replied, “Well, how did you get lung cancer then?”

On another occasion, I went to a hospice for help managing side effects, and someone there asked, “How are you still alive?” That’s quite a thing to hear when you’ve come looking for support!

When people ask about smoking, I try to explain that I’ve never smoked and I’m certainly not alone. Never smoking doesn’t make you immune. But I’m also very clear that nobody deserves lung cancer, whether they’ve smoked or not. We shouldn’t be creating a hierarchy of who deserves compassion.

Smoking history matters medically. But in a personal conversation, “How are you doing?” is a much kinder place to start.

Genetics and biomarker testing 

Explain biomarker or genetic testing as if I’m a friend (which I am!) who’s never heard of it. Why does it matter?

Gini Harrison: Lung cancer isn’t one single disease. Two people can both have lung cancer, but different changes in their cells may be driving it.

Genes contain the instructions that tell our cells how to work, including when to grow and divide. Changes in those genes (called mutations) can cause cells to keep dividing when they shouldn’t, forming a tumor. In my case, there’s a mutation in a gene called EGFR.

EGFR-positive lung cancer is a type of non-small cell lung cancer caused by a genetic mutation in the EGFR gene, which keeps the EGFR receptor switched “on,” leading to uncontrolled cell growth and enhanced cancer cell survival.

Biomarker testing looks for these changes, as well as other features of the cancer that can guide treatment. If we know what’s helping the cancer grow, there may be a medication that specifically targets it.

The word “genetic” can sound scary, particularly when you have children. But these changes usually develop in cancer cells over a person’s lifetime. They aren’t necessarily inherited.

Why early diagnosis is so important 

What would you say to someone reading this who has a symptom that won’t go away?

Gini Harrison: Please get it checked. And if it persists, worsens or doesn’t improve with treatment, go back. Explain what’s changed and how it’s affecting your life. Ask, “What happens next if this doesn’t get better?”

The symptoms don’t always look like lung symptoms. Mine was shoulder pain.

Most symptoms will turn out to be something other than cancer. But when a symptom continues to worsen, the original explanation needs to be reconsidered. Lower risk doesn’t mean no risk.

And I don’t want all the responsibility to fall on patients to keep pushing. Reassurance should come with a next step.

Our Lungs: Time to Breathe – Alex and Sarah’s stories 

How did you get involved in advocacy, and what was the hardest thing about being part of it?

Gini Harrison: Once I had recovered from my initial treatment, I realized that I wanted to help others in my situation, so I became a trustee of the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK. Through this work, we produced a film, Our Lungs: Time to Breathe. At the heart of it, it challenges the perception of what someone with lung cancer looks like.

The hardest part is how close to home it feels. Alex was so young, and that worked against him. Watching his family talk about what happened is heartbreaking. It’s just so unfair.

What did you hope people would see in Sarah’s and Alex’s stories?

Gini Harrison: Sarah’s story shows life continuing alongside lung cancer. She’s a filmmaker with ambitions, people she loves, and things she wants to do.

Through Alex’s family, you see the enormity of the loss. I hope people feel that, rather than simply hearing another statistic.

Lung cancer and the bigger picture

What’s changed since you were diagnosed, and what hasn’t?

Gini Harrison: For me, the biggest thing is that my children are growing up and I’m here to see it. Michael is now five (the age Emily was when I was diagnosed), and Emily is about to turn ten. I can’t tell you how grateful I am to be here with them.

It wasn’t about big bucket-list things. It was about being here every day. Seeing Michael start school. Seeing Emily reach double figures. Those milestones are enormous when you’ve wondered whether you’d get to see them.

After chemotherapy and radiotherapy, I’ve had no evidence of disease since May 2022, and my scans remain stable. But a big change has been seeing the progress in available treatment. It gives me more hope about what might be available if I need treatment again.

The public image of lung cancer still needs to catch up. We need better recognition of symptoms, access to appropriate testing and treatment, and support for the whole person. As a psychologist as well as a patient, I’m particularly aware that psychological well-being and family life need attention alongside the cancer itself.

What do you want people to do after reading this?

Gini Harrison: I’d love for people to share the film and help get it in front of those who wouldn’t normally think lung cancer has anything to do with them. And take something from it into everyday life: book the appointment you’ve been putting off, ask your doctor about testing, or respond with kindness when someone tells you about their diagnosis.

Just being willing to rethink your assumptions could make a difference.

What do you wish more people would say to someone in your position?

Gini Harrison: “You don’t have to put a brave face on with me. How are you really doing?”

When you look well, or your scans are good, people can assume you’re “fine now”. But I’m not sure the fear ever completely goes away. Every scan is terrifying. It feels like there’s a sword of Damocles hanging over my head, and part of me is waiting for the bad scan. I also still have lasting side effects from treatment, and probably always will.

My life has fundamentally changed. Some of that has brought good things, but it doesn’t make the difficult parts disappear. It helps when someone gives you room to be honest about both, without expecting you to reassure them or turn everything into a positive story.

Gini and I have been friends for nearly 30 years, and, while she’d probably cringe to hear me say it, I remain in awe of the way she approaches life with cancer. I am so grateful for her sharing her experiences with me for this column. What Gini asks of us is simple: look again, ask the question, listen, and act on your instincts. Our Lungs: Time to Breathe is where that starts for her. You can learn more about the Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign and the wider work of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK by clicking the links. — Robin Hough

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#4732. At Scotland’s ancient Cladh Hallan, homes were full of human bones

Published Oct 23, 2026 8:56 AM EDT

In 2001, archaeologists were painstakingly excavating a dwelling at the Bronze-Age site of Cladh Hallan in  the Outer Hebrides islands off of Scotland’s northwestern coast. The house had been oval in shape, with a low door leading to an interior hearth. Other conveniences included sunken storage areas and stone-lined water storage tanks. Under the floors of these houses, though, and under the feet of the occupants, was a fascinating feature that evokes the opening scenes of a classic horror film.

As the archaeologists slowly dug through the floor of the structure, they came unexpectedly upon a human skeleton, legs tucked up in the fetal position. Another skeleton, similarly arranged, was found beneath a different roundhouse’s floor. These people had clearly been laid to rest with care, with stones laid out around them.

When archaeologists began to study the remains, though, they were staggered to learn that each body’s parts had not all come from the same person. These were composite mummies, assembled with the remains of at least six people who had died over the span of two hundred years. But this wasn’t a jumbled-together macabre collection or evidence of a Bronze-Age serial killer. These bodies had been treated carefully and buried in stone-lined graves. So what were the people of Cladh Hallan doing with the dug-up remains of their ancestors?

More than four thousand years ago, early farmers began to work the soil on the Outer Hebrides islands off of Scotland’s northwestern coast. At the time, the Roman Empire and even ancient Greece were specks on the chronological horizon. Along the Aegean coast, Minoans and Mycenaeans were building palaces and cities. Farther north, Germanic tribes were exporting amber and expertly forging bronze and gold artifacts.

At Cladh Hallan, early farmers grew barley, herded sheep, and foraged the abundant resources on the island’s beaches. Their descendants continued to thrive on the island for centuries, the population grew, and so did the need for land.

Nearly a thousand years into Cladh Hallan’s occupation, with living space on the small island becoming harder to find, people started building over the burial grounds of their ancestors. But the islanders were definitely aware of the graves beneath their feet—in fact, they went out of their way to acknowledge the people buried in them.

Residents of Cladh Hallan dug down into these older burials containing both cremated and unburnt remains, removing some parts and leaving the rest undisturbed. Then they assembled the exhumed remains into the requisite anatomical combinations that modern archaeologists have found centuries later. Once this was done, the composite bodies were once again laid to rest under the foundations of the roundhouses.

The two best-known adult skeletons from Cladh Hallan are the most unusual. Each was found under a roundhouse. One skeleton was identified as male, the other female, based on their pelvic bones. The bodies date to between 1300 and 1500 BCE…mostly.

The skeleton originally identified as a woman had a man’s skull where her own head used to be. Two of the teeth were missing from the skull, and those had been placed in each of the woman’s hands. By radiocarbon dating the bones, researchers found that the skull actually belonged to a man who had died at least a century earlier, perhaps a relative from an older generation.

The body originally identified as male was put together from three different people—all confirmed as male through DNA testing. The burial was made up of a man’s torso, a second man’s skull, and a third man’s jawbone. Each of these men had died at different times over the span of about 150 years. Again, ancestry and family identity seem to play a big part in these re-burials.

It isn’t terribly uncommon for cultures to include their deceased loved ones in celebratory, ritual, or even daily activities. Every few years, families in the Toraja region of Sulawesi, Indonesia exhume the remains of loved ones, clean them, dress them in fresh garments, and honor them with festivals.

South Korean “death beads” are made from the cremated remains of family members and displayed in the home.

Pre-colonial Inca religious tradition included regular ceremonial processions featuring the remains of people “buried” in natural alcoves in the Andes mountains and mummified by the dry, cold mountain air. The Cladh Hallan skeletons had once been mummified as well, though this was not mummification as it was practiced in ancient Egypt, nor like the mountainside Inca tradition.

First, the bodies were tightly wrapped in fabric, which kept the bones and soft tissue flexed into the fetal position. This is why the limbs remained so tightly curled towards the torso, and why the bodies were so well-preserved. Archaeologists were able to identify this type of preservation based on microscopic changes to the surfaces of the bones from the acidic environment of the peat bogs in which the bodies were submerged after wrapping.

Peat is a dense, wet accumulation of centuries of decaying plant matter, and is a feature of lots of landscapes, particularly in the northern latitudes. People have used dried peat as fuel for thousands of years, since it burns well and slowly, and is plentiful in areas where there aren’t a lot of trees for firewood. Peat from the Scottish isles, notably, is responsible for the smoky, earthy taste characteristic of Scotch whiskey.

Peat bogs are so packed with plant material that there is almost no oxygen present, which is great for preservation, as is the typically cool temperature beneath the peat’s surface. The lack of oxygen keeps microbes and other critters from consuming the body’s soft tissues, and the tannic acid from all the decaying plants in the bog turns skin dark and leathery.

By first burying the bodies of family members under layers of peat, the people at Cladh Hallan ensured that the remains would be as intact as possible when later generations exhumed them. This not only means that the Bronze Age inhabitants of the island were well aware of peat’s preservative properties, but that the entire process was a deliberate sequence of events.

Ancient Romans thought drinking gladiator blood could cure epilepsy

For ancient Romans, pooping was a group activity

Ancient Greece’s most famous oracle was just high on gas fumes

In medieval France, murderous pigs faced trial and execution

The radioactive ‘miracle water’ that killed its believers

Archaeologists interpret the pieced-together reburials as a part of a social or religious tradition that emphasized family lines and reverence for ancestors. Stones clearly marked the graves under the roundhouses, so the homes’ placement was definitely on purpose.

The initial bog burials were deliberately used as a source of family remains, and the parts that composed the buried skeletons seem to have been deliberately chosen, though we may never know why or how. It seems as though ancestor-recycling may have been a regular process at Cladh Hallan in the later part of the Bronze Age, as older generations gave way to new.

Archaeologists also surmise that the exhumed mummified remains were initially kept above ground, maybe in a special place in the house, and included as a part of daily life. These multi-ancestor figures might have been consulted when important decisions were made, or they may have been seen as household protectors.

Archaeology can never really uncover the full story of what people thought and believed thousands of years ago. Thoughts do not fossilize, and decay and weathering affect even the best-preserved archaeological sites.

We will probably never know exactly what it was like to live at Cladh Hallan when it was a busy, thriving settlement. We may never understand how the people that lived there conceived of their world, or how they thought about spirituality. Thanks to the burials at Cladh Hallan, though, we have a tantalizing glimpse of how people may have seen death in their universe as a continuous, pieced-together chain of lives lived over generations.

#4731. Here’s How General Motors Plans to Speed Up Vehicle Development Cycles Using AI

Artificial intelligence is being spread all across GM’s production processes.

Producing an all-new vehicle has, historically, been something of a five-year-long relay race, at least for the world’s major automakers. General Motors is hoping to change that, however. The Detroit-based automaker aims to cut vehicle development timelines to two years—and it sees artificial intelligence as a key player in that mission.

At the GM Forward event in New York City on Wednesday, executives emphasized the importance of a concurrent engineering model that allows designers, engineers, and manufacturers to work simultaneously. Traditionally, the development process was a series of handoffs, like an informational baton passing; now, an internal AI system will underpin this while drawing from new and old development data at GM. Instead of a step-by-step process, the addition of machine learning, virtualization, and agentic engineering will not only improve communication but also speed up mechanical development, GM chief product officer Sterling Anderson explained.

“Historically, designers and engineers have used wind tunnels and what's called computational fluid dynamics models, which is just a virtualized way of assessing how the air effectively flows in and around the vehicle. Wind tunnels take weeks to schedule. CFD models can take upwards of sixty hours to run. You get effectively one iteration per day,” Anderson said. “ To change that, we developed a proprietary tool we call ‘themed aero,’ which—instead of using these numerical techniques that are highly computationally intensive and temporally exhaustive—uses [machine learning] models trained again on GM's data set to get results that are remarkably similar to CFD, in about 60 seconds. You can imagine what that does for the iteration speed. You can imagine what that does for design optimality.”

Machine learning models are helping GM speed up in other arenas, too. Anderson explained that traditional structural analysis processes take four months to complete; however, the new AI process only takes a few hours to dial in front-end stiffness, dynamic standards, and durability requirements. Similarly, developing GM’s forthcoming lithium manganese-rich batteries would have typically required over 60,000 lab hours. Thanks to molecular-level modeling and machine learning processes, GM’s battery engineers only needed 256 such lab hours, and a month from first concept to formal program approval.

These learnings aren’t exclusive to EV development. The new 6.6-liter V-8 in the 2027 Chevrolet Silverado was reportedly developed in a third of the time required for a typical engine at 80 percent of the typical prototyping cost. It can have an immediate impact on real-world performance, too. “We tested [the Corvette ZR1] to 231 miles per hour top speed. Our virtual engineering said we ought to be closer to 233,” Jason Fischer, executive director of virtual integration at GM, said. “That very same day that we had a test, we wrote back to our virtual engineering team and our powertrain team, and we respun our calibration. And we sent it to the test team the next day, and we got to 233 miles an hour.”

Developing a vehicle more quickly is only one part of the production process. Efficient production lines are also essential to GM’s plan, and Anderson said bringing a software-first mindset to the factory floor is the first step toward success. GM promised improved robotic functions for stamping, casting, welding, painting, and final assembly processes, as well as 30 percent faster battery assembly.

But with all this speed, will quality be compromised? Anderson said that faster processes will only lead to higher-quality cars.“The second generation of anything is better than the first. The faster we can iterate, the better your car will get,” Anderson said. “Speed is inherently virtuous. It's inherently good. I think, particularly in the context of a product and product development, the faster you go, the better your product gets. Period.”

When, precisely, this two-year development process will kick in has yet to be announced. GM didn’t explicitly link these updated processes to any specific upcoming models, and it didn’t provide a specific year when these production processes would be fully fleshed out. Whenever it happens, though, the desired impacts are clear: reduced hardware costs and decreased complexity.

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.

#4727. A 750-Square-Foot Home Gets a Fresh and Fun Look, Thanks to Colorful Floors and a Pink Bathroom

The floor of an apartment is sometimes overlooked. It’s often simply a spot to lay out rugs and place furniture. In this 750-square-foot apartment in Monza, Italy, however, it’s a key element of the design by Studio Latino, the architecture and design firm founded by Irene Nocerino and Matteo Lattuada. The team chose a bold, fresh, and slightly irreverent sky blue color for the floor; it runs throughout the home and sets the tone for each space.

That choice transforms what is usually a neutral element into the starting point for an interior that isn’t afraid to play with color, even within the confines of a 750-square-foot space. A pink bathroom, playful furnishings, and colorful accents take center stage without needing to be perfectly coordinated: They meet, contrast, and interact, creating a cheerful, personal, and surprising atmosphere. This floor isn’t just a backdrop in this home. It sets its rhythm.

The famous Lampe de Marseille, designed by Le Corbusier for Nemo Lighting, hangs above the dining table.

The blue chosen for the floors is more than an interesting aesthetic element—it was also the starting point for the entire design of the project. Colored resin covers most of the existing floor, transforming a blank surface into the signature element of the home. The color flows seamlessly through the hallway and living area, guiding the eye and creating a visual continuity that makes the different rooms feel like part of a single, unified space.

In the living room, a Teddy sofa by OMHU sits above a Super Rock rug featuring a graphic and textural design inspired by the shapes of rocks, by Bethan Laura Wood for CC-Tapis.

The blue floor takes center stage, with the rest of the house revolving around it without ever matching it precisely. White, wood tones, red, and pink alternate and play off one another. Red tiles brighten up the kitchen, while the pink ones in the bathroom introduce a softer, unexpected touch.

The result is a deliberately imperfect balance, made up of contrasts and playful combinations, in which that light-blue floor ends up doing much more than simply covering a surface: It brings the sky into the home.

The custom kitchen, constructed by local artisans, features a minimalist design and red tiles that echo the home’s color palette.

The hallway leads to the bedroom, which includes a famous LC4 chaise longue by Le Corbusier, Pierre Jeanneret, and Charlotte Perriand, shown here in a vintage version by Cassina.

In the bedroom, the Swirl rug by Univers Uchronia for CC-Tapis makes a bold color statement. The nightstand and lamp are from Zara Home.

The character of this home reflects its residents: a young couple passionate about photography, art, and technology, who have chosen to surround themselves with objects that tell a story, without ever taking themselves too seriously. The space features iconic pieces and whimsical touches, from a Super Rock rug by Bethan Laura Wood to a Swirl rug by Univers Uchronia (both for CC-Tapis) to the famous Lampe de Marseille by Le Corbusier for Nemo Lighting.

There are also Plia chairs by Giancarlo Piretti for Anonima Castelli, a plush Teddy sofa by OMHU, and a vintage LC4 chaise longue by Cassina, along with custom pieces crafted by local artisans. The result is a blend of design, vintage items, and artisanal creations that follows no specific rule except one—everything must have personality.

A pair of Plia folding chairs by Giancarlo Piretti for Anonima Castelli.

It’s a home that embraces color, combines different shapes, and leaves room for a touch of whimsy. Because even high design can sometimes simply have some fun.

The shower enclosure was custom-made by local artisans.

This article first appeared in AD Italia.

#4726. The creator of last year's cult hit RPG The Necromancer's Tale is back with The Lich Wars, a storybook RPG that follows 30 years of undead botheration

Psychic Software have announced The Lich Wars, a standalone prequel for their widely acclaimed RPG The Necromancer's Tale. Widely acclaimed by me, anyway. I'm pretty wide, these days – the local Morrisons keeps doing discounts on 80-biccy packets of bourbon creams.

I liked The Necromancer's Tale specifically for how it wove a whole RPG around the narrative dynamics of a single RPG class, with players performing rituals in each chapter to develop their morbid spellcraft, while managing the suspicions of nearby townsfolk. I also really loved its choose-your-own-adventure prologue. The Lich Wars sort of spins the prologue out into a full game.

It's a partially voiced, branching storybook experience with skills and stats, evolving relationships and the possibility of character death (and presumably, undeath, if you end up dabbling in tomb wizardry). It casts you as a musketeer in the alt-1700s, battling through two of the titular lich wars in the course of a 30 year career with three main paths that also features plenty of regular, non-lich wars.

Here's how the devs characterise the plot: "The Lich Wars explores the early romantic & gothic themes of technological anxiety, unfettered progress, the ethics of war, and the destructive mental infection brought on by dark magic. This is a time at the cusp of rational thought and superstition; science and magic. The world is dark and dangerous. Courtly intrigue collides with black magic even as chemistry emerges from alchemy."

As regards the presentation, there are still elements of the old 3D world in the shape of maps and tabletop, but the bulk of it appears to consist of hand-drawn illustrations and unfurling paragraphs. "You will visit much of the alt-history kingdom of Rulsthen, near Venice," the devs promise. "You may also travel across Western Europe as you march to the Great Northern War and the War of Spanish Succession." Gosh, whole lotta wars. Can we not just have one big war - a "World War", if you will?

This feels like a more economical production than The Necromancer's Tale, but I think it's change for the better: toing and froing about the 3D town was my least favourite aspect of the older game, much as I enjoyed the writing of the setting. Lift the coffin lid of the Steam page for more. Release date there is currently none.

#4725. Robinhood taps Ripple’s $2.5 billion RLUSD stablecoin despite backing rival USDG

Ripple’s RLUSD stablecoin is set to reach Robinhood’s retail customers later this year, giving the dollar-backed token access to a brokerage platform with roughly 28.6 million funded accounts.

“From bringing RLUSD to retail and institutional markets to helping Robinhood manage its own treasury operations, this partnership reflects growing institutional demand for integrated solutions that support customer-facing offerings and core financial operations,” said Monica Long, president of Ripple.

The companies announced the partnership on Tuesday. RLUSD is already available on Robinhood Chain and Bitstamp, the crypto exchange Robinhood acquired for $200 million in June. Now, RLUSD is also expected to become available through the Robinhood app before the end of 2026.

That decision puts Robinhood in an unusual position, as it helped establish the Global Dollar Network in 2024 to promote USDG, a competing stablecoin issued by Paxos. Now, Robinhood is announcing its plans to offer Ripple’s rival token to its customers.

The Global Dollar Network, founded in 2024, brings together companies that help distribute USDG and share in the revenue it generates. The arrangement gives members a financial incentive to promote USDG, but Robinhood’s partnership with Ripple shows that those incentives do not preclude members from offering competing stablecoins. Other founding members included Kraken, Galaxy Digital, Anchorage Digital and CoinDesk's parent company, Bullish. Arbitrum joined earlier this month.

Robinhood has already given USDG a role in its products. In July, the consortium announced that USDG would be the default lending asset for Robinhood Earn, its onchain lending product.

Robinhood did not announce any changes to its USDG agreement, and it remains unclear whether RLUSD will be offered alongside USDG in the same products. CoinDesk contacted Robinhood regarding this matter, but the fintech did not respond immediately.

Robinhood reported 28.6 million funded customers at the end of August. The companies have not said how many will be eligible to use RLUSD when it becomes available in the app.

The partnership also covers Robinhood’s institutional business. Bitstamp will use RLUSD on the XRP Ledger for settlement, while Robinhood plans to adopt Ripple’s treasury software for cash management, payments and forecasting.

Ripple Prime already provides Bitstamp with lending and margin financing across roughly 20 digital assets. The companies plan to add perpetual futures trading later this year.

RLUSD, launched in December 2024, has grown to a market capitalization of more than $2.5 billion, according to the press release.

Robinhood has not said whether RLUSD will receive the same role as USDG in its lending products, or how the two stablecoins will be promoted to customers.

Also on Tuesday, Ripple, which got final Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) approval to go ahead with the Ripple National Trust Bank (RNTB) yesterday, announced the expansion of its partnership with BNY (BNY) to broaden institutional digital asset custody infrastructure and explore new institutional use cases for blockchain, including tokenized trade. Both firms first partnered in July 2025.

Diversified RWA stablecoins sustain 5-7% yield from real credit as crypto funding compresses to ~4%. GENIUS pushes yield off-chain; TAM grows to $4B in 3 years.

#4724. Wellness Apps, Medically Tailored Meals Help Slow Weight Gain After GLP-1s

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Weight regain is common after stopping GLP-1 medications such as semaglutide (Wegovy, Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro, Zepbound).

In fact, previous research has shown that most people regain at least half of their lost weight within 1 year of stopping treatment, with weight regain often accelerating during the first 3–4 months.

However, a new study suggests that structured support, including a weight management app or medically tailored meals, may help slow weight regain after discontinuing GLP-1 medications.

The small study, published October 7 in Obesity Science & Practice, found that participants who received either intervention regained significantly less weight over four months than those receiving usual care.

The study included 39 adults with an average age of 50, an average weight of 189 pounds, and an average body mass index (BMI) of 31.4.

All participants had lost more than 10% of their body weight while taking GLP-1 medications and had stopped treatment within the previous 30 days.

All participants also completed questionnaires at the beginning and end of the study and used Bluetooth scales to track their weight at home throughout the four-month period.

Researchers randomly assigned participants to one of the following three groups:

Participants continued to receive care from their healthcare providers, which may include nutrition, exercise, and lifestyle counseling from a clinician or registered dietitian. The type and amount of support varied depending on the provider’s recommendations and available resources. The researchers did not provide any additional intervention.

Participants received a subscription to Noom, an app that uses behavioral strategies to support weight loss and maintenance. These include tracking food intake, exercise, and weight, as well as educational lessons on the psychological, physiological, social, and environmental factors that influence eating behaviors.

The app also provides tools to help users recognize and manage triggers that may lead to overeating. Participants were encouraged to use the app for at least 15 minutes daily, including completing lessons, logging food, and tracking their weight.

These are typically nutrient-dense meals developed by registered dietitians to meet the nutritional needs of people with specific health conditions, such as chronic kidney disease or uncontrolled type 2 diabetes.

For this study, participants in this group selected 10 portion-controlled, “weight wellness” meals each week from more than 70 options, which were delivered to their homes.

After four months, participants who received either medically tailored meals or access to the digital weight management app experienced significantly less weight regain than those receiving usual care.

The estimated weight regain was 10.75% of body weight in the usual-care group, compared with 3.6% in the Noom group and 3.58% in the medically tailored meals group.

Although participants in all three groups regained weight, the findings suggest that structured nutritional or behavioral support may help limit weight regain after stopping GLP-1 medications.

“These findings highlight the benefits of ongoing support for patients on a weight loss journey,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA. Ali wasn’t involved in the study.

Denise Pate, MD, a board certified internist with Medical Offices of Manhattan who was also not involved in the study, told Healthline that the findings are significant “because they show that obesity management is multifactorial, not only medication, but the combination of types of meals and behavioral change all play a role.”

However, because this was a small, four-month study, larger, longer-term trials are needed to determine whether these approaches can help people maintain weight loss over time after stopping GLP-1 medications.

Ali noted that research suggests GLP-1 medications are most effective for weight management when taken long-term.

“Obesity needs to be recognized and treated as a chronic condition, similar to diabetes or hypertension,” he said.

Pate agreed, emphasizing that obesity is not always recognized as a chronic condition that may require ongoing treatment.

“I feel that the medical community, insurance, and the population as a whole have yet to fully accept obesity as a chronic medical condition, which it most definitely is for a large part of patients. For this reason, I think patients should stay on these medications long term if needed,” she said.

“GLP-1 medications are highly effective tools for transitioning to a healthier diet and lifestyle, which for most people may require long-term use,” Ali added.

Pate explained that one of the goals of GLP-1 medications is to help people eat smaller portions by recognizing when they feel full and stopping when they do.

“I tell my patients from the start, this is the goal,” she said, adding that she hopes patients will retain those habits after stopping treatment.

Ali recommended maintaining a balanced diet rich in protein and vegetables while limiting added sugars and refined carbohydrates.

He added that tracking apps and regular follow-up appointments with a healthcare professional or registered dietitian may also help people maintain their progress.

“Patients need to understand that the GLP medication is not a miracle drug, although with the dramatic weight loss, it can be perceived as such. It is only a tool to be used in conjunction with an exercise routine as well as a healthy diet,” Pate said.

#4723. World’s largest wildlife crossing nears completion in California

Published Oct 23, 2026 11:03 AM EDT

California is known for its giant, multi-laned highways bustling under bright blue skies. And while this infrastructure is useful to humans, the Golden State’s many highways represent an almost impenetrable barrier for wildlife.

Soon, habitats on either side of the 10-laned 101 Freeway in Agoura Hills, California will be reconnected thanks to the world’s largest wildlife crossing, according to the Los Angeles Times. The Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing is scheduled for completion on December 2.

Wildlife crossings are structures that help animals safely cross roads. In the United States, collisions between motorists and large animals take place more than a million times a year. Experts estimate that this results in 400 human deaths every year. But the problem isn’t just road accidents. Habitat fragmentation is also a concern, especially for species that require a large range. Inbreeding can occur if a small enough group of animals becomes isolated.

In the Los Angeles area, mountain lions (Puma concolor), also known as pumas or cougars, are currently the species most vulnerable to genetic isolation caused by roads and development, according to the project’s website. And the 101 Freeway appears to be the most important obstacle, out of all the roads in the area, to the region’s ecological health.

As such, the Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing aims to reconnect local plants and animals in the ecosystem of the Santa Monica Mountains.  It’s about 210 feet long and about 170 to 174 feet wide. A thick, sturdy-looking vegetation-covered bridge already straddles the busy freeway—but the weather might throw a wrench in the project’s schedule.

“We’re just hoping El Niño‘s on our side,” Beth Pratt, president of the Wildlife Crossing Fund, tells the Los Angeles Times, in reference to the climate pattern that typically brings wetter winter weather along the United States’ southern border. Flooding would put work on pause, according to the outlet. “I keep saying, ‘Mother Nature, we’re trying to help you. Let’s not throw any more complications our way.’”
